\begin{exercise}
You study with someone who says, 
``Yes, obviously there are different sizes of infinity.
The plane $\R^2$ obviously has infinitely many more points then the 
line~$\R$, so it is a larger infinity.''
Convince them that
their argument is wrong because the cardinality of the plane is the same
as the cardinality of the line.
\hint~consider the function $\map{f}{\R^2}{\R}$ such that $f(x,y)$ interleaves 
the digits of the two input numbers.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In mathematics classes we mostly work with rational numbers, perhaps 
leaving the impression that irrational numbers are rare.
Actually, there are more irrational numbers than rationals.
Prove that while the set of rational numbers is countable,
the set of irrational numbers is uncountable.
\begin{answer}\verified
As stated in the question, the set of rational numbers is countable.
Suppose that the irrational numbers were also countable.
Then, because the union of two countable sets is countable, 
the real numbers would be countable.
But the reals are not countable 
and so the irrationals are not countable either. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\Cref{ex:RationalsAreCountable} shows that the rational numbers are countable.
What happens when we apply diagonal argument given in 
\cref{th:RealsNotCountable} is to an enumeration of the rationals?
Consider a sequence $q_0, q_1,\ldots$ that contains all of the rationals.
For each of those numbers use a decimal 
expansion $q_i=d_i.d_{i,0}d_{i,1}d_{i,2}\ldots$ 
(with $d_i\in\Z$ and $d_{i,j}\in\set{0,\ldots 9}$) that does not end
in all~$9$'s, 
so that the decimal expansion is unique.
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  Let $g$ be the map on the decimal digits $0,1,\ldots{} 9$ given by
  $g(1)=2$, and $g(0)=g(2)=g(3)=\cdots=1$.
  Define $z=\sum_{n\in\N}g(d_{n,n})\cdot 10^{-(n+1)}$.
  Show that $z$ is irrational.
\item Use the prior item to conclude that
  the diagonal number $d=\sum_{n\in\N}d_{n,n}\cdot 10^{-(n+1)}$
  is irrational.
  \hint~show that, unlike a rational number, 
  it has no repeating pattern in its decimal expansion.
\item
  Why is the fact that the diagonal is not rational not a contradiction to 
  the fact that we can enumerate all of the rationals?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
  Write the decimal expansion of~$z$ as~$z=0.z_0z_1z_2\ldots$\sentencespace
  It does not end in $9$'s as the output of $g$ is never a~$9$.
  So $z$ has a unique decimal expansion.
  For each~$q_i$ the decimal expansion of $z$ differs from $q_i$'s in that 
  $z_{i}\cdot 10^{-(i+1)}\neq q_{i,i}\cdot 10^{-(i+1)}$,
  by the definition of~$g$.
  Because their decimal expansions differ, and because neither ends
  in $9$'s, the two are different numbers.

  Since $z$ does not equal any rational number, it is irrational.
\item 
  If $d=\sum_{n\in\N}d_{n,n}\cdot 10^{-(n+1)}$ is rational then its
  decimal expansion repeats.  
  That is, there is a decimal place such that past
  that place the expansion consists of a sequence of digits
  $d_{j}d_{j+1}\ldots d_{j+k}$ repeated again and again.
  But then the prior item's~$z$ would also repeat, in that 
  past that same decimal
  place its expansion would consist of a sequence of digits
  $g(d_{j})g(d_{j+1})\ldots g(d_{j+k})$ repeated again and again.
  That would make~$z$ rational, which it isn't.
\item
  Why should it be a contradiction?
  In \cref{th:RealsNotCountable} we start with what 
  purports to be a list of all reals and then produce a real that is 
  not on that list.
  Here we start with what is a list of all rationals and produce something
  that is not a rational.
  Entirely different.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Verify Cantor's Theorem in the finite case by showing that
if $S$ is finite then the cardinality of its power set is 
$\sizeof{\powerset(S)}=2^{\sizeof{S}}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Before we do the proof, consider $S=\set{0,1,2,3}$.
These are the elements of $\powerset(S)$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{l}
  \set{}, \set{0}, \set{1}, \set{2}, \set{0,1}. \set{0,2}, \set{1,2}, \set{0,1,2} \\ 
  \quad \set{3}, \set{0,3}, \set{1,3}, \set{2,3}, \set{0,1,3}. \set{0,2,3}, \set{1,2,3}, \set{0,1,2,3} \\ 
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
So, fixing $\hat{s}=3\in S$ gives two classes of
subsets, those sets containing~$\hat{s}$ and those not containing~$\hat{s}$,
and the two have the same number of members. 

Our proof uses induction on $\sizeof{S}$.
The base case is that $\sizeof{S}=0$, so $S$ is empty.
Then $\powerset(S)=\set{\emptyset}$ and $\sizeof{\powerset(S)}=1$,
as required.

For the inductive step fix $k\in\N^+$, assume that the statement is 
true for $n=0$, $n=1$, \ldots $n=k$, and consider a set $S$ where 
$\sizeof{S}=k+1$.
Because $\sizeof{S}=k+1$ the set has an element, $\hat{s}\in S$.
The inductive hypothesis applies to $S-\set{\hat{s}}$, so 
$\powerset(S-\set{\hat{s}})$ has $2^k$-many elements.
As illustrated in the example above, 
the elements of $\powerset(S-\set{\hat{s}})$, which do not contain $\hat{s}$, 
are in correspondence with the elements of $\powerset(S)$ that do 
contain~$\hat{s}$.
Thus the total number of elements of~$\powerset(S)$
is $2\cdot 2^k$, which equals $2^{k+1}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://viterbi-web.usc.edu/~mjneely/docs/infinity.pdf}{Michael J~Neely }
\end{credit}
The definition $R=\set{s\suchthat s\notin f(s)}$ is the key to the proof of 
Cantor's Theorem, \cref{th:RealsNotCountable}.
This story illustrates the idea:~a high school yearbook asks 
each graduating student~$s_i$ make a list $f(s_i)$ of class
members that they predict will someday be famous. 
Define the set of humble students~$H$ to be those who are not on their
own list.
Show that no student's list equals~$H$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Suppose that $f(s_i)=H$.
There are two possibilities,~either $s_i\in H$ or else $s_i\notin H$.
If $s_i\in H$ then student~$s_i$ is a member of their own list, so 
$s_i$ is not humble, so this violates its case assumption.
On the other hand, if $s_i\notin H$ then $s_i$ is not a member of their own
list, so $s_i$ is humble, which also violates this case assumption.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The proof of \cref{th:RealsNotCountable} must work around the fact that
some numbers have more than one base ten representation.
Base two also has the property that 
some numbers have more than one representation; 
an example is $0.01000\ldots$ and $0.00111\ldots$.
% But in a base~two argument, when building~$z$ there is no way to avoid
% the digits~$0$ and~$1$.
How could you make the argument work in base~two?
\begin{answer}\verified
One way is to take the diagonal bits two at a time; 
for instance changing $01$ to $10$, and changing any other two-bit
sequence to $01$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Answer from Stack Exchange member
  \href{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/271126}{Alex Becker}.
\end{credit}
The discussion after the statement of \cref{th:RealsNotCountable}
includes that the real number~$1$ has two different
decimal representations, $1.000\ldots=0.999\ldots$\sentencespace
\begin{exparts}
\item
Verify this equality using the formula for an infinite geometric series, 
$a+ar+ar^2+ar^3+\cdots =a\cdot 1/(1-r)$.
\item 
Show that if a number has two different decimal representations then
in the leftmost decimal place where they differ, they differ by~$1$.
\hint~that is the biggest difference that the remaining decimal places
can make up. 
\item In addition show that, for the one with the larger digit in
that first differing place, all of the digits to its right are~$0$,
while the other representation has that all of the remaining digits are $9$'s. 
\hint~this is similar to the prior item.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
In $a+ar+ar^2+ar^3+\cdots =a\cdot 1/(1-r)$,
take $a=9$ and $r=0.1$.
The left side is $9+0.9+0.09+0.009+\cdots=9.999\,99\ldots$\sentencespace
The right side is $9\cdot 1/(1-(0.1))=9/0.9=1/0.1=1/(1/10)=10$.
Subtract~$9$ from both sides to get $0.999\,99\ldots=1$.
\item
Consider a real number with two representations.
Write the representations
as $\vec{x}=\sequence{x_N,x_{N-1},\ldots}$ and 
$\vec{y}=\sequence{y_N,y_{N-1},\ldots}$,
subject to $x_i,y_i\in\set{0,1,\ldots 9}$ and to this condition.
\begin{equation*} 
  \sum_{N\geq i}x_i\cdot 10^i=\sum_{N\geq i}y_i\cdot 10^i
  \tag{*}
\end{equation*} 
(The number $N\in\Z$ is the furthest left nonzero decimal place among 
the two representions.)  
For instance, for $1.000\ldots=0.999\ldots$ we have
$x_0=1$, $x_{-1}=0$, $x_{-2}=0$, \ldots, 
giving $1\cdot 10^{0}+0\cdot 10^{-1}+0\cdot 10^{-2}+\cdots$,
and
$y_0=0$, $y_{-1}=9$, $y_{-2}=9$, \ldots,
giving $0\cdot 10^{0}+9\cdot 10^{-1}+9\cdot 10^{-2}+\cdots$\,.

Let the leftmost decimal place where the two differ be~$M$,
so that $x_M\neq y_M$ and $x_i=y_i$ for $i\in\set{N,N-1,\ldots M}$.
One of $x_M$ or~$y_M$ is larger; without loss of generality
suppose that $x_M>y_M$.
We must show that $x_M=y_M+1$ 
(as it does for the example $\sequence{1,0,0\ldots}$ and
$\sequence{0,9,9\ldots}$).

The reason is that the remaining digits can add to a number no larger than
$\sum_{M>i}9\cdot 10^{i}$.
This adds to $10^{M}$ by the geometric series formula.
If $x_M-y_M>1$ then
$(\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}x_i10^i)-(\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}y_i10^i)=(x_M-y_M)\cdot 10^M$
is at least $2\cdot 10^M$, and the remaining digits cannot make up the 
difference to satisfy condition~($*$). 
\item
Continuing with the notation from the prior item, 
we want to show that $x_{j}=0$ and $y_{j}=9$ for 
$j\in\set{M-1,M-2,\ldots}$\sentencespace
The argument is similar to the prior one and we will do only $j=M-1$.
We have that the representations differ by one in decimal place~$M$.
\begin{equation*}
\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}x_i10^i\big)
  -\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}y_i10^i\big)
  = 10^{M}
\end{equation*}
Suppose that $y_{M-1}-x_{M-1}\leq 8$.
Then
\begin{equation*}
\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M-1}x_i10^i\big)
  -\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M-1}y_i10^i\big)
  \geq 10^{M}-8\cdot 10^{M-1}
\end{equation*}
and the remaining digits can add to a number no larger than
$\sum_{M-1>i}9\cdot 10^{i}=1\cdot 10^{M-1}$,
again by the geometric series formula.
Adding that into the prior equation is not enough to satisfy~($*$).
(The cases of $j=M-2$, $j=M-3$, etc.\@ are similar.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that there is no set of all sets.
\hint~use \cref{th:CantorsThm}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Suppose otherwise, that $\mathcal{C}$ is the set of all sets.
  Then $\powerset(\mathcal{C})$ would be a collection of sets so we would have
  $\powerset(\mathcal{C})\subseteq \mathcal{C}$.
  That would give $\sizeof{\powerset(\mathcal{C})}\leq\sizeof{\mathcal{C}}$,
  which would contradict Cantor's Theorem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:SchroderBernstein}
\Cref{def:CardLessThanOrEqual} extends the definition of equal cardinality 
to say that $\sizeof{A}\leq \sizeof{B}$ if there is a one-to-one 
function from $A$ to~$B$.
The \definend{Schröder–Bernstein theorem} is that if both 
$\sizeof{S}\leq \sizeof{T}$ and $\sizeof{T}\leq \sizeof{S}$ 
then $\sizeof{S}=\sizeof{T}$.
We will walk through the proof.
It depends on finding chains of images:~for 
any $s\in S$ we form the associated chain by iterating application of the
two functions, both to the right 
and the left, as here. 
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),g^{-1}(s),s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s)))\, \ldots{}
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
(Starting with $s$ the chain to the right is 
$s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s))), \ldots$ while the chain to the left 
is $\ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),g^{-1}(s),s$.)
For any $t\in T$ define the associated chain similarly. 

An example is to take a set of integers $S=\set{0,1,2}$ and 
a set of characters $T=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$,
and consider the two 
one-to-one functions $\map{f}{S}{T}$ and $\map{g}{T}{S}$
shown here.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{c|c}
    $s$    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$f(s)$}  \\ \hline
    $0$    &$\str{b}$  \\
    $1$    &$\str{c}$  \\
    $2$    &$\str{a}$  
  \end{tabular}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/maps/maps03.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{c|c}
    $t$       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$g(t)$}  \\ \hline
    $\str{a}$ &$0$   \\
    $\str{b}$ &$1$    \\
    $\str{c}$ &$2$     
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
Starting at $0\in S$ gives 
a single chain that is cyclic,
$\ldots{}\,0,\str{b},1,\str{c},2,\str{a},0\,\ldots$\sentencespace
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  Consider $S=\set{0,1,2,3}$ and $T=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$.
  Let $f$ associate $0\mapsto\str{a}$, $1\mapsto\str{b}$, $2\mapsto\str{d}$
  and $3\mapsto\str{c}$.
  Let $g$ associate $\str{a}\mapsto 0$, $\str{b}\mapsto 1$, $\str{c}\mapsto 2$
  and $\str{d}\mapsto 3$.
  Check that these maps are one-to-one.
  List the chain associated with each element of $S$ and the 
  chain associated with each element of $T$.
\item For infinite sets a chain can have a first element, an 
   element without any preimage.  
   Let $S$ be the even numbers and let $T$ be the odds.
   Let $\map{f}{S}{T}$ be $f(x)=x+1$ and let $\map{g}{T}{S}$ be $g(x)=x+1$.
   Show each map is one-to-one.
   Show there is a single chain and that it has a first element.
\item Argue that we can assume without loss of generality that $S$ 
  and~$T$ are disjoint sets.
\item Assume that $S$ and $T$ are disjoint and that $\map{f}{S}{T}$
  and $\map{g}{T}{S}$ are one-to-one.
  Show that every element of either set is in a unique chain, 
  and that each chain is of one of four kinds: (i)~those that
  repeat after some number of terms
  (ii)~those that continue infinitely in both directions without repeating
  (iii)~those that continue infinitely to the right but stop on the left
  at some element of~$S$, 
  and (iv)~those that continue infinitely to the right but stop on the left
  at some element of~$T$.
\item Show that for any chain the function below 
  is a correspondence between the 
  chain's elements from~$S$ and its elements from~$T$.
  \begin{equation*}
    h(s)=
    \begin{cases}
      f(s) &\case{if $s$ is in a sequence of type~(i), (ii), or~(iii)}  \\
      g^{-1}(s) &\case{if $s$ is in a sequence of type~(iv)}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item These are the chains for~$S$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{r|c}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{s\in S}
  &\text{\makebox[2in][c]{\tabulartext{Chain}}}  \\ \hline
  0 &  \ldots\,0,\str{a},0,\str{a}\,\ldots   \\
  1 &  \ldots\,1,\str{b},1,\str{b}\,\ldots    \\
  2 &  \ldots\,2,\str{d},3,\str{c},2,\str{d}\,\ldots\\
  3 &  \text{--\, same as the chain for $2$\,--}
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
These are the chains for~$T$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{r|c}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{t\in T}
  &\text{\makebox[2in][c]{\tabulartext{Chain}}}  \\ \hline
  \str{a} &  \ldots\,\str{a},0,\str{a},0\,\ldots   \\
  \str{b} &  \ldots\,\str{b},1,\str{b},1\,\ldots    \\
  \str{c} &  \ldots\,\str{c},2,\str{d},3,\str{c},2\,\ldots\\
  \str{d} &  \text{--\, same as the chain for \str{c}\,--}
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
\item Let $S=\set{0,2,4,6,\ldots}$, let $T=\set{1,3,5,\ldots}$,
and take $\map{f}{S}{T}$ to be $f(s)=s+1$, and 
$\map{g}{T}{S}$ to be $g(t)=t+1$.
The function~$f$ is one-to-one because if $f(s)=f(\hat{s})$ then
$s+1=\hat{s}+1$, and so $s=\hat{s}$.
The function~$g$ is one-to-one in the same way.

The chain associated with~$0\in S$ is
$0,1,2,3\ldots$\sentencespace
It contains all the elements of both sets, and so the chain is unique.
It also has a first element, that is, there is no element of~$T$ 
that is~$g^{-1}(0)$.
\item If they are not already disjoint, if $S\cap T\neq\emptyset$,
then we can just color all the elements of~$S$ purple and all the elements 
of~$T$ gold and that will make them disjoint for sure.
More precisely, consider the set $\set{0}\times S$ of pairs
$\sequence{0,s}$ along with the set $\set{1}\times T$ of pairs
$\sequence{1,t}$.
These are disjoint sets because any two elements differ in the
first entry.
Obviously they correspond to the original sets $S$ and~$T$.
\item First we show that every element is in a unique chain.
Fix some $x\in S\cup T$.
Because we assume that the two sets are disjoint, the function operation that 
applies to $x$ is determined, meaning that if $x\in S$ then for the chain
we next consider $f(x)$ while if $x\in T$ then we are next doing~$g$.
With that, all the elements of the chain to the right of~$x$ are determined.
And, because the two functions are one-to-one, all of the
element to the left are likewise determined also.

As to the four possibilities, either a chain is finite, that is, it repeats, 
or it is infinite.
If it repeats then it is case~(i).
If it is infinite then it either does not have an initial element, and
so is case~(ii), or else it does.
If it does have an initial element then either that element is from 
the set~$S$, as in case~(iii), or else that element is from the
set~$T$, as in case~(iv).
\item  
We will consider the correspondence for each chain type below.
But first note that because each case is a restriction of a function, each is
well-defined.
That is, never does any of these maps associate two different outputs
with the same input.

A type~(i) chain
repeats after some number of elements 
$s_0,t_0,s_1,t_1,\ldots,s_{n-1},t_{n-1},s_n=s_0$
(because the sets are disjoint it cannot repeat after a single element, and
we can thus take the first element to be a member of~$S$).
% So this is the chain, with $s=s_0$.
% \begin{equation*}
%   s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s))),\ldots{} g(f(\,\cdots f(s)))=s
% \end{equation*}
The function~$f$ makes these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  s_0\mapsto t_0\quad s_1\mapsto t_1,\quad\ldots\quad s_{n-1}\mapsto t_{n-1}
\end{equation*}
This is clearly a correspondence among the elements of the chain.
It is manifestly onto the set 
$\set{t_0,t_1,\ldots t_{n-1}}$ and
it is one-to-one because the~$t_j$'s are 
distinct, or else the chain would be shorter.

For a type~(ii) chain 
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),\,g^{-1}(s),\,s,\,f(s),\,g(f(s)),\,f(g(f(s))),\, \ldots{}
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
the function~$f$ associates these.
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots\quad
  f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s))\mapsto g^{-1}(s)\quad
  s\mapsto f(s)\quad 
  g(f(s))\mapsto f(g(f(s))),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
This also is clearly a correspondence.

A type~(iii) chain 
\begin{equation*}
  s,\,f(s),\,g(f(s)),\,f(g(f(s))),\, \ldots{}
\end{equation*}
has these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  s\mapsto f(s)\quad 
  g(f(s))\mapsto f(g(f(s))),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
Again, clearly a correspondence.

The one that takes some thought is a chain of type~(iv).
\begin{equation*}
  t,\,g(t),\,f(g(t)),\,g(f(g(t)),\, \ldots{}
\end{equation*}
We want a correspondence function from $S$ to~$T$.
Consider these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  g(t)\mapsto t\quad 
  g(f(t))\mapsto f(t),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
Because the function~$g$ is one-to-one its inverse is defined,
over its range. 
So this association is a well-defined function.
Like the chain types above, this also is clearly a correspondence.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{cardinality|)}\index{infinity|)}

\index{diagonalization|)}












%================================================
\section{Universality}\index{universality|(}

We have seen a number of Turing machines, such as one whose
output is the successor of its input, 
one that adds two input numbers, and others.
These are single-purpose devices, where to get different input-output behavior
we need a new machine, that is, new hardware.
This was what we meant by
saying that a good first take on Turing machines is that they 
are more like a modern computer program than a modern computer\Dash
on a modern computer, to change behavior you don't change 
the hardware.

This picture shows
programmers of an early computer. 
They are changing its behavior by 
changing its circuits, using the patch cords.
% \begin{center}  \small
%   \includegraphics[width=.53\textwidth]{background/pix/rewiring.jpg} \\ 
%   \begin{credit}
%     \textit{ENIAC Programmers, 1946} \href{https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/eniac-programmers-united-states-army-united-states-army/4AFgcMwMqg1mRw}{U. S. Army Photo from Army Research Labs Technical Library}

%   \end{credit}
%   \captionof{figure}{\protect\citetext{ENIAC, reconfigure by rewiring.}{Jean Jennings (left), Marlyn Wescoff (center), and Ruth Lichterman program the ENIAC, circa 1946. U. S. Army Photo}}
% \end{center}
\begin{fig}[eniac]{\protect\citetext{ENIAC, reconfigure by rewiring.}{Jean Jennings (left), Marlyn Wescoff (center), and Ruth Lichterman program the ENIAC, circa 1946. US Army Photo.}}  \small
  \includegraphics[width=.53\textwidth]{background/pix/rewiring.jpg} \\

    \textit{ENIAC Programmers, 1946} \href{https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/eniac-programmers-united-states-army-united-states-army/4AFgcMwMqg1mRw}{U. S. Army Photo from Army Research Labs Technical Library}
    % (US government works not subject to copyright.)
  \end{credit}
\end{fig}
Imagine having a cellphone where  
to change from running a browser to taking a call you must 
pull one chip and replace it with another. 
The picture's patch cords are an improvement over a soldering iron, but are 
not a final answer.



\subsection{Universal Turing machine}\index{Universal Turing machine|(}\index{Turing machine!universal|(}
\citetext{A pattern in technology is for jobs done in hardware to migrate to software}{%
  One story that illustrates the naturalness of this involves
  the English mathematician C~Babbage, and his protogee A~Lovelace.
  In 1812 Babbage was developing tables of logarithms.
  These were calculated by computers\Dash the word then current
  for the people who computed them by hand.
  To check the accuracy he had two people do the same table and compared.
  He was annoyed at the number of discrepencies and 
  had the idea to build a machine to do the computing.
  He got a government grant to design and construct a
  machine called the difference engine, 
  which he started in 1822. 
  This was a single-purpose device, what we today would call a calculator.
  One person who became interested in the computations was an
  acquaintance of his, Lovelace (who at the time was named Byron, as
  she was the daughter of the poet Lord Byron).  
  \protect\begin{center}
  \protect\small
  \protect\begin{tabular}[t]{c}
  \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{background/pix/babbage.jpg} \\
  \protect\label{fig:babbage}\protect\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Babbage}{Charles Babbage, 1791--1871} % wikipedia
  \protect\end{tabular} 
  \protect\hspace{3em}
  \protect\begin{tabular}[t]{c}
  \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{background/pix/adabyron.jpg} \\
  \protect\label{fig:adabyron}\protect\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada_Lovelace}{Ada Lovelace (nee Byron), 1815--1852} % 
  \protect\end{tabular}
  \protect\end{center}
  However, this machine was never finished because
  Babbage had the thought to make a device that would be programmable,
  and that was too much of a temptation.
  Lovelace contributed an extensive set of notes on a proposed
  new machine, the analytical engine, and has become known as the
  first programmer.}.
The classic example is weaving.
\begin{center}
  % http://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Loomwork.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=.33\textwidth]{background/pix/loom.jpg}
  \hspace*{2em}
  % http://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Deutsches_Technikmuseum_Berlin_February_2008_0013.JPG 
  \includegraphics[width=.33\textwidth]{background/pix/jaquardpunchedcards.jpg}
\end{center}
Weaving by hand, as the loom operator on the left is doing,
is intricate and slow.
We can make a machine to reproduce her pattern.
But what if we want a different pattern; do we need another machine? 
In 1801 
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard_weaving}{J~Jacquard} 
built a loom like the one on the right, 
\citetext{controlled by cards}{%
  It weaves with hooks whose positions, raised or lowered, are determined by 
  holes punched in the cards}.
Getting a different pattern does not require a new loom, it only requires 
swapping cards.

%<*discussion:UniversalTM> 
Turing introduced the analog to this for computers.
He produced a Turing machine~$\UTM$ that
can be fed a tape containing a description
of a Turing machine~$\mathcal{M}$, along with input for that machine. 
Then $\UTM$ will have the same input-output behavior
as would $\mathcal{M}$.
If~$\mathcal{M}$
halts on the input then $\UTM$ 
will halt and give the same output, 
while if $\mathcal{M}$ does not halt on that input 
then $\UTM$ also does not halt.

This single machine can be made to have any desired computable behavior.
So we don't need infinitely many different machines, we can just use $\UTM$.
%</discussion:UniversalTM> 

\margingraphic[l]{0.2\textwidth}{background/pix/snake.png}{\label{fig:ouroboros}\href{https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=36915535}{An ouroboros, a snake swallowing its own tail}}\index{ouroboros}
Before stating Turing's theorem, 
we first address an often-asked question about whether it is even possible.
The machine~$\UTM$ may seem to present a 
chicken and egg problem:~how can we give a Turing machine as input to 
a Turing machine?
In particular, since $\UTM$ is itself a Turing machine,
the theorem seems to allow the possibility of giving it
to itself\Dash won't feeding a machine to itself lead to infinite regress?

\label{page:RepresentingTM}
We run Turing machines 
by loading symbols on the tape
and pressing \texttt{Start}.
So we don't feed machines with machines; we feed them symbols, representations
of things. 
True, we can feed $\UTM$ a specification of 
itself, such as a pair $e,x$
where~$e$ is the index number of $\UTM$, and is thus 
computationally equivalent to that machine's source,
and $x$ is the input.
Even so,
the universe won't collapse\Dash
we can absolutely use a text editor to edit its own source,
or ask a compiler to generate its own executable.
Similarly, 
we can feed $\UTM$ its own index number. 
Lots of interesting things happen as a result, 
but there is no inherent impossibility.

%<*th:UniversalTM>
\begin{theorem}[Turing, 1936] \label{th:UTM}
There is a Turing machine that when
given the inputs $e$ and~$x$ will 
\citetext{have the same output behavior}{%
  A technical point:~Turing machines have a tape alphabet.
  So a universal machine's input or output can only
  involve symbols that it is defined as able to use. 
  If another machine has a different tape alphabet
  then how can the universal machine simulate it?
  As usual, we define things so that the universal machine
  manipulates representations of the other machine's alphabet.
  This is similar to the way that an everyday computer represents decimals
  using binary.} 
as does $\TM_e$ on input~$x$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:UniversalTM>

\margingraphicnomag*{0.2\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp14.pdf}
This is a 
\definend{Universal Turing Machine}.\footnote{%
  We could also define a Universal Turing machine to 
  take the single-number input $\cantor(e,x)$.}
\index{Universal Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!universal}%
% We denote such a machine with~$\UTM$.
Universal machines are familiar from
everyday computing.
% Universal machines
% change hardware behavior in software.
% \citetext{This is what a computer's operating
% system does}{%
% }.
This figure\footnote{% 
  This is a 
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  flowchart
}{%
  \citetext{flowchart}{%
    Flowcharts are widely used to sketch algorithms; here is one from XKCD.
    \protect\begin{center}
    \protect\small
    \protect\begin{tabular}{c}
      \protect\includegraphics[width=0.25\textwidth]{background/pix/xkcdcalc.png} \\
      \protect\label{fig:xkcdcalc}\protect\href{https://xkcd.com/2117/}{Courtesy xkcd} % wikipedia
    \protect\end{tabular}
    \protect\end{center}
    See also~\protect\url{http://archive.computerhistory.org/resources/text/Remington_Rand/Univac.Flowmatic.1957.102646140.pdf}.}, 
}
  which gives a high level sketch of a routine.
  We use three types of boxes.
  Round corner boxes are for \protect\texttt{Start} and 
  \protect\texttt{End}.
  Rectangles are for ordinary operations on data.
  Diamond boxes, which will appear in later charts, 
  are for decisions, \protect\texttt{if} statements.
}\index{flow chart} 
outlines the action of a computer operating system
that is given a program to run and some data for that program.
Think of the program 
as like some Turing machine, $\TM_e$, and 
think of the data as a string of \str{0}'s and \str{1}'s that we can 
interpret as a number,~$x$.
The operating system loads the program and then feeds it the input.
That is, the operating system arranges that 
the hardware will
behave like machine~$e$ with input~$x$. 
As with an operating system, Universal Turing machines
change their behavior in software.
No patch chords.

Another computing experience that is like a universal
machine is an interpreter.
Below is an interaction with the Racket interpreter.
At the first prompt we enter the source of a routine that inputs 
\codeinline!i! and returns the 
sum of the first \codeinline!i!~numbers.
At the second prompt the interpreter 
runs that source with~$i=4$.
\begin{lstlisting}
$ racket
Welcome to Racket v8.3 [cs].
> (define (sum i tot)
      (if (= i 0)
          tot
          (sum (- i 1) (+ tot i))))
> (sum 4 0)
10
\end{lstlisting}  % $
\label{page:UTM}

The most direct example of
computing systems that act as universal machines 
is a language's \codeinline!eval! statement.\index{eval}
At the first prompt below we define a routine that has
\citetext{the interpreter evaluate the expression that is input}{%
  Writing a program that allows general users to  
  evaluate arbitrary code
  is powerful but not safe,
  especially if these users are just surfing in from the Internet.
  Restricting which commands the user can evaluate,
  known as sandboxing, forms part of
  being careful with that power.
  For us, however, the software engineering issues are not relevant.}
In the next prompt we define a list, quoted so that it is not interpreted.
This list is the source of a function; 
\codeinline!lambda (i) ...! defines a function of one input, $i$
(in contrast to the definitions
of the functions \codeinline!sum! and \codeinline!utm!,
this function does not have a name and so the definition syntax is
different).
In the third and fourth prompts,
the interpreter evaluates that list and applies 
it to the numbers~$5$ and~$0$.
That is, like the loom's punched cards, 
different inputs cause \codeinline!utm!
to have different behaviors.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (define (utm s)
      (eval s))
> (define test '(lambda (i) (if (= i 0) 1 0)))
> ((utm test) 5)
0
> ((utm test) 0)
1
\end{lstlisting}

Finally, as to the proof of the theorem, 
the simplest way to prove that something exists is to produce it.
We have already exhibited what amounts to a Universal Turing machine.
At the end of Chapter One, on
page~\pageref{extra:TuringMachineSimilator}, 
we gave code for a Turing machine simulator, which
reads a Turing machine from a file
and then runs it.
The code is in Racket but 
Church's Thesis asserts that we could write a Turing machine
with the same behavior.
% (For a Universal Turing machine that meets the criteria, we must not input
% the arbitrary Turing machine from a file, but instead we
% input its index, which we then convert to a Turing machine.
% But we've already argued that we can write a routine to go from 
% the index to the Turing machine.)
\index{Universal Turing machine|)}\index{Turing machine!universal|)}




\subsection{Uniformity}\index{uniformity|(}
Consider this job:~given a real number $r\in\R$,
write a program to produce its digits.
More precisely, the job is to produce a $\TM_r$
such that when given $n\in\N$ as input, $\TM_r$ outputs the
$n$-th decimal place of~$r$ (for~$n=0$,
it outputs the integer to the left of the decimal point).

We know that 
this is not possible for all~$r$ because there
are uncountably many real numbers but only countably many Turing machines.
But what stops us?
One of the enjoyable things about coding is the feeling of being able to
get the machine to do anything we want\Dash why can't we 
output whatever digits we like?  

There certainly are real numbers 
for which there is such a program.
One is~$0.25$.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (one-quarter-decimal-places n)
  (cond 
      [(= n 0) 0]
      [(= n 1) 2]
      [(= n 2) 5]
      [else 0]))
\end{lstlisting}
For a more generic number, say, some $r=0.703\ldots$\,,
we might momentarily imagine brute-forcing it.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (r-decimal-place n)
  (cond
    [(= n 0) 0]
    [(= n 1) 7]
    [(= n 2) 0]
    [(= n 3) 3]
    ...
  ))
\end{lstlisting}
But that's silly.
Programs have finite length and so  
can't have infinitely many cases.

That is, because of the \codeinline!if!,
what the following program does on~$n=7$ 
is unconnected to what it does on other inputs.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (foo n)
  (if (= n 7)
      42
      (* 2 n)))
\end{lstlisting}
But a program can only have finitely many such differently-behaving
branches.
The fact that a Turing machine has only finitely many instructions
imposes a condition of uniformity on its behavior.

\begin{example} \label{ex:NineInPi}
Connecting in this way the idea that `something is computable' with
`it is uniformly computable'
has some surprising consequences.
Consider the problem of producing
a program that inputs a number~$n$ and  
decides whether somewhere in the decimal expansion of $\pi=3.14159\ldots$
there are $n$ 
\citetext{consecutive nines}{%
  At the $762$-nd decimal point there are 
  six nines in a row.  
  This is call the Feynman point; see 
  \protect\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feynman_point}.
  Most experts guess that for any~$n$ the decimal 
  expansion contains a sequence of 
  $n$ consecutive nines but no one has proved or disproved that.}.    

There are two possibilities.
Either for all~$n$ such a sequence exists, or else there is some
$n_0$ where a sequence of nines exists for lengths less than~$n_0$ 
and no sequence exists when $n\geq n_0$.
Consequently the problem is solved:~one of the two below is the right program
(although at this moment we don't know which).
\begin{center}
\vspace*{-4ex}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (sequence-of-nines-0 n)
  1)
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (sequence-of-nines-1 n)
  (if (< n n0)
      1
      0))
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}

One surprising aspect of this argument 
is that neither of the two routines appears to have much to do with $\pi$.
Also surprising, and perhaps unsettling, is that
we have shown that the problem is solvable
without showing how to solve it.
That is, 
\citetext{there is a difference between showing that this function is computable}{%
  This is a little like  
  Schr\"odinger's cat paradox
  (see \protect\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schr\%C3\%B6dinger's_cat}) 
  in that it seems that one of the two is right 
  but we just don't know which.
  }
\begin{equation*}
  f(n)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\pi$ has $n$ consecutive nines}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
and possessing an algorithm to compute it.
This observation shows that if the assertion
\citetext{``something is computable if you can write
  a program for it''}{From \autocite{SE:JohnL}:
  ``Most people, I believe, felt a bit disoriented the first time when this 
  kind of proof/conclusion was encountered. 
  % Or at least myself.
  % The essential point is 
  % we do not have to identify/construct/bind to one 
  % algorithm that decides [it].
  \ldots{} 
  We do not have to understand fully what is [the problem]. 
  All we need is there exists an algorithm that decides [it], 
  whatever [the answer] turns out to be. 
  This deviates from \ldots{} the naive sense of decidability \ldots{} 
  that you might have even before you encountered the theory of 
  computation.''}
is not actually false, at the very least it 
suppresses some important subtleties.

In contrast, consider a routine that inputs~$i\in\N$ and outputs 
\citetext{the $i$-th decimal place of $\pi$}{%
  As we have noted, some real numbers have two decimal
  representations, one ending in $0$'s and one ending in $9$'s.
  But every such number is rational (as ``ending in $0$'s'' implies)
  and $\pi$ is not rational, so $\pi$ is not one of these numbers.}.
Using it, we can write a program that takes in~$n$ and
steps through the digits of~$\pi$, 
looking for~$n$ consecutive nines.
With this approach we are constructing the answer,
not just saying that it exists.
This approach is also uniform in the sense that we could 
modify it to use other routines
and so look for strings of nines in other numbers. 
However this approach 
has the disadvantage that if there is an $n_0$ 
where $\pi$ does not have $n$ consecutive nines for $n\geq n_0$
then this program will
search forever without discovering that.
\end{example}
\index{uniformity|)}




\subsection{Parametrization}\index{parametrization|(}
Universality says that there is a Turing machine that 
takes in inputs $e$ and~$x$  and returns the same value as
we would get by running $\TM_e$ on input~$x$,
including not halting if the machine does not halt on that input.
That is, there is a computable
function $\map{\phi}{\N^2}{\N}$ such that
$\phi(e,x)=\TMfcn_e(x)$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ and
$\phi(e,x)\diverges$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\diverges$.

There, the~$e$ travels from the function's argument to the index.
We now generalize.
Start with a program that takes two inputs
such as this one.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (P x y)
  (+ x y))
\end{lstlisting}
Freeze the first argument.
The result is a one-input program.
Here we freeze $x$ at~$7$ and at $8$.
\begin{center}
\vspace*{-4ex}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (P_7 y)
  (P 7 y))
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (P_8 y)
  (P 8 y))
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}%
\vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}
This is \citetext{\definend{partial application}}{%
  See \autocite{wiki:PartialApplication}.}
because we are not freezing all of the input variables.
Instead, we are 
\citetext{\definend{parametrizing}}{%
  A parameter is a constant that varies across 
  equations of the same form.
  For instance, someone studying quadratics may consider the
  family of equations $y=ax^2$;~here,~$a$ is a parameter.
  So a parameter is a kind of fixed variable.
  (Memorable in this context is one of A~Perlis's epigrams, 
  ``One man's constant is another man's variable.''
  \autocite{Perlis})
}\index{parametrizing}\index{parameter}
the variable~$x$, resulting in a family of programs 
\codeinline!P_0!, \codeinline!P_1!, etc.

The programs in the family are related to the starting one, obviously. 
Denoting the function computed by the above starting program~\codeinline{P} 
as $\psi(x,y)=x+y$,
partial application gives a family of functions:
$\psi_0(y)=y$, $\psi_1(y)=1+y$, $\psi_2(y)=2+y$, \ldots\sentencespace
The next result says that in general,
from the index of a starting Turing machine or computable function 
and from the values that are frozen,
we can compute the family members.

%<*th:smn>
\begin{theorem}[\smn{} theorem, or Parameter theorem] \label{th:smn}
\index{Parameter theorem}\index{s-m-n theorem@$s$-$m$-$n$ theorem}
For every $m,n\in\N$
there is a computable total function $\map{s_{m,n}}{\N^{1+m}}{\N}$  
such that for an $m+n$-ary function
$\TMfcn_e(x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$,
freezing the initial $m$~variables at $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}\in\N$
gives the $n$-ary computable function
$\TMfcn_{s(e,a_0,\ldots a_{m-1})}(x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$.\footnotemark[1]
\end{theorem}\footnotetext[1]{%
  This result is stated in terms of functions, not machines, because
  the machines need not be the same. 
  That is, they are not equal sets of four-tuple instructions.
  But they will have the same input-output behavior, which means
  the same function.}
%</th:smn>
% The function $\TMfcn_e(x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$ could be
% partial, that is, it could be that the Turing machine
% $\TM_e$ fails to halt on some inputs $x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$.

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:smni>
We will produce the function~$s$ to satisfy three 
requirements:~\citetext{it must be effective}{%
  In fact, careful analysis shows that it is primitive recursive.}, 
it must input an index~$e$ and an $m$-tuple $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}$,
and it must output the index of a machine~$\hat{\TM}$ that,
when given the input $x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$,
will return the value $\TMfcn_e(a_0,\ldots a_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$, 
or fail to halt if that function diverges.

The idea is that the machine that computes~$s$
will construct the instructions for~$\hat{\TM}$.
We can get from the instruction set to the index
using Cantor's encoding, so with that we will be done.

Below on the left is
the flowchart for the machine that computes the function~$s$.
In its third box it creates the set of four-tuple instructions shown
on the right, which make the machine $\hat{\TM}\!$.
The machine on the left needs $a_0,\ldots{} a_{m-1}$ for the
right side's second, third, and fourth boxes, 
and it needs $e$ for the fifth box. 
%</pf:smni>
(In this book
we are flexible about
the convention for input and output representations for Turing machines.
However, 
in this proof, to be as clear as possible in the right side's flowchart, 
we assume that input
is encoded in unary, that
inputs are separated with a single blank, and that
when the machine is started the head should be under the input's
left-most \str{1}.)
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts00.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts01.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:smnii>

The Turing machine $\hat{\TM}$ 
does not first read its inputs $x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$.
Instead, it first moves left and writes $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}$ on the tape,
in unary and separated by blanks, and with a blank between $a_{m-1}$ 
and~$x_m$.
(Recall that the $a_i$ are parameters, not variables.
They are fixed. 
They are, so to speak,
hard-coded into $\hat{\TM}\!$, which is how it knows what to write.)
Then using universality, $\hat{P}$ simulates
Turing machine~$P_e$ and lets it run on the entire list of inputs
now on the tape,
$a_0,\ldots{} a_{m-1},x_m,\ldots{} x_{m+n-1}$.
%</pf:smnii>
\end{proof}

% That argument uses $a$'s for the values that the $x$~variables are frozen to.
% In the future we won't maintain that convention, as we will be thinking of the $x$'s as parameters and
% so sometimes varying them. 

In the notation $s_{m,n}$, 
the subscript~$m$ is the number of inputs being frozen
while~$n$ is the number of inputs left free.
These subscripts can be a bother and 
we often omit them.

The key point about the \smn{} theorem
is that it gives not just one computable function but instead a family.

\begin{example}
Consider the two-input routine sketched by this flowchart.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1007.pdf}}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine 
following the sketch, 
computing the function $\psi(x,y)=x\cdot y$.
Let that machine have index~$e_0$.
(The subscript emphasizes that this index
is a constant.)

On the left below is the flowchart sketching the machine $\TM_{s(e_0,0)}$,
which freezes the value of~$x$ to~$0$ and so 
computes the function $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0)}(y)=0$.
For example, $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0)}(5)=0$.
Similarly, the other two are flowcharts summarizing $\TM_{s(e_0,1)}$ 
and~$\TM_{s(e_0,2)}$, 
freezing the value of $x$ at $1$ and~$2$
and therefore computing the functions $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,1)}(y)=y$
and $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,2)}(y)=2y$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1008.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1009.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1010.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}\ldots
\end{center}
Here is the generic member, $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1011.pdf}}
  \tag{**}
\end{equation*}
Compare ($**$) to~($*$). 
The difference is that this machine does not read~$x$;  
rather, thinking of these as programs instead of
Turing machines, $x$ is hard-coded into the source body.
\end{example}

In summary, the \smn{} Theorem gives a sequence of computable 
functions such as $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$ that
is a family in that the indices
are given by a computable function.  
The family is parametrized by~$x$, since $e_0$ is fixed. 

Restated, this family is uniformly computable\Dash there 
is a computable function~$s$ (more precisely, $s_{1,1}$) going from 
the index~$e$ and the parameter value~$x$ to the index of the
result in~($**$).
So the \smn{} Theorem is about uniformity.
\index{parametrization|)}




% ..............................................................
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Started on \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/questions/69197/difference-between-turing-machine-and-universal-turing-machine}{Stack Exchange}
\end{credit}
Someone in your study group asks,
``What can a Universal Turing machine do that a regular Turing machine 
cannot?''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  A Universal Turing machine is a regular machine, in that it is just another
  machine in the list $\TM_0$, $\TM_1$, \ldots\sentencespace
  Perhaps your friend meant something more like, ``What does a 
  Universal Turing machine
  do that a non-universal machine does not do?''
  Every machine does a job.
  Some machines expect a single input and then double it, 
  some interpret their input as two numbers and then add them.
  A Universal Turing machine expects two inputs, $e$ and~$x$,
  and the output of this machine is the same as the output
  of the machine $\TM_e$ on input~$x$,
  including failing to halt if that machine fails to halt.
  It is universal in that any input-output behavior that can be mechanically 
  produced can be produced by this one device.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Has anyone ever built a Universal Turing machine 
or a device equivalent to one, or is it a theory-only thing?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Every general purpose computer, including any standard laptop or cell phone,
  is equivalent in computable power to a Universal Turing machine.

  A person can object that a standard laptop for instance
  does not have unboundedly much memory.
  That's true.
  But such a machine can store things in the Cloud and so is not restricted to
  the installed memory, and in this sense its memory is unbounded.

  A person can go on to worry that there are only so many bits available in the
  physical universe and thus memory is not in the end 
  unbounded.
  Perhaps that is true\Dash although we could worry back that we have not 
  got enough time
  to exhaust what bits that there are before the Big Bang becomes a Big Crunch,
  or whatever it will become\Dash 
  but at the least we can take
  general purpose devices to be so very close to 
  Universal Turing machines that it is quite hard to tell them apart.
  In any event, the Universal Turing machine is the right
  mathematical idealization.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can a Universal Turing machine simulate another Universal Turing machine,
or for that matter can it simulate itself?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes.
  If $\TM_{e_0}$ and $\TM_{e_1}$ are universal then giving the first one
  the inputs $e_1$ and~$x$ will result in an input-output behavior
  exactly like the second.
  Likewise, giving  $\TM_{e_0}$ the inputs $e_0$ and~$x$ will result in 
  the machine behaving just like itself. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  From a 
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/41854/67754}{Stack Exchange question}.
\end{credit}
Your class has someone who says, 
``Universal Turing machines make no sense to me.
How could a machine simulate 
another machine that has more states?''
Correct their misimpression.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer is that it does those things in software.
  The universal machine does not use its states to simulate the states
  of the simulated machine.
  Rather, it has codes for those states that it manipulates on the tape.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there more than one Universal Turing machine?
\begin{answer}\verified
Yes, every computable function has infinitely many 
indices by~\cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}.
So if $\TM_e$ is universal, then the associated function~$\TMfcn_e$
has infinitely many unequal indices so that 
$\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e_0}=\TMfcn_{e_1}=\cdots\,$.
The machines 
$\TM_{e_0}$, $\TM_{e_1}$, \ldots{} are all universal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What happens if we feed a Universal Turing machine to itself?
For instance, where the index~$e_0$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e,x)=\TMfcn_e(x)$ for all~$x$,
what is the value of 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$?
\begin{answer}
We know that $5$ equals $\cantor(2,0)$, that is, $\pair(5)=\sequence{2,0}$.
So in $\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$ the argument to $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ is the
value of $\TMfcn_2(0)$.
So, where $\pair(\TMfcn_2(0))=\sequence{a,b}$, the value of 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$ is $\TMfcn_a(b)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the function $f(x_0,x_1)=3x_0+x_0\cdot x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$4$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeze $x_1$ to be~$0$.
  What is the resulting function?  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We write $f_{i,a}$ for the function produced by freezing $x_i$
to the value~$a$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The resulting one-variable function is $f_{0,4}(x_1)=12+4x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{0,5}(x_1)=15+5x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{1,0}(x_0)=3x_0$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$f(x_0,x_1,x_2)=x_0+2x_1+3x_2$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$1$.
  What is the resulting two-variable function?
\item What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $2$?
\item Let $a$ be a natural number.
  What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $a$?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$ and $x_1$ at~$3$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item What one-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be~$a$
  and $x_1$ to be~$b$, for $a,b\in\N$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We use $\hat{f}$ for the name of each function.
\begin{exparts}
\item The resulting two-variable function is 
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=1+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item The result is the two-variable function  
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=2+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item This gives 
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=a+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item This results in the one-variable function  
  $\hat{f}(x_2)=11+3x_2$.
\item The result is   
  $\hat{f}(x_2)=a+2b+3x_2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e_0$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1000.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(2)$?
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,0}(e_0,0)}(2)$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The two subscripts on $s_{1,1}$ mean that
  we will freeze the first input and leaves the second one as
  a variable.
  So $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}$ will be a one-input function.
  The arguments $e_0$ and~$1$ indicate that we are working on 
  Turing machine~$e_0$,
  the one whose flow chart is given in the problem statement,
  and that the input is frozen to be $x_0=1$.
  This flowchart gives a sense of the result.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1001.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(x_1)=1+x_1$.
\item The values are $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(1)=2$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(2)=3$.
\item The notation $s_{1,1}(e_0,0)$ means that we work on Turing machine~$e_0$,
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=0$, and 
  that we leave the second input as
  a variable.
  This flowchart gives the idea.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1002.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(x_1)=0+x_1=x_1$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(0)=0$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(1)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(2)=2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart have index~$e_0$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1003.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(0,1)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(1,0)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(2,3)$
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(2)$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The notation $s_{1,2}(e_0,1)$ indicates that
  we work on $\TM_{e_0}$, 
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=1$, 
  and that we leave the second and third inputs as variables
  This flowchart sketches the behavior.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1004.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(x_1,x_2)=1+x_1\cdot x_2$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(0,1)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(1,0)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e_0,1)}(2,3)=7$.
\item The $s_{2,1}(e,1,2)$ says that we start with $\TM_{e_0}$,
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=1$ and the second input to 
  $x_1=2$, 
  and leave the third input as a variable.
  This sketches the result.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1005.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(x_2)=1+2\cdot x_2$.
\item The values are $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(1)=3$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,1,2)}(2)=5$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e_0$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1006.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(5)$.
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(5)$.
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,2)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,2)}(5)$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,0)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,1)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,2)}(x_1)\diverges$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,2)}(5)\diverges$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Let the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart have index~$e_0$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1012.pdf}
\end{center}
We will describe the family of functions parametrized by the arguments~$x_0$
and~$x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Use \cref{th:smn}, the \smn{} theorem, to fix $x_0=0$ and~$x_1=3$.
  Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0,3)}(5)$?
\item Use the \smn{} theorem to fix $x_0=1$.
  Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,1,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,1,3)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,a,b)}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This flowchart sketches $\TM_{s(e_0,0,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1013.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0,3)}(y)=3y$.
  Consequently,  $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,0,3)}(5)=15$.
\item This is the flowchart sketching $\TM_{s(e_0,1,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1014.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The computed function is $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,1,3)}(y)=3+y$,
  and consequently  $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,1,3)}(5)=8$.
\item This is the flowchart sketching $\TM_{s(e_0,a,b)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1015.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The distinction between this and the flowchart in the question statement
  is that here the machine does not read in~$x_0$ or~$x_1$.  
  Those two are hard-coded, as $a$ and~$b$, 
  into the program body.
  This is a family of functions
  parametrized by~$a$ and~$b$, and the indices of these functions are
  uniformly computable from $e_0$, $a$, and~$b$, using the 
  \smn{} function~$s$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a total computable function $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$
such that Turing machine $\TM_{g(n)}$ computes the function 
$y\mapsto y+n^2$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Start with the function $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$ defined by $\psi(x,y)=y+x^2$. 
  It is intuitively computable, since it is computed by the
  machine sketched by this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1016.pdf}
  \end{center}
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
  Suppose that machine has index~$e_0$, that is, 
  suppose that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x,y)$.
  By the \smn{} theorem there is a family of computable functions
  parametrized by~$n$, $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,n)}$,
  with the desired behavior, $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e_0,n)}(y)=y+n^2$.  
  Take $g(n)$ to be~$s_{1,1}(e_0,n)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a total computable function $\map{g}{\N^2}{\N}$
such that Turing machine $\TM_{g(m,b)}$ computes
$x\mapsto mx+b$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Start with the function $\psi(u,v,x)=ux+v$. 
  It is intuitively computable as
  sketched by this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1017.pdf}
  \end{center}
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
  Let that machine have index~$e_0$, that is, 
  suppose that $\psi(u,v,x)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(u,v,x)$.
  The \smn{} theorem gives a family of computable functions
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,m,b)}$
  parametrized by~$m$ and~$b$
  with the desired behavior, $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e_0,m,b)}(x)=m\cdot x+b$. 
  Take $g(m,b)$ to be $s_{2,1}(e_0,m,b)$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that $e_0$ is such that $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ is the function computed by a 
Universal Turing machine, meaning that if given the input $\cantor(e,x)$
then it returns the same value as $\TM_e(x)$.
Suppose also that~$e_1$ is such that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=4x$ for all~$x\in\N$.
Determine, if possible, the value of these
(if it is not possible then briefly describe why not).
\begin{exparts}
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_1,5))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_1}(\cantor(e_0,5))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_0,\cantor(e_1,5)))$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  This gives the same result as $\TMfcn_{e_1}(5)$, that is, it converges
  and gives~$20$.
\item
  This returns $4$ times the the value of
  $\cantor(e_0,5)$.
\item This returns $\TMfcn_{e_1}(5)=20$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Suppose that $e_0$ is such that $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e,x))$
returns the same value as $\TMfcn_e(x)$ (or does not converge if
that function does not converge).
Suppose also that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=x+2$
and that $\TMfcn_{e_2}(x)=x^2$, for all~$x\in\N$.
If possible determine the value of these
(if it is not possible, say why not).
\begin{exparts}
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_1,4))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(4,e_1))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_1}(\cantor(e_0,\cantor(e_2,3)))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor{e_0,\cantor(e_0,4)})$
% \item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor{e_0,\cantor{e_2,e_1}})$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  This gives the same result as $\TMfcn_{e_1}(4)$, that is, it gives~$6$.
\item
  This returns the value of $\phi_4(e_1)$, whatever that is.
\item This returns $\TMfcn_{e_2}(3)$ plus~$2$, which is~$11$.
\item Because $\pair(4)=\sequence{1,1}$, this returns the same as 
  $\TMfcn_1(1)$, whatever that is.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

%================================================
\section{The \HP}\index{Halting problem|(} \label{sec:HaltingProblem}
We've showed that
there are functions that are not mechanically computable.
We gave a counting argument, that there are countably many Turing machines but 
uncountably many functions and so there are functions with no
associated machine.
While knowing what's true is great,
even better is to exhibit a specific 
function that is unsolvable.
We will now do that.



\subsection{Definition} \label{sec:Unsolvability}
The natural approach to producing such a function is to go through 
Cantor's Theorem and effectivize it,
to turn the proof into a construction.

Here is an illustrative table adapted from the discussion of
Cantor's Theorem on page~\pageref{ex:DiagonalizationTable}.\index{diagonalization!effectivized}
Imagine that this table's rows are the computable functions and 
its columns are the inputs.
For instance, this table lists $\phi_2(3)=5$.
\begin{center} \small \label{HPDiagonalizationTable}
  %<*table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \setlength{\arraycolsep}{0.75\arraycolsep}
  \begin{minipage}{4em}\vspace*{0cm}\tabulartext{Function}\end{minipage}
  \begin{tabular}{r|ccccccc@{\hspace*{1em}$\ldots$}}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } 
    &\multicolumn{7}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Input}}}  \\
     % \multicolumn{1}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Function}}}
     \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &$0$ &$1$ &$2$ &$3$ &$4$ &$5$ &$6$ \\ 
   \cline{2-8}
   \rule{0em}{2.15ex}% bit more vertical room 
   $\phi_0$  &$3$  &$1$  &$2$  &$7$  &$7$  &$0$  &$4$  \\  
   $\phi_1$  &$0$  &$5$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  \\  
   $\phi_2$  &$1$  &$4$  &$1$  &$5$  &$9$  &$2$  &$6$  \\  
   $\phi_3$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  \\  
   $\phi_4$  &$1$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  \\
   $\phi_5$  &$6$  &$2$  &$5$  &$5$  &$4$  &$1$  &$8$  \\
   \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\vdotswithin{0}$} 
    &\multicolumn{6}{c}{$\vdotswithin{0}$}  
  \end{tabular}
  %</table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.75fil}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:HP>
Diagonalizing means considering the machine on the right.
It moves down the array's diagonal, changing the~$3$, changing the~$5$, 
etc.\sentencespace
Thus, when~$e=0$ then the output is~$4$,
when~$e=1$ then the output is~$6$, etc.\sentencespace
Our goal with this machine is to 
ensure that no computable function, none of the table's rows,
has the same input-output relationship as the machine.

But that's a puzzle, an apparent contradiction.
The flowchart outlines
an effective procedure\Dash we can implement this  
using a Universal Turing machine\Dash and thus its output should be one of the
rows.

What's the puzzle's resolution?
The program's first, second, fourth, and fifth boxes are trivial, so the
issue must involve the box in the middle.
The answer is that there must be
an $e\in\N$ so that $\phi_e(e)\diverges$, and for that number the 
machine in the flowchart never gets through the middle box
and consequently never prints the output.
That is, 
to avoid a contradiction the above table must contain $\uparrow\,$'s.

So this puzzle has led to an important insight:~the fact that some computations 
fail to halt on 
some inputs is very important.
%</discussion:HP>

%<*prob:HP>
\begin{problem}[\HP]\hspace*{-0.25em}\footnotemark %
\index{Halting problem}\index{problem!Halting}\label{prob:HaltingProblem}
Given~$e\in\N$, determine whether $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$, 
that is, whether Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.
\end{problem}\footnotetext{%
  We use a distinct typeface for problem names, as in 
  `\protect\probname{Halting}'.}
%</prob:HP>

%<*def:K>
\begin{definition}\index{K, the Halting problem set@$K$, the Halting problem set}
  The \definend{\HP{} set} is $\definend{K}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:K>

% Obviously for any $e\in\N$, either $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$ or
% $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$.
% The \HP{} is about whether we can settle which numbers are
% members of the set~$K$ with a computer.

%<*th:HPIsUnsolvable>
\begin{theorem} %[Unsolvability of the \HP]
\index{Halting problem!unsolvability}
The \HP{} is unsolvable by any Turing machine.
\end{theorem}
%</th:HPIsUnsolvable>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
Assume otherwise, that there exists a Turing machine with this behavior.
\begin{equation*}
  \charfcn{K}(e)=K(e)=
  \computablefunction{halt\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
  1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}  \\
  0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Then the function below is also mechanically computable.
The flowchart illustrates how $f$ is constructed;
it uses the above function in its decision box.
(\citetext{In the top case the particular output value $42$ doesn't matter}{%
  We often use $42$ when we need a nominal output value
  because of its connection with 
  \protect\textit{The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy},
  \autocite{Adams}.
  See also \autocite{wiki:foobar}.},
all that matters is that~$f$ converges.)
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.35\textwidth}
  \begin{equation*}
  f(e)=
    \begin{cases}
    42    &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$}  \\
    \uparrow  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{minipage}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
Since this is mechanically computable, it has a Turing machine index.
Let that index be~$e_0$, so that
$f(x)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x)$ for all inputs~$x$.

Now consider $f(e_0)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0)$
(that is, feed the machine $\TM_{e_0}$ its own index).
If it diverges then the first clause in the definition of~$f$ means that
$f(e_0)\converges$,
which contradicts the assumption of divergence.
If it converges then $f\,$'s second clause 
means that $f(e_0)\diverges$, also a contradiction.
Since assuming that $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$ is 
mechanically computable leads to a contradiction,
that function is not mechanically computable.
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
\end{proof}

We say that a problem is
\definend{unsolvable}\index{unsolvable problem}\index{problem!unsolvable} 
if no Turing machine has the desired input-output
behavior.
If the problem is to compute the answers to `yes' or `no' questions,
that is, to decide membership in a set,
then we say that the set is
\citetext{\definend{undecidable}}{%
  The word `undecidable' is used in mathematics in two different ways.
  The definition here of course applies to the Theory of Computation.
  In relation to results such as 
  G\"{o}del's theorems, it means that a statement is 
  cannot be proved true or proved false within a given formal system.}.\index{undecidable}\index{set!undecidable}\index{Halting problem|)}\index{problem!Halting|)}
With Church's Thesis in mind, we interpret these to mean that the problem or set
is unsolvable by any discrete mechanism.



%================================================
\subsection{General unsolvability}\index{general unsolvability|(}
We have named one task, the \HP{}, that no mechanical device can solve.
With that one in hand we are able to show that 
a wide class of jobs cannot be done. 
That is, the \HP{} is part of a larger unsolvability phenomenon.

\begin{example} \label{ex:HaltOnThree}
Consider this problem:~we want to know if a 
given Turing machine halts on the input~$3$.\index{Halts On Three problem@\probname{Halts On Three} problem}
That is: given~$e$, does $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$?

We will show 
that if this 
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
were a computable function then we could compute the solution of the \HP.
That's impossible, so we will then know that 
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ is also not
computable.
% \margingraphicnomag*[r]{.3\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}

Our strategy is to create a scheme where being able to determine
whether an arbitrary machine halts on~$3$ allows us to settle questions about
the \HP{}.
Imagine that we have a particular~$e$ and want to know whether 
$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$.
Consider the machine outlined on the right below.
It reads the input~$y$ and ignores it, and also gives a nominal 
output.
% (as mentioned earlier, we find it convenient for routines to 
%  have input and output even if they are only nominal).
Its action is in the middle box, where the code uses a universal Turing machine 
to simulate running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$.
If that halts then the machine on the right as a whole halts, for any input.
If not then this machine as a whole
does not halt.

\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}
\end{center}
In particular, the machine on the right halts on input $y=3$ if and only if 
$\TM_x$ halts on~$x$ (this is also true for all other $y$'s but
we don't care).
So with this we can leverage being able
to answer questions about
halting on~$3$ to answer
questions about whether $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.

We are ready for the argument.
Consider this function.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    42 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Observe that~$\psi$ 
is computed by the flowchart above on the left.
So it is intuitively computable. 
By Church's Thesis
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That machine has some index,~$e_0$, meaning that $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$.

Use the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, giving $\phi_{s(e_0,x)}$.
This is a family of functions, one for~$x=0$, one for~$x=1$, etc. %\sentencespace
Below are the associated machines.
Note that each has a `Read~$y$' but no `Read~$x$';~for these machines 
the value used in the middle box is hard-coded into the source.
Note also that the flowchart on the right
is the same as the one on the right in the strategy discussion above.
That is, machine $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}$ halts on input $y=3$ if and only
if $\TM_x$ halts on input~$x$. 
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp15.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp16.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}\hspace*{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}
\end{center}

Therefore, for all $x\in\N$ we have this.
\begin{equation*}
 \phi_x(x)\converges 
  \quad\text{if and only if}\quad
 \computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}(\,s(e_0,x)\,)=1
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
The function~$s$ is computable so 
if $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ were  
computable then the entire right side of ($*$) would be computable.
That would in turn imply that the \HP{} on the left side is computably solvable, 
which it isn't.
Therefore $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$
is not computable.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
The subscript~$0$ is there as 
signal that $e_0$ is constant, since it is the index of the 
machine that computes~$\psi$.
In the above family of infinitely many computable functions, 
the parameter~$x$ is what varies.
We use that in~($*$).
\end{remark}

\begin{example} \label{ex:OutputsSeven}
We will show that this problem is not mechanically solvable: given~$e$,
determine whether $\TM_e$ outputs~$7$ for some input.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=7$ for some~$y$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
We will follow the prior example as a model.
Consider this.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    7 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The flowchart on the left below sketches how to compute~$\psi$.
Thus it is intuitively mechanically computable
and Church's Thesis says that
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That Turing machine has an index,~$e_0$, so that~$\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp08.pdf}
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp09.pdf}
\end{center}
The \smn{} theorem gives 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e_0,x)}$ parametrized by $x$.
% As in the prior example, there is an associated family of 
% infinitely many different machines, 
% one with $x=0$, one with $x=1$, etc.  %\sentencespace 
On the right is the flowchart for the $x$-th machine in the family. 
As in the prior example note that~$x$ is hard-coded into its source, 
so it is a single-input machine.
This $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}$ has the property that there exists an input~$y$
such that the machine outputs a~$7$ if and only if $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.

Then $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
If the function $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}$ were 
computable then because the composition of two computable functions
$\composed{\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}}{\mspace{2mu}s}$
is computable we would have that the \HP{} is computably solvable, 
which is not right.
Therefore $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}$ is not computable.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:Doubles}
We next show that this problem is unsolvable:~given $e$,
decide whether $\TMfcn_e$
is the doubler function, that is, whether $\TMfcn_e(y)=2y$ for all~$y$. 

We will show that this function is not computable.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{doubler\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=2y$ for all~$y$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The function on the left below
is intuitively computable by the flowchart in the middle.
\begin{center}
  $
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    2y &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  $
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp10.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp11.pdf}}
\end{center}
So Church's Thesis says that there is a Turing machine that computes it.
Let that machine's index be~$e_0$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e_0,x)}$ parametrized by $x$. 
The machine $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}$ is sketched by the flowchart on the right.
Then $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{doubler\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
So the supposition that $\computablefunction{doubler\_decider}$ is 
computable implies that the \HP{} is computably solvable, 
which is wrong.
\end{example}

These examples show that the \HP{}
serves as a touchstone for unsolvability:~often
we prove that something is unsolvable by demonstrating that
if we could solve it then we could solve the \HP{}.
We say that the \HP{}  
\definend{reduces to}\index{reduction from the Halting problem to another}\index{Halting problem!reduction to another problem}\index{reductions between problems}\label{pageref:reducesto}
the given problem.\footnote{%
  Often newcomers get this terminology backwards.
  \protect\citetext{We are using `reduces to'}{%
      An engineer, a physicist and a mathematician are asleep 
      in a conference hotel when their coffee machine breaks into fire.
      The engineer grabs the fire extinguisher, sprays like crazy,
      and when the fire is out they all go back to sleep.

      Then the room refrigerator breaks out. 
      The physicist 
      remembers that the fire extinguisher is now empty, quickly estimates the
      fire's temperature and energy output, and fills the trash can with 
      the right amount of water, putting the fire out.
      They all go back to sleep.

      Incredibly now the TV starts.
      The mathematician wakes up, remembers what happened, and hands the
      trash can to the physicist, thus reducing to the prior solution.
    }
   in the same sense that we would in saying in Calculus, 
  ``finding the area under the graph of a polynomial reduces to 
  antidifferentiating that polynomial,'' 
  meaning that if we can do the latter then we can do the former.}
Thus for instance the \HP{} reduces to the problem of determining whether
a given Turing machine halts on input~$3$.
\index{general unsolvability|)}



\subsection{Discussion}\index{Halting problem!discussion|(}
The unsolvability of the \HP{} is one of the most important results
in the Theory of Computation.
Before closing this section we will make a few points.

First, to reiterate, when we say that a problem is
unsolvable we mean that it is unsolvable
by a mechanism, that  
no Turing machine computes the solution to the problem.
There are functions that solve it, but those functions are
\citetext{not effectively computable}{%
  \autocite{wiki:ouija}}.

Second,  
the fact that the \HP{} is unsolvable does not mean that, 
given any program, we cannot tell if it halts. 
Obviously this program halts for every input.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (define (successor i)
    (+ 1 i))
\end{lstlisting}
Nor does the unsolvability of the \HP{} mean that we cannot tell if a program 
does not halt.
This one,
\begin{lstlisting}
> (define (f x)
    (displayln x)
    (f (+ 1 x)))
\end{lstlisting}
once started, just keeps going 
(below, control-C interrupted the run).
\begin{lstlisting}
> (f 0)
0
1
  ...
97806
97807
; user break [,bt for context]
\end{lstlisting}
\noindent Instead, the unsolvability of the \HP{}
says that there is no single program that for all~$e$ correctly decides 
in a finite time whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.

That sentence contains the qualifier `single program' because for any index~$e$,
either $\TM_e$ halts on~$e$ or else it does not and
thus for any~$e$ 
\citetext{one of these two programs produces the right answer}{%
  See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yes_(Unix)}.}.
\begin{center}
% \vspace*{-4ex}
\vspace*{-\ht\strutbox}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (yes e)
  (display 1))
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (no e)
  (display 0))
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
% \vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}
Of course, guessing which one applies is not what we have in mind
for solving the \HP.
We want uniformity.
We want a single effective procedure, one program, that inputs~$e$
and that outputs the right answer.

The sentence above also includes the qualifier `finite time'. 
We could write code that
