reads an input~$e$ and simulates $\TM_e$ on input~$e$.
This is a uniform approach because it is a single program. 
If $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ halts then our code would discover that.  
But if it does not then 
our code would not get that result in a finite time.

In short, the second point is that 
the unsolvability of the Halting Problem is about 
the non-existence of a single program that works across all
indices.
The result speaks to uniformity,
or rather, it says that uniformity is impossible.


% \subsection{Significance}\index{Halting problem!significance|(}  \label{subsec:Significance}
Our third point is about why unsolvability of the \HP{} 
is so important in the subject.\index{Halting problem!significance}
A beginning programming class could leave the 
impression that if a program doesn't halt then
it just has a bug, something fixable.
So it could seem to a student in that course 
that the \HP{} is not interesting. 

That impression is wrong.
Imagine that we could somehow write a 
utility $\computablefunction{always\_halt}$
that inputs any source~$\TM$
and adjusts it so that for any input where $\TM$ does not halt,
the modified program will halt (with some nominal output) but the utility
does not change any outputs where $\TM$ does halt.
That would give a 
list of total functions like the one on page~\pageref{HPDiagonalizationTable},
and diagonalization would give a contradiction.
Thus, in any general computational scheme
there must be some computations that halt on all inputs, 
some that halt on no inputs, 
and some that \citetext{halt on a proper subset of inputs but not on the rest}{%
  A 
  Turing machine could fail to halt because it has an infinite loop.
  The Turing machine
  $\TM_0=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},
    \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}$
  never halts, cycling forever in state $q_0$.
  We could patch this problem; we could write a program
  $\computablefunction{inf\_loop\_decider}$ that at each
  step checks whether a machine has ever before in this computation
  had the same configuration as it has now.
  This program will detect infinite loops like the prior one.
  
  However, note that there are machines that fail to halt but
  do not have loops, in that they never repeat a configuration.
  One is $\TM_1=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{1}{1},
        \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{0}}$
  which when started on a blank tape will endlessly move to the right,
  writing \str{1}'s.}.
Unsolvability of the \HP{} is inherent in the nature of computation.

This alone is enough to justify study of the problem but we will give another 
reason.
If we had a computable $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$
then we could solve many other problems.
Some we saw above in this section 
but there are others that we currently don't know how to solve
that involve unbounded search. 

For instance, a \definend{perfect number}\index{perfect number} 
is a natural number that is the 
sum of its proper positive divisors.
An example is that $6$ is perfect because $6=1+2+3$.
Another is $28 = 1 + 2 + 4 + 7 + 14$.
The next two perfect numbers are 
\citetext{$496$ and $8128$}{%
  The divisors of $496$ are
  $1$, $2$, $4$, $8$, $16$, $31$, $62$, $124$, $248$, and $496$.
  The divisors of $8128$ are
  $1$, $2$, $4$, $8$, $16$, $32$, $64$, $127$, $254$, $508$, 
  $1016$, $2032$, $4064$, and $8128$.}.
These numbers have been studied since Euclid and
today we 
\citetext{understand the form of all even perfect numbers}{%
  A number is an even perfect number if and only if it has the form 
  $(2^p-1)\cdot 2^{p-1}$ where $2^p-1$ is prime.}.
But no one knows if there are any odd perfect numbers.\footnote{%
  People have done computer checks up to $10^{1500}$ and not found any.}

\margingraphicnomag*[l]{.425\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp00.pdf}
With a solution to the Halting Problem we could settle the question.
This
program searches for an odd perfect number.\footnote{%
  This program takes an
  input $x$ but ignores it; in this book we find it convenient to have the
  machines that we use take an input and give an output.}
If it finds one then it halts.
If not then it does not halt.
So if we had a $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$ and we gave it 
the index of this program, then that
would settle whether there exists any odd perfect numbers.

There are many open questions that would 
\citetext{fall to this approach}{%
  A computer program that solved 
  the Halting Problem, if one existed, could be very slow. 
  So this might not be a feasible way to settle this question.  
  But we are studying what can be done in principle.}. 
Just to name one more:~no one knows if there is 
\citetext{any $n>4$ such that $2^{(2^n)}+1$ is prime}{%
  A number of this form that is prime is a Fermat prime.
  For $n=0$ through $4$ they are $3$, $5$, $17$, $257$, and $65537$,
  all  of which are prime.
  Computer searches up to $30$ have not found any more.}.
We could answer by writing a $\TM$ to search for one
and give its index to 
$\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$.

Before moving to the last point, 
note that unbounded search is a theme in this subject.
We saw it in defining general recursion
using $\mu$~recursion.
And, 
the obvious way to test whether $\phi_e(e)\converges$
is to 
search for a stage at which the computation halts.
It even lurks on this book's first page, since the natural algorithm
for the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}!unsolvability} 
is to, given a statement, start with the axioms and
do a breadth-first enumeration of the theorems, looking for the given one.
Turing and Church independently 
used an approach like the one in this section
to show that the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} is
unsolvable:~if there were a computable way 
to answer all mathematical 
questions then we could use it to
answer questions about Turing machines, including whether they halt.
%\index{Halting problem!significance|)}

Our final point is to make a contrast, that not every problem involving 
Turing machines is unsolvable. 
There are problems beginning with ``Given $e$'' that we can do.
One is: given~$e$, decide whether  
$\tminstruction{0}{B}{L}{1}$ is an instruction in $\TM_e$.
Note the difference between this and the ones earlier
in this section. 
The unsolvable ones 
are about the behavior of the machine but the solvable one is about
the machine's source\Dash about 
$\TM_e$ rather than $\phi_e$.
% This contrast is like
% the difference in languages between syntax and semantics.
% Syntactical tasks such as parsing a formal language are,
% as with the solvable problem of determining which instructions are in 
% a machine, doable on a computer.
% But semantic tasks involving determining the meaning of the sentences
% are, as with the earlier unsolvable problems, hard to computerize.

That point brings us back to 
the opening of the first chapter where we said
that we are most
interested in the input-output behavior of the machines, 
in what they do, and less interested
in things such as internal program structure. 
\index{Halting problem!discussion|)}


\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
Someone asks the professor, ``I don't get the point of the \HP.
If you want programs to halt then just watch them and when they exceed a
set number of cycles, send a kill signal.''
How to respond?
\begin{answer}
We have shown that there are tasks that no computer can do.
The \HP{} is a concrete example of such a task.
We will see many more.

Besides, there are many programs that we want to run without a time bound.
One is an operating system.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false: there is no function that solves the Halting Problem, that is,
there is no $f$ such that $f(e)=1$ if $\phi_e(e)\converges$ and $f(e)=0$ if
$\phi_e(e)\diverges$.
\begin{answer}\verified
False.  
There is such a function.  
But it is not computable; there is no Turing machine whose behavior is
that function.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your study partner asks you, ``The Turing machine 
$\TM=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}$
fails to halt for all inputs, that's obvious.
But these unsolvability results say that I cannot know that.
Why not?''
Explain what they missed.
\begin{answer}
The unsolvability results do not say that you cannot prove that a
particular machine does a particular thing.
The given machine does indeed fail to halt on all inputs and it
is indeed obvious.
Instead the results say that no machine can take in the index~$x$ and
correctly determine whether $\TM_x$ does that thing.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A person in your class asks,
``What is wrong with this approach to solving the \HP?
For any given Turing machine there are a finite number of states,
isn't that right?
And the tape alphabet is finite, right?
So there are only finitely many state and character pairs that can happen.
As the machine runs, just monitor it for a repeat of some pair.
A repeat means that the machine is looping, and so it won't halt.
No repeat, no loop.''
What are they missing?
\begin{answer}
First, to detect a loop we must look for a repeated configuration, 
not just a repeated state-character pair.
Machines can run forever without repeating a configuration,
for instance by just writing more characters to the tape,
as in code that keeps incrementing a variable.

Further, the \HP{} is not to detect an infinite loop.
Rather, the \HP{} is to detect whether the machine will halt.
Here also, code that just keeps incrementing a variable will not loop,
but also not halt.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  CS SE user Kyle Strand \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/11645/50343}.
\end{credit}
(This is related to the prior exercise.)
Would it be possible for a computer to detect infinite loops and 
subsequently stop the associated process, or would implementing such logic 
be solving the \HP?
Specifically, 
could the runtime environment do this:~after each instruction is executed,
it makes a snapshot of all of the relevant memory, the stack and heap data, 
the registers, the instruction pointer, etc., and
before executing a instruction it checks its snapshot against
all prior ones, and if there is a repeat then it declares that the program is
in an infinite loop?
% (Thus, for instance, a program generating an indefinitely long decimal 
% expansion of pi isn't in an infinite loop because at every iteration, 
% it has more digits of pi in its memory.
\begin{answer}
A machine could do that. 

However, as to the unsolvability of the \HP,
a machine can fail to halt for reasons other than that it is in
an infinite loop.
The code below would not be flagged 
as being in an infinite loop
by the runtime environment described, but 
this routine does not halt. 
\protect\begin{lstlisting}
i=0
while True:
    i=i+1
\end{lstlisting}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user npostavs, \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/44875/50343}
\end{credit}
This is the \definend{hailstone function},\index{hailstone function}
which inputs natural numbers.
\begin{equation*}
  h(n)=
  \begin{cases}
    1      &\case{if $n=0$ or $n=1$}  \\
    h(n/2)  &\case{if $n$ is even}     \\
    h(3n+1) &\case{else}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The \definend{Collatz conjecture}\index{Collatz conjecture} 
is that $h(n)=1$ for all $n\in\N$, that is, $h(n)$ halts in that it does not
keep expanding forever.
No one knows whether the Collatz conjecture is true.
Is it an unsolvable problem to determine whether $h$ halts on all input?
\begin{answer}
No, it is not an unsolvable problem.  
Either $f$ does halt on all inputs or it doesn't.
There are two possibilities, both of which are computable. 
(It is perhaps a unsettling that we don't know which of the
two is true but nonetheless both are computable.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
\begin{exparts}
\item
The problem of determining, given~$e$, whether $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$ is
unsolvable because no function
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$
exists.
\item
The existence of unsolvable problems indicates weaknesses in the
models of computation, and we need stronger models.
% \item
% Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem is unsolvable.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
False.
The function exists.
We say that the problem is unsolvable because that function is not
mechanically computable.
\item
False.
Church's Thesis asserts that Turing machines are a maximally-powerful
model of computation.
The existence of unsolvable problems is inherent in effective computation.
% \item
% False.
% Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem of a certain kind
% (about index sets) is unsolvable.
% There are plenty of solvable problems.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is computable if its characteristic function
is a computable function.
Consider the set consisting of~$1$ if Mallory reached the summit of Everest
in 1924,
and otherwise consisting of~$0$. 
Is that set computable?
\begin{answer}
Any finite set is computable, so yes it is computable.
We should be cautious about coflating `computable' with knowable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Describe the family of computable functions
that you get by using the \smn{} Theorem 
to parametrize~$x$ in each function.
Also give flowcharts sketching the associated machines
for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item $f(x,y)=3x+y$
\item $f(x,y)=xy^2$
\item 
  $f(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    x  &\case{if $x$ is odd} \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The function $f(x,y)=3x+y$ is intuitively computable, as sketched
  on the left of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine with
  that behavior; let that machine have index~$e$, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1008.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1009.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1010.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1011.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}
  \end{figure}
  Applying the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$ gives this computable family of
  one-input computable functions:
  $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=3x+y$.
  Flowcharts sketching some associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}.  
\item The function $f(x,y)=xy^2$ is intuitively computable, as in
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine~$\TM_e$ with
  that behavior, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1012.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1013.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1014.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1015.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}
  \end{figure}
  Applying the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$ gives
  $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=xy^2$, which is a family of one-input functions.
  Flowcharts sketching some associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}.  
\item The function $f$ is intuitively computable, as sketched on the left of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine~$\TM_e$ with
  that behavior, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1016.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1017.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1018.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1019.pdf}}
    \caption{A sketch of the machine for the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and of the machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ when $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$.
  The resulting family of one-input functions is this.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
      \begin{cases}
         x  &\case{if $x$ is odd}  \\
         0  &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Flowcharts sketching associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}.  
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is a solvable problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given an index~$e$, determine whether Turing machine~$\TM_e$
runs for at least $42$ steps on input~$3$.
\item Given an index~$e$, determine whether $\TM_e$
runs for at least $42$ steps on input~$e$.
\item Given~$n$, decide whether~$\TM_e$
runs for at least $e$ steps on input~$e$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
The answer to all three is the same.
Running a Turing machine for a fixed number of steps 
is not a problem
(and in the third item,
the number of steps is fixed for a given input).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\item[\parbox{\textwidth}{\it Each exercise from \ref{exer:FirstReduceExercise} through \ref{exer:FinalReduceExercise} states a problem.  Show that the problem is unsolvable by reducing the \HP{} to it.}]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:FirstReduceExercise}
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given
an index~$e$, 
determine if $\TMfcn_e$ is total, that is,
if it converges on every input.
\begin{answer}
  We will show that this function is not mechanically computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{total\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  The function
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}.
  Thus Church's Thesis says that there is a
  Turing machine that computes it;
  let that machine have index~$e_0$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x,y)$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1004.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1005.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}.
  Notice that 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{total\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.

  Since~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{total\_decider}$
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP.
  We cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
  compute  $\computablefunction{total\_decider}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given an index~$e$, decide if the Turing machine~$\TM_e$ squares its
  input.  
  That is, decide if $\TMfcn_e$ maps $y\mapsto y^2$.
\begin{answer}
  We will show that this function is not mechanically computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{square\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=y^2$ for all~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}

  Consider this function.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      y^2        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  The flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable} shows that
  $\psi$ is intuitively computable so by Church's Thesis there is a
  Turing machine that computes it.
  Let that machine have index~$e_0$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1000.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1001.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable}.
  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{square\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.

  So, since the function~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{square\_decider}$,
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP.
  But we can't mechanically solve the \HP{} so we cannot mechanically
  compute  $\computablefunction{square\_decider}$.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given~$e$, determine if the function~$\TMfcn_e$ returns the same value
  on two consecutive inputs, so that $\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$ 
  for some~$y\in\N$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We will show that this is not computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$ for some~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}

  This function
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}.
  So, by Church's Thesis there is a
  Turing machine that computes it;
  let that machine have index~$e$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1002.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1003.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}.
  Observe that these machines produce the function with constant
  output~$42$ if and only if the machine gets through the middle box.
  More precisely stated, 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.

  Since the function~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}$
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP,
  which we cannot do.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given~$e$, decide whether $\TMfcn_e$ fails to converge on input~$5$.
\begin{answer}
    We will show that this function is not computable;~no Turing machine
    has this input-output behavior.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(5)\diverges$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1006.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1007.pdf}}
      \caption{The machine associated with $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
    Because the function~$s$ is mechanically computable, this means that if 
    we could mechanically compute 
    $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given an index, determine if the computable function with 
  that index fails to converge on all odd numbers.  
\begin{answer}\verified
    We want to show that this function is not computable.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{diverge\_on\_odds\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)\diverges$ for all odd $y\in\N$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The argument given in the prior item will do again here.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} 
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given~$e$, decide if the function~$\TMfcn_e$
computed by machine~$\TM_e$ has the action
$x\mapsto x+1$.
\begin{answer}
    We will show that no Turing machine
    has this input-output behavior.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{successor\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(x)=x+1$ for all $x\in\N$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        y+1        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e_0$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x,y)$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1020.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1021.pdf}}
      \caption{Sketches of the machine associated with $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
    The function~$s$ is mechanically computable,
    so if 
    we could mechanically compute $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we can't do that, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FinalReduceExercise}
\textit{See the instructions above.}
Given~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
fails to converge on both inputs $x$ and~$2x$, for some~$x$.
\begin{answer}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e_0$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1022.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1023.pdf}}
      \caption{Outline of the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
    So if 
    we could mechanically compute 
    $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each problem, fill in the blanks to show that it is unsolvable.
\begin{quotation}\it
\noindent
We will show that this is not mechanically computable.
\begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}\_decider}(e)=
   \begin{cases}
     1  &\case{if \fillinblankLabelled{(2)}}   \\
     0  &\case{otherwise}
   \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
For that, consider this function.
\begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
   \begin{cases}
     \text{\fillinblankLabelled{(3)}}  
       &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}   \\
     0  &\case{otherwise}
   \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The flowchart on the left shows that $\psi$ is 
intuitively mechanically computable.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1050.pdf}
  \qquad\qquad
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1051.pdf}
\end{center}
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
Let that machine have index~$e_0$, so that 
$\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x,y)$.
Apply the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$.
A member of the resulting family of Turing machines is sketched above on the 
right.
Observe that
$
  \TMfcn_x(x)\converges\;
  \Longleftrightarrow\;
  \computablefunction{\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}\_decider}(s(e_0,x))=1
$.
Because the function $s$ is mechanically computable, if 
$\computablefunction{\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}\_decider}$ were 
to be mechanically
computable then the right side would be mechanically computable.
But the left side is not mechanically computable,
by the unsolvability of the \HP.
Therefore $\computablefunction{\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}\_decider}$ is 
not mechanically computable.
\end{quotation}
\begin{exparts}
\item Given machine index $e$,
  decide if there is an input $y$ so that  $\TM_e$ outputs $y$. 
\item Given $e$,
  decide if there is a $y$ so that $\TMfcn_e(y)=42$. 
\item Given $e$,
  decide if there is a $y$ so that $\TMfcn_e(y)=y+2$. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
(1)~$\computablefunction{same\_output\_as\_input}$,
(2)~there exists $y\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_e(y)=y$,
(3)~\texttt{Print $y$}.
\item
(1)~$\computablefunction{outputs\_42}$,
(2)~there exists $y\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_e(y)=42$,
(3)~\texttt{Print $42$}.
\item
(1)~$\computablefunction{ever\_adds\_2}$,
(2)~there exists $y\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_e(y)=y+2$,
(3)~\texttt{Print $y+2$}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix integers $a,b,c\in\Z$.
Consider the problem of determining, given $\cantor(x,y)$, whether $ax+by=c$.
Is that problem solvable or unsolvable?
\begin{answer}
  This problem is solvable.
  We can write a program that inputs $\cantor(x,y)$, applies the 
  unpairing function to get $x$ and~$y$, and then 
  plugs them into $ax+by$, to see if the result equals~$c$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each problem, state whether it is solvable, unsolvable,
or you cannot tell.
You needn't give a proof, just decide.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Given $e$, decide if $\TM_{e}$ halts on all even numbers~$y$.
\item Given $e$, decide if $\TM_{e}$ halts on three or fewer inputs~$y$.
\item Given $e$, decide if $\TM_4$ halts on input~$e$.
\item Given $e$, decide if $\TM_e$ contains an instruction with state $q_e$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Unsolvable.
\item Unsolvable.
\item Cannot tell; whether this is solvable or not 
  depends on the specifics of the
  way Turing machines are counted, that is, 
  it depends on the details of $\TM_4$.
\item Solvable.
  The set of five-tuples $\TM_e$ is finite so we can just search.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In some ways a more natural set than 
$K=\set{x\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_x(x)\converges}$
is $K_0=\set{\sequence{e,x}\in\N^2\suchthat \TMfcn_e(x)\converges}$.\index{K0, set of halting pairs@$K_0$, set of halting pairs}
Use the fact that $K$ is not computable to prove that $K_0$ is not computable.
\begin{answer}
Suppose that we could compute $K_0$.
That is, suppose that there is a Turing machine~$\TM_{e_0}$ such that 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(\sequence{e,x})=1$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ and 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(\sequence{e,x})=0$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\diverges$.
Then we could solve the \HP{} by just applying~$\TM_{e_0}$ to the 
input~$\sequence{x,x}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The \HP{} of determining membership in the set 
$K=\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_x(x)\converges}$ cuts across all 
Turing machines.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Produce a single Turing machine,~$\TM_e$, such that
the question of determining membership in 
$\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_{e}(y)\converges}$ is undecidable.
\item
Fix a number~$y$.
Show that the question of whether $\TM$ halts on~$y$ is decidable.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
One such machine takes input~$x$, uses a Universal Turing machine to 
run $\TM_x$ on input~$x$,
and then exits.
\figureref{fig:SingleMachineHP} has a flowchart.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1027.pdf}}
    \caption{A single machine that reflects the \HP.}
    \label{fig:SingleMachineHP}
  \end{figure}
\item
For a fixed input~$y$, one of these two machines works:~(1) for all input,
print~$1$, or (2)~for all inputs, print~$0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, if it is mechanically solvable
then sketch a algorithm to solve it.
If it is unsolvable then show that.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given $e$, determine the number of states in $\TM_e$.
\item Given $e$, determine whether $\TM_e$ halts when the input is the
  empty string.
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TM_e$ halts on input~$n$ within one
  hundred steps. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is solvable.
  Given the index~$e$, start by translating it to the set of 
  Turing machine instructions, $\TM_e$
  (remember that we are using numberings that are acceptable in 
  the sense defined on page~\pageref{def:AcceptableNumbering}).
  Now count the number of states that appear in that Turing machine.
  \item This is not solvable; $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$
  is not mechanically computable.
  For, consider this function. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      42          &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$} \\
      \uparrow    &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*} 
  It is intuitively mechanically computable, and so by Church's Thesis 
  there is a Turing machine that computes it.
  Let the index of that machine be~$e_0$.
  Apply the \smn~theorem to parametrize~$x$,
  giving a uniformly computable family of functions~$\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.
  (The $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$'s are functions of no input; that's fine as the
  associated Turing machine simply does not read any input from the tape.)

  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}(s(e_0,x))=1$.
  If $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ were mechanically computable
  then we could mechanically solve the \HP,
  so $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ is not mechanically computable.
  \item This is solvable.
  From the index~$e$, use a Universal Turing machine to run~$\TM_e$
  on input~$e$ for one hundred steps. 
  By the end either it has halted or it hasn't.     
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is $K$ infinite?
\begin{answer}
Yes. 
If $K$ were finite then it would be computable,
since any finite set is computable.
For example, we could compute membership in a finite set with a sequence
of if-then-else statements.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false: the number of unsolvable problems is countably infinite.
\begin{answer}
False.
It is indeed infinite, but it is uncountably infinite.
There are uncountably infinitely many problems, that is, functions
from $\N$ to $\N$, but only countably many Turing machines.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user Raphael \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/44901/50343}
\end{credit}
Show that for any Turing machine, the problem of determining whether 
it halts on all inputs is solvable.
\begin{answer}
Either $f$ does halt on all inputs, or it doesn't.
So there are two possibilities, both of which are computable
(either the answer is `yes' or the answer is `no'), 
although we may possibly not know which one is true.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\definend{Goldbach's conjecture},\index{Goldbach's conjecture} 
is that every even natural number 
greater than two is the sum of two primes.
It is one of the oldest and best-known unsolved problems in mathematics.
Show that if we could solve the \HP{} then we could in principle settle 
Goldbach's conjecture.
\begin{answer}
Consider a Turing machine that, for any input,
just searches for an even number greater than two that is not
the sum of two primes.
If, on any input, that Turing machine halts then the conjecture is
false.
Otherwise it is true.
So if we determined whether that Turing machine halts then we would
have settled the question.
See \figureref{fig:GoldbachsConjectures}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth]{background/pix/goldbach_conjectures.png}}
    \caption{Goldbach's conjectures.  (Courtesy \protect\href{https://xkcd.com/1310/}{\texttt{xkcd.com}})}
    \label{fig:GoldbachsConjectures}
  \end{figure}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\definend{Brocard's problem}\index{Brocard's problem} 
asks whether there are any numbers for which $n!+1$
is a perfect square besides $4$, $5$, and~$7$
(no other solutions exist up to a quadrillion).
Show that if we could solve the \HP{} then we could in principle settle
this problem.
\begin{answer}
We can write a program that takes input~$x$,
ignores it, and searches for a solution larger than~$7$, 
and halts if it finds one.
With a solution to the \HP{} we could check whether that
program halts.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
If we could solve the \HP{} then could we solve all problems?
\begin{answer}
No.
The set of Turing machines is countable.
So the set of solvable problems is countable.
Adding one more, by adding the \HP, would still mean
that we have uncountably many problems but only countably many
are solvable.
So there would still be problems that are not solvable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that most problems are unsolvable by showing
that there are uncountably many functions $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$
that are not computed by any Turing machine, while the number of
function that are computable is countable.
\begin{answer}
  The set of functions $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ is the disjoint union of two
  subsets:~the functions that are computable and the functions that are not.
  We know that the set of computable functions is countable.
  Because the union of two countable sets is countable, if the set
  of non-computable functions were also countable then there would be 
  countably many functions overall, which there are not.
  Thus the set of non-computable functions is not countable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an example of a computable function that is total,
meaning that it converges on all inputs, 
but whose range is not computable.
\begin{answer}
  Fix an element $k\in K$.
  The range of this function is~$K$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x,n)=
    \begin{cases}
    x  &\case{if $\TM_x$ with input~$x$ halts in $n$ steps}  \\
    k  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Clearly the function is computable and converges on all inputs.
  For all elements of $K$ there is an $n$ where $x$ appears in the range.
  And, for the same reason, only the elements of~$K$ appear in the range.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set of bit strings is a 
\definend{decidable language}\index{decidable!language}\index{language!decidable} 
if its
characteristic function is computable.
Prove each.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The union of two decidable languages is a decidable language.
\item
The intersection of two decidable languages is a decidable language
\item
The complement of a decidable language is a decidable language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  Start with decidable languages~$\lang_0$ and~$\lang_1$ and 
  consider the union $\lang=\lang_0\cup\lang_1$.
  Because they are decidable, there are Turing machines $\TM_{e_0}$
  and $\TM_{e_1}$ that decide membership.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{e_0}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in\lang_0$}  \\
      0  &\case{if $x\notin\lang_0$}  
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in\lang_1$}  \\
      0  &\case{if $x\notin\lang_1$}  
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Note that because they decide membership, these machines halt on all inputs.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1024.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1025.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches of machines to decide union and intersection of decidable languages.}
    \label{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}
  \end{figure}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  A sketch of the machine that decides membership in $\lang_0\cup\lang_1$ 
  is in \figureref{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}.
  A number is an element of the union if it is a member of either set.  
  So, given~$x$, this machine first runs $\TM_{e_0}$ on~$x$.
  If that returns~$1$ then $x$ is a member of the union, so the new
  machine outputs~$1$.
  Otherwise the new machine runs~$\TM_{e_1}$ on~$x$ and returns the result.
  \item
  A sketch of the machine that decides membership in $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$ 
  is in \figureref{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}.
  A number is an element of the union if it is a member of both sets.  
  So, given~$x$, this machine first runs $\TM_{e_0}$ on~$x$.
  If that returns~$0$ then $x$ is not a member of the intersection, so the new
  machine outputs~$0$.
  Otherwise the new machine runs~$\TM_{e_1}$ on~$x$ and returns the result.
  \item 
  Complement is straightforward.
  For the complement of~$\lang_0$ the new machine just runs $\TM_{e_0}$
  and subtracts the result from~$1$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{Halting problem|)}



%================================================
\section{Rice's Theorem}\index{Rice's theorem|(}\label{sec:RicesThm}
Our finishing point in the prior section was that the results and examples there
give the intuition that we cannot 
mechanically analyze the behavior of Turing machines.
In this section we will make this intuition precise.

Mechanical analysis does apply to some properties of Turing machines.
We can write a routine that, given~$e$, 
determines whether or not $\TM_e$ has a four-tuple instruction whose first 
entry is the state~$q_5$.
The analogue  in ordinary programming
is that we can write a program to parse source code for
a variable named \str{x1}. 
But these are not what we mean by ``behavior.'' 
Instead, they are properties of the implementation.

A property is 
\definend{semantic}\index{semantic property}\index{property!semantic}
if it has to do with the meaning of a thing; in our case,
with what the machine does.\footnote{%
  Often writers contrast these with properties that are 
  \definend{syntactic},\protect\index{syntactic property}\protect\index{property!syntactic}
  which have to do with how a formal object is constructed.
  We covered the syntax of Turing machines in the book's first section.}
Briefly, a property is semantic if it is about $\TMfcn$ more than~$\TM\!$.
For example,
consider a Turing machine~$\TM$ that inputs a natural number and outputs~$1$ 
if the number is prime and~$0$ otherwise.
This machine's behavior is that it
acts as the characteristic function of the set of primes.
In contrast, suppose that it has a state~$q_5$ but no state~$q_6$.
If we change all of the $q_5$'s in its transition table to $q_6$'s 
then we get a machine that is different but with the same behavior.

%<*def:SameBehavior>
\begin{definition}
Two computable functions
\definend{have the same behavior, $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$},\index{same behavior, functions with}\index{functions!same behavior}
if they converge on the same inputs~$x\in\N$
and when they do converge, they have the same outputs.\footnotemark[2] 
\end{definition}\footnotetext[2]{%
  Strictly speaking, we don't need the symbol~$\samebehavior$. 
  By definition, a function is a set of ordered pairs. 
  If 
  $\TMfcn_e(0)\converges$ while~$\TMfcn_e(1)\diverges$ then the set $\TMfcn_e$
  contains a pair with first entry~$0$ but no pair starting with~$1$.
  Thus for partial functions, if they converge on the same inputs
  and when they do converge they have the same outputs, then we can simply say 
  that the two are equal, $\TMfcn=\hat{\TMfcn}$.
  But we use $\samebehavior$ as a reminder that the
  functions may be partial.}
%</def:SameBehavior>

%<*def:IndexSet>
\begin{definition}
A set $\mathcal{I}$ of natural numbers is an 
\definend{index set}\footnotemark[3]\index{index set}\index{set!index} 
when for all~$e,\hat{e}\in\N$,
if $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ 
then also~$\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$. 
\end{definition}\footnotetext[3]{%
  It is called an index set because it is a set of indices.}
%</def:IndexSet>

\begin{example}
If we fix a behavior and consider the indices  of all of the Turing machines
with that behavior then we get an index set.
Thus the set 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$
is an index set. 
To verify, suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that 
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$. 
Then $\phi_{\hat{e}}$ also doubles its input: 
$\phi_{\hat{e}}(x)=2x$ for all~$x$.
Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
We can also get an index set by collecting multiple behaviors together.
The set
$\mathcal{J}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$, or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$}}
$
is an index set.
For, suppose that $e\in\mathcal{J}$ and that  
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$  where $\hat{e}\in\N$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{J}\!$, either 
$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$.
From $\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$ we know that
either $\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$, and  
consequently $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{J}\!$.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NotIndexSet}
The set $\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\TM_e$ contains an instruction starting with $q_{10}$}}$ 
is not an index set.
We can easily produce two Turing machines having the same behavior
where one machine contains such an instruction while the other does not.
\end{example}

%<*th:RicesTheorem>
\begin{theorem}[Rice's theorem]\index{Rice's theorem}   \label{thm:RicesThm}
Every index set that is not trivial, that is not empty and not 
all of $\N$, is not computable.
\end{theorem}
%</th:RicesTheorem>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremi>
Let $\mathcal{I}$ be an index set.
Choose an~$e\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_{e}(y)\diverges$ for all~$y$.
Then either $e\in\mathcal{I}$ or $e\notin\mathcal{I}$.
We shall show that in the second case $\mathcal{I}$ is not computable. 
The first case is similar and is \cref{exer:OtherHalfRicesThm}.

So assume $e\notin\mathcal{I}$.
Since $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty there is an index~$\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
Because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, $\TMfcn_{e}\not\simeq\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Thus there is an input~$y$ such that $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y)\converges$.

Consider the flowchart on the left below.
Note that $\hat{e}$ is not an input, it is the number from the prior paragraph.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior,
let it be~$\TM_{e_0}$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$,
resulting in the uniformly computable 
family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$ whose computation is 
outlined on the right.
%</pf:RicesTheoremi>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp12.pdf}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp13.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremii>
We've constructed the machine on the right so that if 
$\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e}$
and thus $s(e_0,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$. 
Further,
if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$
and thus $s(e_0,x)\in\mathcal{I}$.
It follows that if $\mathcal{I}$ were mechanically computable, so that
we could effectively check whether $s(e_0,x)\in\mathcal{I}$,
then we could solve the \HP.
%</pf:RicesTheoremii>
\end{proof}


\begin{example}
We will use Rice's Theorem to show that this problem is unsolvable:~given 
$e$, decide if $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$.   
We must define an appropriate set~$\mathcal{I}$ and then verify that 
it is not empty, that 
it is not all of~$\N$, and that it is an index set.

Let 
$
  \mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}
$.
The simplest way to verify that this set is not empty is to exhibit
a member. 
The routine sketched on the left below is intuitively computable and
so Church's Thesis says there is a Turing machine with that 
behavior.
That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$
and thus $\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1052.pdf}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1053.pdf}}
\end{center}
Likewise, to verify that $\mathcal{I}$ does not contain every number,
consider the routine on the right.
Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine with that 
behavior. 
That machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$
and so $\mathcal{I}\neq\N$.

We finish by verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set. 
Assume that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and 
let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, we have that  $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$.  
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, we have that
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(3)\converges$ also, and thus
$\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
Hence, $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.  
\end{example}

The above example is the same problem as in the first example
of the prior subsection.
Note that Rice's Theorem makes the answer considerably simpler.
(Of course, our development of the theorem requires the 
prior section's work.)

\begin{example}
We can use Rice's Theorem to show that the prior section's second 
problem is unsolvable:~given $e$, decide if $\phi_e(x)=7$ for some~$x$.    
Rice's Theorem asks us to produce an appropriate~$\mathcal{I}$ 
and verify that it is nontrivial and that it is an index set.

Let
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)=7$ for some $x$}}$.
This set is not empty because there is a Turing machine that acts as
the identity function, so that $\TMfcn(x)=x$,
and the index of that machine is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.
This set is not all of $\N$ because
there is a Turing Machine that never halts, $\TMfcn(x)\diverges$ for all~$x$, 
and that machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.
Hence~$\mathcal{I}$ is nontrivial.

To show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set,
assume that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
By the assumption, $\TMfcn_e(x_0)=7$ for some input~$x_0$.
Since the two have the same behavior, the same input gives
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x_0)=7$. 
Consequently, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This problem is also unsolvable:~given~$e$, 
decide whether $\TMfcn_e$ equals this.
\begin{equation*}
  f(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    4  &\case{if $x$ is prime}  \\
    x+1 &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Let $\mathcal{I}=\set{j\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_j=f}$.
The set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not empty because we can write a program with this 
behavior and so by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with this
behavior, and its index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.
Also, $\mathcal{I}\neq\N$ because there is a Turing machine that fails to halt
on any input and its index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

To finish we argue that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
So suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ we have that $\TMfcn_e(x)=f(x)$ for all 
inputs~$x$.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ we have that 
$\TMfcn_e(x)=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x)$ for all~$x$, and so $\hat{e}$ is also
a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.
Hence, $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\end{example}


We close by reflecting on the significance of Rice's Theorem.

% This result addresses the properties of computable functions.
% It does not speak to properties of machines unless those
% properties are about input-output behaviors.
% For example, the set of functions
% computed by C~programs whose first character
% is `k' is not an index set. 

At this chapter's start we saw that 
unsolvable problems exist.
However, the proof had the disadvantage of being a counting argument 
that left us without natural examples.
In the prior section, starting with the \HP{} we saw
unsolvable problems that are interesting in practice.

In this section the definition of index set 
gave us a way to formalize a `behavior'.
It brings us back to the declaration
that opened the first chapter
that we wish to concentrate more what the machines do than on
the implementation details.
This definition 
led to Rice's Theorem, which says that \emph{every} nontrivial index set
is unsolvable.
So we've gone from taking unsolvable problems as exotic, to taking them
as things that genuinely do come up, to 
a point where it may seem that there are too many of them.

Of course, that characterization of Rice's Theorem is an overstatement, 
an overly narrow
interpretation of the word interesting; we've all seen
and written real-world programs with behaviors
that we would at least informally characterize as interesting.
Nonetheless, Rice's Theorem is especially significant
for understanding what can be done with a computer.



% ...................................................
\begin{exercises}
% possible: https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/117066/50343

\begin{exercise}
Your friend is confused, ``According to Rice's Theorem, everything is 
impossible. 
Every property of a computer program is non-computable.
But I do this supposedly impossible stuff all the time!''
Help them out.
\begin{credit}
  Question by SE user MathematicalOrchid,
  \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/2811/67754}, 
  and answer by SE user Andrej Bauer.
\end{credit}
\begin{answer}
Briefly, we have to distinguish between a 
machine and the function that it computes. 
Many different machines compute the same function. 
Rice's theorem is about those properties of the machine that extend to be 
properties of the computed functions.
For instance, ``Does this code compute the squaring function?'' is 
unsolvable, but ``Does this code contain the letter K?'' is not. 

A more sophisticated answer is that
two machines `extensionally equal' if they compute the 
same function, so that for every input either both 
machines run forever on that input, 
or they both terminate and output the same value.
An `extensional property' of programs is a property that respects 
extensional equality, i.e., 
if two machines are extensionally equal then they either both have 
the property or both not have it.
Then Rice's Theorem says that a computable extensional property of machines 
either holds of all machines or of none.

Here are some non-extensional properties,
to which Rice's Theorem does not apply:
(i)~the machine halts within $100$ steps (we can always modify a machine 
to an extensionally equal one that runs longer),
(ii)~the machine uses fewer than~$n$ memory cells within the first~$m$ 
steps of execution (we can always modify a machine to an extensionally 
equal one so that it uses extra memory), and
(iii)~the machine source contains state~$q_{10}$ (we can add extra states).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ runs for at least $100$ steps on input~$5$}}$
an index set?
\begin{answer}
  No, it is not an index set.
  We can write two Turing machines with the same behavior, say, 
  on input~$x$ they return output~$x$.
  But one of them takes only a few steps to do this while
  the other takes $101$ steps. 
  The index of the second set is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$ but the
  index of the first set is not a member.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Why does Rice's theorem not show that this problem is unsolvable:~given~$e$,
decide whether 
$\emptyset\subseteq\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_e(x)\converges}$?
\begin{answer}
The set of indices~$e\in\N$ such that
$\emptyset\subseteq\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_e(x)\converges}$ is all of $\N$.
This problem is solvable, although trivially in the sense that
for any~$e$, the answer is `yes'.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false: the given property of machines is semantic.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The machine halts on input~$5$.
\item It has exactly four instructions.
\item It computes twice its input.
\item The number of steps that a machine takes is five times its input. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item True, this is about the input-output behavior of the machine.
\item False, we can make two machines with the same behavior but where one
  has four instructions and the other does not.
\item True.    
\item False, we can make two machines with the same behavior but where one
  has this behavior and the four other does not.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Give a trivial index set:~fill in the blanks
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}$\TM_e$\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}}}$
so that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ is empty.
\begin{answer}
  There is only one empty set, but there are of course many ways to describe
  it;
  one is $\mathcal{I}_0=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ solves the \HP}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a trivial index set:~fill in the blanks
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}$\TM_e$\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}}}$
so that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ is all of $\N$.
\begin{answer}
  There is only one set $\N$ but there are of course many ways to describe it;
  one is $\mathcal{I}_0=\set{e\suchthat \text{every member of $\TM_e$ is a four-tuple}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each problem, produce the index file needed to apply Rice's Theorem.
(You needn't give the entire argument, just produce the file.)
\begin{exparts}
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TMfcn_e(7)=7$ (and, of course, it converges).
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TMfcn_e(e)=e$.
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TMfcn_{2e}(y)=7$ for any $y\in\N$.
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TMfcn_e(7)$ converges and is a prime number.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(7)\converges$ and  equals $7$}}$
\item $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$ and  equals $e$}}$
\item $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is a $y$ where $\TMfcn_{2e}(y)\converges$ and equals $7$}}$
\item $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(7)\converges$ and is prime}}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\item[\parbox{\textwidth}{\it For each of the problems from \cref{exer:FirstRiceExercise} to \cref{exer:FinalRiceExercise}, show that it is unsolvable by applying Rice's theorem.  (These repeat the problems from \cref{exer:FirstReduceExercise} to \cref{exer:FinalReduceExercise}.)}]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:FirstRiceExercise}
Given
an index~$e$, 
determine if $\TMfcn_e$ is total, that is,
if it converges on every input.
\begin{answer}
Let $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$\TMfcn_e$ is total}}$.
We will show that it is a nontrivial index set.

We first show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is not the empty set.
Consider the program sketched on the left of 
\figureref{fig:INotTrivialTotalFcnsNotComputable}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1028.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1029.pdf}}  
    \caption{The machines used to show that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not trivial.}
    \label{fig:INotTrivialTotalFcnsNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
Clearly this sketch outlines a computation.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine fitting this sketch.
It has an index and that index is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
So $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty.

Next we show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$.
Consider the program sketched on the right of 
\figureref{fig:INotTrivialTotalFcnsNotComputable}.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine fitting this sketch.
Its index is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$ and so 
$\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$.

To finish, we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
So suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, 
we know that $\TMfcn_e(a)\converges$ for all $a\in\N$.
Because $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ we therefore know that
$\TMfcn_e(a)\converges$ for all $a\in\N$.
Thus, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ and
consequently $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.

By the prior three paragraphs, Rice's theorem shows that
the problem of determining totality, the problem of computing
whether, given~$e$, the Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts on all inputs,
is unsolvable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
Given an index~$e$, decide if the Turing machine~$\TM_e$ squares its
  input.  
  That is, decide if $\TMfcn_e$ maps $y\mapsto y^2$.
\begin{answer}
Let $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$\TMfcn_e(y)=y^2$ for all $y$}}$.
We will show that it is a nontrivial index set.

We first show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is not the empty set.
Consider the program sketched on the left of 
\figureref{fig:INotTrivialSquarerFcnsNotComputable}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1030.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1031.pdf}}  
    \caption{The machines used to show that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ of indices of the squarer function is not trivial.}
    \label{fig:INotTrivialSquarerFcnsNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
Clearly this sketch outlines a computable routine.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine matching this sketch.
It has an index, and that index is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
So $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty.

Next we show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$.
Consider the program sketched on the right of 
\figureref{fig:INotTrivialSquarerFcnsNotComputable}.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine fitting this sketch.
Its index is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$ and so 
$\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$.

To finish we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
So suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ 
we know that $\TMfcn_e(y)=y^2$ for all $y\in\N$.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ we therefore know that
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y)=y^2$ for all $y\in\N$.
Thus, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ and
consequently $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.

By the prior three paragraphs, Rice's theorem shows that
the problem of determining totality, the problem of computing
whether, given~$e$, the Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts on all inputs,
is unsolvable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Given~$e$, determine if the function~$\TMfcn_e$ returns the same value
  on two consecutive inputs, so that $\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$ 
  for some~$y\in\N$.
\begin{answer}
We will show that 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$ for some $y\in\N$}}$
is a nontrivial index set.

We first show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is not empty.
Consider the program sketched on the left of 
\figureref{fig:IRepeatedValueFcnNotTrivial}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1028.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1029.pdf}}  
    \caption{The machines used to show that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not trivial.}
    \label{fig:IRepeatedValueFcnNotTrivial}
  \end{figure}
It is intuitively computable so 
by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine fitting this sketch.
That Turing machine's index is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
So $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty.

Next we argue that~$\mathcal{I}\neq\N$.
Consider the program outlined on the right of 
\figureref{fig:IRepeatedValueFcnNotTrivial}.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine fitting this.
Its index is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$, and so 
$\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$.

To finish, we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, there exists $y\in\N$ so that
$\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, we know that
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y)=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y+1)$ for the same~$y$.
Thus, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
Therefore $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.

Consequently, by Rice's theorem,
the problem of determining membership in~$\mathcal{I}$
is unsolvable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}

To finish, we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FinalRiceExercise}
Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
fails to converge on both inputs $x$ and~$2x$, for some~$x$.
\begin{answer}
We will show that 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{both $\TMfcn_e(x)\diverges$ and $\TMfcn_e(2x)\diverges$ for some~$x$}}$
is a nontrivial index set.

First we show that~$\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset$.
Consider the program sketched on the left of 
\figureref{fig:IDivergesOnXAndTwoXNotTrivial}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1034.pdf}}
    \qquad

    \label{fig:IDivergesOnXAndTwoXNotTrivial}
  \end{figure}
It is intuitively computable, so 
by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine fitting this sketch.
That machine's index is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

Next we argue that~$\mathcal{I}\neq\N$.
Consider the program outlined on the right of 
\figureref{fig:IDivergesOnXAndTwoXNotTrivial}.
By Church's Thesis, there is a Turing machine fitting this.
Its index is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

To finish, we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, we know that for some input~$x$ we have
both $\TMfcn_e(x)\diverges$ and  $\TMfcn_e(2x)\diverges$.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ we know that
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x)\diverges$ and $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(2x)\diverges$, 
for the same~$x$.
Thus, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$. 
Therefore $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.

Consequently, by Rice's theorem,
the problem of determining membership in~$\mathcal{I}$
is unsolvable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by applying
Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of determining if a function is total, that is,
if it converges on every input.
\item The problem of determining if a function is partial, that is,
if it fails to converge on some input.
% \item The problem of deciding if the function ever converges, on any input.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item We first state the problem precisely:~given an index~$e$, 
  decide whether~$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y\in\N$.
  To show that this problem is unsolvable we will verify that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.

  It is not empty because we can write a program that
  returns its input.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  It is not equal to~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that does not halt on any input.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior
  and the machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  We finish verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ we know that $\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for
  all $y\in\N$.
  Because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y)\converges$ for all~$y$ also. 
  Therefore $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$
  and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\item This set is the complement of the one in the prior item.
  \Cref{exer:IndexSetsClosedSetOps} shows that the complement of an
  index set is also an index set.
  But we don't have that result in the main section body so we will 
  verify that the given problem is unsolvable without reference to it.

  The problem is:~given an index~$e$, 
  decide whether~$\TMfcn_e(y)\diverges$ for some $y\in\N$.
  To show that this is unsolvable we will verify that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\diverges$ for some $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.

  The set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not empty because we can write a program that
  that does not halt on any input and then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  The set is not equal to~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that returns its input and then
  by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  We finish verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ we know that $\TMfcn_e(y)\diverges$ for
  at least one $y\in\N$.
  Because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(y)\diverges$ for at least one~$y$ also. 
  Therefore $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$
  and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each problem, fill in the blanks to prove that it is 
unsolvable.

$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}}}$

To finish, we show that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, \fillinblankLabelled{(4)}.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$,
\fillinblankLabelled{(5)}.
Thus, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.

\end{quotation}
\begin{exparts}
\item Given~$e$, determine if Turing machine~$e$ halts on

\item Given~$e$, decide if Turing machine~$e$ ever outputs a seven.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
Each answer just gives the numbered fill-ins.
\begin{exparts}
\item
(1)~$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ for all inputs~$x$ that are is a multiple of~$5$.

\begin{exercise}
Define that a Turing machine 
\definend{accepts}\index{Turing machine!accept a language}\index{language!accepted by Turing machine} 
a set of bit strings $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$
if that machine inputs bit strings, and it halts on all inputs, and 
it outputs~$1$ if and only if the input is a member of~$\lang$.
Show that each problem is unsolvable, using Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The problem of deciding, given $e\in\N$, whether $\TM_e$ accepts an
infinite language. 
\item
The problem of deciding, given $e\in\N$, whether $\TM_e$ accepts the string
\str{101}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  For both items we will use Rice's Theorem.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  We will show that this is a nontrivial index set:
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ accepts an infinite language}}$.

  First, $\mathcal{I}$ is nonempty because the set 
  $\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$ is infinite and
  we can easily write a Turing machine to output~$1$ on all inputs, which
  accepts this subset of $\B$.
  The index of that Turing machine is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ does not equal~$\N$ 
  because we can easily write a Turing machine to output~$0$ on all inputs, which
  accepts the empty subset of~$\B$, which is not infinite.
  The index of that Turing machine is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Finally, to verify that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, suppose that
  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that 
  $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ there is an infinite $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$
  so that $\TM_x$ accepts~$\lang$. 
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ the set of bit strings
  for which $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ outputs~$1$ equals the set for which 
  $\TM_x$ outputs~$1$, and so $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ also accepts an infinite
  set of bit strings.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$, and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \item 
  We will show that 
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ accepts \str{101}}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.
  First, $\mathcal{I}$ is nonempty because
  we can write a Turing machine to output~$1$ on all inputs, which
  accepts the string~\str{101}.
  The index of that Turing machine is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ does not equal~$\N$ 
  because we can write a Turing machine to output~$0$ on all inputs, which
  does not accept the string~\str{101}.
  The index of that Turing machine is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Finally, to verify that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, suppose that
  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that 
  $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the machine $\TM_e$ accepts~\str{101}. 
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the 
  machine $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ also accepts~\str{101}.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that this problem is mechanically unsolvable: give~$e$, determine whether
there is an input~$x$ so that~$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$.
\begin{answer}
  We will use Rice's Theorem so consider
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat  \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges $ for some $x\in\N$}}$.
  This set is not empty because we can write a Turing machine that halts on all
  inputs.
  This set is not equal to~$\N$ because we can write a Turing machine that
  fails to halt for any input.

  To show this is an index set suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that
  the number~$\hat{e}$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ there is a number~$x_0$ such that 
  $\TMfcn_{e}(x_0)\converges$.
  Then $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x_0)\converges$ also, 
  because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ and this is an index set.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We say that a Turing machine has an 
\definend{unreachable state}\index{unreachable state}\index{state!unreachable}
if for all inputs, during the course of the computation the machine never
enters that state.
Show that 
$\mathcal{J}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ has an unreachable state}}$
is not an index set.
\begin{answer}
We can produce two Turing machines, $\TM_e$ and~$\TM_{\hat{e}}$ 
that have the same behavior, $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$,
but $e\in\mathcal{J}$ and $\hat{e}\notin\mathcal{J}$.
Here are two such machines.  
\begin{align*}
  \TM_e &=
  \set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0},\tminstruction{1}{B}{B}{0}}        \\
  \TM_{\hat{e}} &=
  \set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}
\end{align*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} 
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/86660}{SE user Rajesh R} 
  % https://cs.stackexchange.com/users/78255/rajesh-r), Whether TM accepts epsilon?, URL (version: 2018-01-13): 
\end{credit}
Your classmate says, ``Here is a problem that is about the behavior of
machines but is also solvable:~given~$e$, determine whether $\TM_e$ only halts
on an empty input tape.
To solve this problem, give machine~$\TM_e$ an empty input and
see whether it goes on.''
Where are they mistaken?
\begin{answer}
  Briefly, we cannot tell anything by whether the machine `goes on'.
  It can go on to do quite complex things.

  For more, we can reduce this problem to the \HP.
  Consider this $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42  &\case{if machine $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$ and $y=\emptystring$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  (We take this machine to have tape alphabet 
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$.)
  This is intuitively computable so Church's Thesis gives that there is 
  a Turing machine with this behavior, and we can assume it has index~$e_0$.

  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$,
  giving a family of machines and associated computable 
  functions as here.
  \begin{equation*}
     \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
     \begin{cases}
       42 &\case{if machine $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$ and $y=\emptystring$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
     \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ if and only if the function
  $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$ halts only on $y=\emptystring$.
  So if we could check the latter mechanically then we could mechanically 
  solve the \HP, which of course we cannot.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} 
% As described in \cref{ex:NotIndexSet}, 
% produce two Turing machines 
% giving an output of $0$ for all inputs, and such that
% and one machine contains an instruction starting with
% $q_{10}$ while the other does not.    
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that no set that is nonempty and finite is an index set.
\begin{answer}
The Padding Lemma, \cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}, 
says that every computable function has infinitely many indices.
So if the set contains $e$ then it must contain the infinitely many
