indices~$\hat{e}$ such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an index set.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{machine $\TM_e$ halts on at least five inputs}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the function $\TMfcn_e$ is one-to-one}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the function $\TMfcn_e$ is either total or else $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
  We will call each set~$\mathcal{I}$.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose that $\hat{e}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the machine $\TM_e$ halts on at least five inputs,
  that is, the function $\TMfcn_e$ converges on at least five inputs.
  Because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ also halts on at least five inputs, namely, the 
  same five.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose also 
  that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the function $\TMfcn_e$ 
  is one-to-one.
  Because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ is also one-to-one.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and also 
  that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the function $\TMfcn_e$ 
  is either total or else $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$.
  In the first case, that $\TMfcn_e$ is total,
  because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ is also total.
  In the case that $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$,
  because they have the same behavior, $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(3)\diverges$ also.
  In either case, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Index sets can seem abstract.
Here is an alternate characterization.
The Padding Lemma on 
page~\pageref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}
says that every computable function has infinitely many indices.
Thus, there are infinitely many indices for the 
doubling function $f(x)=2x$,
infinitely many for the function that diverges on all inputs, etc.
In the rectangle below imagine the set of all integers and
group them together when they are indices of equal computable functions.
The picture below shows such a partition. 
Select a few parts, such as the ones shown shaded.
Take their union.
That's an index set. 
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/indexsets/indexsets.pdf}}     
\end{center}
More formally stated,
consider the relation $\simeq$ between natural numbers 
given by $e\mathbin{\simeq}\hat{e}$ 
if $\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
\begin{exparts*} 
\item
Show that this is an equivalence relation.
\item 
Describe the parts, the equivalence classes.
\item
Show that each index set is the union of some of the equivalence classes.
\hint~show that if an index set contains one element of a class then it contains
them all.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  The relation~$\simeq$ is reflexive because a Turing machine $\TM_e$ 
  has the same behavior as itself:~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_e$.
  It is symmetric because if $\TM_e$ has the same 
  behavior as $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ then the converse also holds.
  Transitivity is just as clear.
  \item
  Each equivalence class is a set of integers~$e\in\N$.
  Two integers are together in a class, $e,\hat{e}\in\mathcal{C}$ if 
  the input-output behaviors of the associated Turing machines
  $\TM_e$ and $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ are the
  same,~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
  (As an example, one class contains the indices of all Turing machines 
  that double the input, 
  $\mathcal{C}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$.)
  \item
  We follow the hint.
  Suppose that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set 
  and that $e\in\mathcal{C}$ is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
  If $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{C}$ then $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}\samebehavior\TMfcn_e$
  because $\mathcal{C}$ is an equivalence class for the relation~$\simeq$.
  But~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set so $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:IndexSetsClosedSetOps}
Because being an index set is a property of a set, we naturally 
consider how it interacts with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Show that the complement of an index set is also an index set.
\item
Show that the collection of index sets is closed under union.
\item
Is it closed under intersection?
If so prove that and if not then give a counterexample.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Let $\mathcal{I}$ be an index set and consider the 
  complement,~$\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.
  Suppose that $e\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$ and suppose also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.

  By the definition of set complement, either $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$
  or $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.
  If $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ then because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$
  and because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, we have that $e\in\mathcal{I}$
  also.
  This contradicts that $e\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$ and
  therefore $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$. 
  \item 
  Fix a collection $\mathcal{I}_j$ of index sets and consider
  the union $\mathcal{I}=\cup_j\mathcal{I}_j$.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.

  The number~$e$ is an element of the union $\cup_j\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{I}$
  so it is an element of some $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$.
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ and because
  $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$ is an index set, the number~$\hat{e}$ is an element
  of $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$, and is therefore an element of
  the union~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \item 
  Let $\mathcal{I}_j$ be a collection of index sets and let
  $\mathcal{I}=\cap_j\mathcal{I}_j$.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$, and also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$

  Since~$e$ is an element of the intersection 
  $\cap_j\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{I}$, it is an element of each set
  $\mathcal{I}_{j}$.
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ and because
  $\mathcal{I}_{j}$ is an index set, the number~$\hat{e}$ is an element
  of $\mathcal{I}_{j}$ also.
  As $\hat{e}$ is an element of each $\mathcal{I}_{j}$, 
  it is an element of the intersection~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:OtherHalfRicesThm}
Do the $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$ case in the proof of Rice's Theorem, 
\cref{thm:RicesThm}.
\begin{answer}
  The differences between this case and the one in the section are very small.
  The proof there starts by considering an index set~$\mathcal{I}$.
  It fixes an index $e_0\in\N$ so that 
  $\TMfcn_{e_0}(y)\diverges$ for all inputs~$y\in\N$.
  It then does the $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$ case.
  So what's left is to do $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
  Here's a difference:~because $\mathcal{I}$ is nontrivial,
  it does not equal~$\N$ so there is some~$e_1\notin\mathcal{I}$.  
  Because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, $\TMfcn_{e_0}\not\simeq\TMfcn_{e_1}$.
  Thus there is an input~$y$ such that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(y)\converges$.

  Consider the flowchart on the left of
  \figureref{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior;
  call it~$\TM_{e}$.
  Apply the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$,
  resulting in the uniformly computable 
  family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$, whose computation is 
  outlined on the right of \figureref{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp12.pdf}}
    \hspace{4em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp13.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches of machines for the proof of half of Rice's Theorem.}
    \label{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}
  \end{figure}
  We've constructed the machines so that if 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_0}$
  and thus $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$. 
  Further,
  if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_1}$
  and thus $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$.
  Therefore if $\mathcal{I}$ were mechanically computable, so that
  we could effectively check whether $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$,
  then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
  (This part of the argument is like the one in the section, but with the 
  cases of $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$ and $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$
  reversed.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{Rice's theorem|)}  
\index{unsolvability|)}\index{unsolvable problem|)}\index{problems!unsolvable|)}






% ========================================
\section{Computably enumerable sets}\index{computably enumerable|(}\index{set!computably enumerable|(}\label{sec:ComputablyEnumerableSets}
% Required for the section on oracles, which in turn is required
% for the seciton on barriers to solving P=NP.
 
To attack the \HP{} the natural thing is to 
start by simulating $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a single step.
Then simulate $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a second step and
also simulate $\TM_1$ on input~$1$ for one step.
After that, run $\TM_0$ on~$0$ for a third step,
followed by $\TM_1$ on~$1$ for a second step,
and then $\TM_2$ on~$2$ for one step.
We cycle among the $\TM_e$ on~$e$ simulations,
running each for a step.\footnote{%
    That is, we run a loop that at iteration~$i$
    will simulate $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ for $s$~steps, 
    where $i=\cantor(e,s)$.
  }
Eventually some of these halt and 
the elements of $K$ start to fill in.
On computer systems this interleaving is called time-slicing 
but in theory discussions it is called 
\citetext{\definend{dovetailing}}{% https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Joinery-throughdovetail.svg
  A dovetail joint is used by carpenters or woodworkers for 
  building strong wood drawers.
  It weaves the two sides in alternately, as shown here,
  an interlocking way.
  \protect\begin{center}
  \protect\small
  \protect\includegraphics[width=0.15\textwidth]{background/pix/dovetail.png} 
  \protect\end{center}}.\index{dovetailing}

We are imagining a computable~$f$ such that $f(0)=e$, where
it happens that $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ is the first of these to halt in the dovetailing, etc.
% And, $f(1)=\hat{e}$, where
% $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ on input~$\hat{e}$ is the second to halt in the dovetailing, etc.
The stream of numbers $f(0)$, 
$f(1)$,~\ldots{}
gives the elements of~$K$.

Why won't this process solve the \HP?
If $e\in K$ then this we will eventually find that out. 
But if $e\notin K$ then we will not.

%<*def:ComputableSet>
Recall that
a set of natural numbers
is computable\index{computable!set}\index{set!computable}
% or decidable,\index{decidable!set}\index{set!decidable}
if its
characteristic function is computable.
%</def:ComputableSet>
We are contrasting that with 
another way to describe a set, % other than giving a criteria for membership, 
listing its members.
\cref{def:Countable} gives the terminology that a function~$f$ with
domain~$\N$ `enumerates' its range.

%<*def:ComputablyEnumerable>
\begin{definition}  \label{def:ComputablyEnumerable}
A set of natural numbers is 
\definend{computably enumerable}\index{computably enumerable set}\index{set!computably enumerable}\index{set!c.e.} 
if it is empty or if it is effectively listable, that is, if it is
the range of a total computable function.
Alternate terms are  
\definend{recursively enumerable}\index{recursively enumerable set|see{computably enumerable set}}\index{set!recursively enumerable|see{computably enumerable set}}\index{set!r.e.|see{computably enumerable set}} 
(or \definend{c.e.},\index{c.e. set|see{computably enumerable}} 
or \definend{r.e.}),\index{r.e. set|see{computably enumerable set}}
or \definend{semicomputable}\index{semicomputable set}\index{set!semicomputable}
or \definend{semidecidable}.\index{semidecidable set}\index{set!semidecidable}
\end{definition}
%</def:ComputablyEnumerable>

Picture 
the stream 
$\TMfcn(0)$, $\TMfcn(1)$, $\TMfcn(2)$,~\ldots{} gradually filling 
out the set.
(It may contain repeats and the numbers may appear 
not in ascending order.)

\begin{remark}
Some streams are particularly interesting.
For example, fix a mathematical topic such as elementary number theory.
Statements in that topic are strings of symbols and we can give each a number
(perhaps by writing that statement in Unicode and the number is its
binary encoding, prefixed with a \str{1} to mitigate against the
ambiguity of leading \str{0}'s).
Set up a process that starts with the axioms for this topic and
does a breadth-first traversal of all logical derivations
from those axioms.
Perhaps first it combines axiom~1 with axiom~2, then next it combines 
axiom~1 with axiom~3, etc.
In this way it generates a list of all of this topic's 
possible proofs.
Whenever it finishes a proof, the process 
outputs the number of the final statement in the derivation,
the proved statement.  

Suppose that we have a statement from this topic that we want to prove,
such as \definend{Goldbach's conjecture}\index{Goldbach's conjecture} 
that every even number is the sum of at most two primes.
We could watch the process enumerate this theory's theorems.
If our statement is provable then its number will eventually appear.
\end{remark}


%<*lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
The following are equivalent for a set of natural numbers.
\begin{thmparts}
\item It is computably enumerable, that is, either it is empty or it is
  the range of a total computable function.
\item It is the domain of a partial computable function.
\item It is the range of a partial computable function.
\end{thmparts}
\end{lemma}
%</lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>

\begin{proof}
We will show that the first two are equivalent.
That the second and third are equivalent is 
\cref{exer:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}.
Assume first that a set $S$ is computably enumerable.
If it is empty then it is the domain of the partial computable function that 
diverges on all inputs.
The other possibility is that $S$ is the range of a total computable~$f$,
and we will describe a partial computable~$g$ with domain~$S$.
To compute $g$:~given the input~$x\in\N$, 
enumerate $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots{} 
and wait for~$x$ to appear as one of these values.
If~$x$ does appear then halt the computation of~$g$, with some nominal output.
If~$x$ never appears then the computation of $g$ never halts.
The domain of~$g$ is~$S$.

For the other direction,
assume that $S$ is the domain of a partial computable function~$\hat{g}$, 
to show that $S$ is computably enumerable.
If it is empty then it is computably enumerable by definition.

Otherwise
we must produce a total computable~$\hat{f}$ whose range is~$S$.
Fix some $s_0\in S$.
Given $n\in \N$, run the computations of
$\hat{g}(0)$, $\hat{g}(1)$, \ldots{} $\hat{g}(n)$, each for $n$-many steps.
Possibly some of these halt.
Define $\hat{f}(n)$ to be the least $k$ where $\hat{g}(k)$ halts within~$n$ 
steps and so 
that $k\notin\set{\hat{f}(0),\hat{f}(1),\ldots \hat{f}(n-1)}$.
If no such~$k$ exists then define $\hat{f}(n)=s_0$ (this makes 
$\hat{f}$ a total function).

We finish by verifying that $\hat{f}$ is the desired enumeration.
If $t\notin S$ then $\hat{g}(t)$ never converges 
and so~$t$ is never enumerated by~$\hat{f}\!$.
If $t\in S$ then eventually $\hat{g}(t)$ must converge,
in some number of steps,~$n_t$.
The number~$t$ is then queued for output by~$\hat{f}$ in the sense that
it will be enumerated as, at most, $\hat{f}(n_t+t)$. 
\end{proof}

So for instance, the collection of listable sets is the same as the collection
of domains of computable functions.
To describe this collection, \cref{def:ComputablyEnumerable} is more natural 
but also more awkward in that it has the clause about the empty set.

%<*def:WSubE>
\begin{definition}
\definend{$W_e=\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_e(x)\converges}$}
\end{definition}
%</def:WSubE>

%<*description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>
The contrast between computable and computably enumerable is that
a set~$S$ is computable if there is a
Turing machine that decides membership, that
inputs a number~$x$ and 
decides either `yes' or `no' whether $x\in S$.
But with computably enumerable, given some~$x$
we can set up a machine to monitor the number stream
and 
if $x$ appear then this machine decides `yes'. 
However this machine need not ever discover `no'.
(Although there may well be a way to get no's, as the first item in the next
result notes.)
Restated, 
a set is computable if there is a Turing machine that 
recognizes both members and nonmembers
while
a set is computably enumerable if there is a Turing machine that
recognizes members.
%</description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:ComputableAndCE}
\begin{thmparts}
\item \label{lem:SetComputableThenSetCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEi>
  If a set is computable then it is computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEi>
\item \label{lem:SetComputableIffSetAndCompCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEii>
  A set is computable if and only if both it and its
  complement are computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEii>
\end{thmparts}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let~$S\subseteq\N$ be computable so that its characteristic function
is the computable function~$\TMfcn$.
We will produce a partial computable~$f$ whose domain is~$S$.
Begin by finding the smallest element of~$S$ 
by testing whether $0\in S$, that is, whether $\TMfcn(0)=1$.
Then test whether $1\in S$, \ldots\sentencespace 
If this testing ever halts with a $k_0$ so that $\TMfcn(k_0)=1$,
then set $f(0)=k_0$.
Next, iterate:~find the second smallest element of~$S$ 
by testing whether $k_0+1\in S$,  $k_0+2\in S$, \ldots{} and
if this testing ever halts with a $k_1$
then set $f(1)=k_1$.
Clearly the domain of~$f$ consists of the elements of~$S$,
even when $S=\emptyset$.

As to the second item, suppose first that $S$ is computable.
The complement $\setcomp{S}$ is also computable because
its characteristic function is $\charfcn{\setcomp{S}}=1-\charfcn{S}$.
Then this result's first item gives that both $S$ and 
$\setcomp{S}$ are computably enumerable.

Finally,
suppose that both $S$ and $\setcomp{S}$ are computably enumerable.
Let $S$ be enumerated by the function~$g$ and let $\setcomp{S}$ be
enumerated by $\hat{g}$. 
Given~$x\in\N$, we will run an effective procedure to determine whether~$x\in S$.
We dovetail the two enumerations:~first run 
the computation of $g(0)$ for a step
and the computation of $\hat{g}(0)$ for a step.
Next run the computations of $g(0)$ and
$\hat{g}(0)$ for a second step, along with the computations of $g(1)$ and
$\hat{g}(1)$ for a step each.
Under this procedure,
eventually $x$ will be enumerated into either $S$ or~$\setcomp{S}\!$.
\end{proof}

%<*lem:HPIsCE>
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:KCompNotCE}
The \HP{} set $K$ is computably enumerable.\index{K, the Halting problem set@$K$, the Halting problem set}
Its complement $\setcomp{K}$ is not.
\end{corollary}
%</lem:HPIsCE>

\begin{proof}
The set~$K$ is the domain of
$f(x)=\TMfcn_x(x)$,
which is a partial computable function by Church's Thesis.
If the complement~$\setcomp{K}$ were computably enumerable then
\cref{lem:ComputableAndCE} would imply that $K$ is computable,
but it isn't.
\end{proof}

That result gives one reason to be interested in computably
enumerable sets, namely that the
\HP{} set~$K$ falls into the class of computably enumerable sets,
as do such sets as 
$\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
and $\set{e\suchthat \text{there is an $x$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$. 
So the collection of computably enumerable sets contains
lots of interesting members.

Another reason that these sets are interesting is 
philosophical:~with Church's Thesis we can think that in a sense
computable sets are the only sets 
that we will ever know,
and computably enumerable sets are those that we at least half know.

% .......................................
\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A question on the quiz asked you to define computably enumerable.
A friend says that they answered, 
``A set that can be enumerated by a Turing machine
but that is not computable.''
Is that right?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Not completely right.
  It is right that 
  a set is computably enumerable if it can be enumerated by a Turing machine.
  That is, a Turing machine exists that, when started on a blank tape, will
  print all of the sets members on the tape. 
  (This machine just outputs $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots\@ 
  It may run forever if there are
  infinitely many elements in the set.)
  However, the ``but is not computable'' is wrong.
  Any computable set is computably enumerable (although there are 
  computably enumerable sets that are not computable).
  For instance, the set of even numbers is computably enumerable and also
  computable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each set, produce a function that enumerates it
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\N$
\item the even numbers
\item the perfect squares
\item the set $\set{5,7,11}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
For each part we will produce a computable total
function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ that has the given set as range. 
\begin{exparts}
  \item The identity function, $f(x)=x$, works.
  \item The  function $f(x)=2x$ comes to mind.
  \item $f(x)=x^2$
  \item This will do.
  \begin{equation*}
     f(x)=
     \begin{cases}
       5  &\case{if $x=0$}  \\
       7  &\case{if $x=1$}  \\
      11  &\case{otherwise}
     \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each, produce a function that enumerates it
\begin{exparts*}
\item the prime numbers
\item the natural numbers whose digits are in non-increasing order
  (e.g., $531$ or $5331$ but not $513$).
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  For each item we produce an effective function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$. 
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The association $n\mapsto\text{$n$-th prime}$ is a function.
    Clearly we can write a program that computes it, so by Church's Thesis
    it is effective.
  \item To compute this function one approach is brute 
    force:~generate the $n$-th one by 
    searching through the natural numbers, $0$, $1$, \ldots{}\,, checking
    each to see if its digits appear in non-increasing order.
    Output the $n$-th such number.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Are there computably enumerable sets that are infinite?
Finite?
Empty?
All of the natural numbers?
\begin{answer}
Yes to all four.
For the first and fourth, 
we can easily write a Turing machine that halts on all
numbers, so the set of all natural numbers is computably
enumerable.
For the second and third, in the first section of the first 
chapter we saw a Turing machine for which there are no inputs
that result in it halting, so the empty set is computably enumerable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these two is computable and the other is computably enumerable but
not computable.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in less than twenty steps}}$
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in more than twenty steps}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first is computable, since to decide if a number~$e$ is a member of 
  this set we can run a simulation for twenty steps.
  The second is computably enumerable but not computable.
  It is computably enumerable because we can dovetail computations for the 
  indices $e\in\N$.
  It is not computable because clearly if we could solve it then we could 
  solve the problem of deciding membership in 
  $\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(y)\converges}$, but we cannot solve that problem
  by Rice's Theorem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Which of these sets are decidable, which are semidecidable but not decidable, 
and which are neither?
Justify in one sentence.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
on input~$7$.
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
on input~$7$.
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
% on every input.
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
% on all inputs.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
This set is computable, since we can just run the machine for a hundred steps.
Because it is computable, it is also computably enumerable.
\item 
This set is also computable, again since we can just run the machine
for a hundred steps.
Because it is computable, it is also computably enumerable.
% \item
% This set is neither computable nor computably enumerable.
% Testing all inputs would never halt.
% \item
% As with the prior item, this set is neither computable nor computably 
% enumerable.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Short answer:~for each set state whether it is computable, 
computably enumerable but not computable, or neither.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of indices~$e$ of Turing machines that contain an instruction using
state~$q_4$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$ in fewer than
 $100$ steps.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Decidable.
  Indices are source-equivalent: there is a program that takes in the index~$e$
  and puts out the set of instructions for~$\TM_e$.  
  With that set of instructions, just scan it for~$q_4$,
  \item Semidecidable but not decidable.
  To show it is computably enumerable, use dovetailing on the indices~$e\in\N$
  to run $\TM_e$ on input~$3$.
  When a the machines halts, enumerate its~$e$ into the set.
  As to being not computable, we know from the prior section that this is not 
  a computable set.
  \item Computable.
  Given~$e$, decide if it is in the set by running~$\TM_e$ on~$3$ for the
  hundred steps.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Someone online says, 
``every countable set~$S$ is computably enumerable because if
$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ has range~$S$ then you have the enumeration 
$S$ as $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots''\sentencespace
Explain why this is wrong.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The function is an enumeration but unless it is computable, it is not
  a computable enumeration.
  This can happen; an example of a countable set that is not 
  computably enumerable is $\setcomp{K}$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The set $A_5=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(5)\converges}$ is not
computable.
Show that it is computably enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Briefly, we dovetail.
  First run $\TM_0$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ on input~$5$ for a second step
  and  $\TM_1$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ for a third step,
  along with $\TM_1$ for its second step
  and $\TM_2$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Continue in this way.
  The enumerating function~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ is:~$f(0)$ is the index
  of the first such computation to halt, $f(1)$ is the index of the second, etc.
  (Note that $A_5$ is infinite since any finite set is computable, so 
  computation of any $f(i)$ will halt in a finite number of steps.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the set 
$\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(2)=4}$
is computably enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Dovetail.
  Start by running $\TM_0$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ on input~$2$ for a second step
  and  $\TM_1$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Next, run  $\TM_0$ for a third step,
  along with $\TM_1$ for its second step
  and $\TM_2$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Continue in this way.
  Then~$f(0)$ is the index
  of the first such computation to halt and output~$4$, 
  $f(1)$ is the index of the second, etc.
  (This set is infinite since any finite set is computable
  and this set is clearly not computable.
  Thus 
  computation of any $f(i)$ will halt in a finite number of steps.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name a set that has an enumeration but not a computable enumeration.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The complement of the \HP{} set, $\setcomp{K}$,
  is countable since it is a subset of~$\N$.
  Thus it has an enumeration, a function with domain~$\N$ whose
  range is this set.
  But \cref{cor:KCompNotCE} shows that it has no computable enumeration.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name three sets that are computably enumerable but not computable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  There are of course many correct answers.
  One answer is to take~$K$ as the first set.
  For the second, take~$K$ with its smallest element omitted and
  for the third, take~$K$ with the smallest two elements omitted. 

  A perhaps less wiseguy-ish answer is to take~$K$ along with some sets
  that we used as examples in the prior section, such as 
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
  and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Let $K_0=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$.\index{K0, set of halting pairs@$K_0$, set of halting pairs}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Show that it is computably enumerable.
\item
Show that its columns, the sets
$C_e=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$,
make up all of the computable enumerable sets.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  Enumerate it by dovetailing.
  The difference from some of the dovetail examples is that here there are
  two numbers, $e$ and~$x$.
  So recall Cantor's pairing function.
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{r|ccccccc@{\hspace{6pt}}c}
      \tabulartext{Number}  
         &$0$    &$1$   &$2$  &$3$  &$4$  &$5$  &$6$ &\ldots   
     \\ \cline{2-9}
      \tabulartext{Pair} 
                 &$\sequence{0,0}$  
                 &$\sequence{0,1}$ 
                 &$\sequence{1,0}$ 
                 &$\sequence{0,2}$  
                 &$\sequence{1,1}$ 
                 &$\sequence{2,0}$ 
                 &$\sequence{0,3}$  
                 &\ldots 
       \end{tabular}
  \end{center}       
  To dovetail, first run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for one step.
  Then run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a second step
  along with $\TM_0$ on input~$1$ for one step.
  Next, run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for its third step,
  and $\TM_0$ on input~$1$ for its second step,
  and $\TM_1$ on input~$0$ for one step.
  The enumerating function outputs $\sequence{e,x}$ when 
  $\TM_e$ halts on~$x$.
  \item
  This is immediate from the second item of 
  \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We know that there are subsets of~$\N$ that are not computable.
Are the computably enumerable sets the rest of the subsets?
\begin{answer}\verified
  The two collections are not disjoint.
  Rather, the collection of computable sets is a subset of the 
  collection of computably enumerable sets.

  Further, clearly the collection 
  $\set{W_e\suchthat e\in\N}$ is countable.
  Since there are uncountably many subsets of~$\N$ (because
  the power set of~$\N$ has cardinality greater than~$\N$), the 
  computably enumerable sets do not include every subset of~$\N$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the set 
$\text{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ for all $x$}}$\index{Tot@$\text{Tot}$, set of total computable functions}
is not computable and not computably enumerable.
\hint~if this collection is computably enumerable then we can get a table
like the one that starts Section~II.\ref{sec:HaltingProblem} on 
the \HP. 
\begin{answer}\verified
  The computable sets are also computably enumerable
  so we will have shown that $\textrm{Tot}$ 
  is neither computable nor computably enumerable if we have shown
  that it is not computably enumerable.

  So, assume that 
  $\text{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ for all $x$}}$
  is computably enumerable, enumerated by~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$.
  Following the hint, that gives a table like the one starting
  \cref{sec:Unsolvability} on Unsolvability, because 
  the $i,j$ entry $\TMfcn_{i}(j)$ is sure to halt.
  Diagonalize:~consider $\map{h}{\N}{\N}$ given by $h(e)=1+\TMfcn_e(e)$.
  It is clearly effective, total, and unequal to any member of
  $\textrm{Tot}$, which is a contradiction. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the set $\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\diverges}$
is not computably enumerable.
\begin{answer}
We can easily show that the set
$S=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\diverges}$ is unsolvable, for instance with 
Rice's theorem.
Clearly the complement, $\setcomp{S}=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$,
is computably enumerable. 
If $S$ were also computably enumerable then by \cref{lem:ComputableAndCE}
that would contradict that it is not computable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can there be a set such that the problem of determining membership in that
set is unsolvable, and also the set is computably enumerable?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes, the \HP{} set~$K$ fits both criteria.
  Three other examples are the set
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$,
  the set 
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$,
  and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$\TMfcn_e(x)=2x$ for all $x\in\N$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the collection of computably enumerable sets is countable.
\begin{answer}
There are only countably many Turing machines to do the enumeration.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Prove that every finite set is computably enumerable.
\item
Sketch a program that inputs a finite set and returns
a function enumerating the set.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer to the second effectively answers the first, but we will do both
  items anyway.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  Let $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$ for~$k\in\N$.
  Then this function enumerates~$S$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x=s_0$ or \ldots{} or $x=s_{k-1}$}  \\
      0  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  It is clearly computable.
  \item
  The algorithm inputs a set of numbers $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$.
  It returns a function, the one in the prior item.
  This function is clearly computable.

  Another approach is to start with the number $k=\sizeof{S}$.
  Define a function with  $k+1$ inputs, $x_0,\ldots x_{k-1},y$ that 
  tests whether $y$ equals any of the $x_i$'s, and returns~$1$ if 
  it does or~$0$ if it does not.
  Where $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$, apply the \smn{} Theorem to freeze
  the value of each $x_i$ to be~$s_i$.
  That's the desired function.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that every infinite computably enumerable set has an infinite computable
subset.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Let the computably enumerable set be $W$ 
  and fix a computable enumeration $\TMfcn(0)$, $\TMfcn(1)$, \ldots\sentencespace
  We will define an $S\subseteq W$ that is computable.
  The first element of~$S$ is~$s_0=\TMfcn(0)$.
  The second element~$s_1$ is the first member of the enumeration that
  is larger than $s_0$;~such a number must eventually appear
  because $W$ is infinite.
  In general~$s_i$ is the first element of the enumeration that is larger 
  than $s_{i-1}$ (as with $s_1$, one must eventually appear).

  The set~$S$ is computably enumerable because we have just described a 
  mechanical procedure to enumerate it.
  It is computable because given a number $n\in\N$, to determine
  whether it is an element of~$S$ just wait until either it is enumerated 
  into~$S$ 
  or a number larger than it is enumerated into~$S$, which shows
  $n\notin S$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Consider the function $\operatorname{steps}$ defined by: 
$\operatorname{steps}(e)$ is 
the minimal number of steps so that Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts if
started with $e$ on its
input tape, or is undefined if the machine never halts.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Argue that this function is partial computable.
\item Argue that it is not total.
\item Prove that it has no total extension, no total computable 
$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ so that if $\operatorname{steps}(e)\converges$ then
$\operatorname{steps}(e)=f(e)$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Arguing by Church's Thesis:~compute $\operatorname{steps}(e)$ by running
$\TM_e$ on input~$e$ and if it ever halts, outputting the number of steps.
\item
If $\operatorname{steps}$ were total in addition to being computable
then we could solve the \HP.
To decide whether $e\in K$, first compute $\operatorname{steps}(e)$. 
Then run $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ for $\operatorname{steps}(e)$-many steps.
If this computation has not halted by then, it will never halt.
\item
This is the same as the prior argument, but with~$f$ in place of 
$\operatorname{steps}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $f$ be a partial computable function that 
enumerates an infinite set~$R\subseteq \N$.
Produce a total computable function
that enumerates~$R$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Fix an $\hat{r}\in R$.
  Then this function
  \begin{equation*}
    g(i)=
    \begin{cases}
      f(i)    &\case{if $f(i)\converges$}  \\
      \hat{r} &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
   is total, computable, and enumerates~$R$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Suppose that there is a set $A$ and a computable function $f$ such that
% the set difference $A-\operatorname{range}(f)$ is finite.
% Show that $A$ is computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Write a program that will, given two enumerating programs, dovetail them.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \definend{computable enumerable in increasing order}\index{computably enumerable set!in increasing order}
if there is a computable function~$f$ 
that is increasing:~$n<m$ implies~$f(n)<f(m)$, and whose range is the set.
Prove that
an infinite set~$S$ is computable if and only if it is 
computably enumerable in increasing order.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Suppose that $S$ is computable, so that its characteristic function
  \begin{equation*}
    \charfcn{S}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in S$}  \\
      0  &\case{if not}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is a computable function.
  To enumerate~$S$ in increasing order the idea is to run $\charfcn{S}(0)$, 
  $\charfcn{S}(1)$, \ldots{}\,, each of which must converge because the
  function is computable, and then the first element of~$S$ is the smallest, the
  second element is the second smallest, etc.

  More formally, we define the enumerating function by:~where $s_0$ 
  is the smallest element of~$S$ then $f(0)=s_0$, and where 
  we have values for $f(0)$, \ldots $f(k)$ then the value for
  $f(k+1)$ is the smallest number~$x$ such that $x>f(k)$ and 
  $\charfcn{S}(x)=1$.
  Because $S$ is infinite, there is always such an~$x$.

  For the other direction, assume that $S$ is an infinite set that is 
  enumerable in increasing order, by the computable function~$h$.
  Given~$x$, to decide whether $x\in S$, compute $h(0)$, $h(1)$, \ldots{}
  until one of the outputs is~$x$
  (in which case $x\in S$) or one of the outputs is a number larger 
  than~$x$ (in which case $x\notin S$).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is 
\definend{computably enumerable without repetition}\index{computably enumerable set!without repetition} if it is
the range of a computable function that is one-to-one.
Prove that a set is computably enumerable and infinite if and only if
it is computably enumerable without repetition.
\begin{answer}\verified
One direction is easy.
If a set is computably enumerable without repetition then 
it must be infinite, simply because if it were finite then
the enumeration would run out of new elements to output.

The other direction is about memoizing.
Let the set~$S$ be infinite and computably enumerable with
enumerating function $f$.
We will produce an enumerating function~$g$ that is one-to-one.
Define $g(0)=f(0)$.
For each $i\in\N$ define $g(i+1)$ to be the first element of~$S$ enumerated
by~$f$ after it enumerates~$g(i)$ that is not an element 
of~$\set{g(0),\ldots\, g(i)}$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is 
\definend{co-computably enumerable}\index{computably enumerable set!co-computably enumerable}\index{co-computably enumerable} 
if its complement is computably enumerable.
Produce a set that is neither computably enumerable nor co-computably 
enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The set of ordered pairs 
  $\set{\sequence{a,b}\suchthat 
       \text{$a\in K$ and $b\in\setcomp{K}$}}$ is not
  computably enumerable, or else $\setcomp{K}$ would be computably enumerable.
  Similarly it is not co-computably enumerable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\textit{(See also the next exercise.)}
Computability is a property of sets so we can consider its
interaction with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Must a subset of a computable set be computable?
\item
Must the union of two computable sets be computable?
\item
Intersection?
\item
Complement?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1024.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1025.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1026.pdf}}
    \caption{Machines to decide union, intersection, and complement of computable sets.}
    \label{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}
  \end{figure}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No.
  The set $\N$ is obviously computable.
  Its subset~$K$ is not.
  \item Yes.
  Let $S_0\subseteq \N$ and $S_1\subseteq\N$ be computable via
  the functions $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and~$\TMfcn_{e_1}$.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the union is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \item Yes.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the intersection is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \item Yes.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the complement is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\textit{(See also the prior exercise.)}
We can consider the interaction of computable enumerability 
with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Must a subset of a computably enumerable set be computably enumerable?
\item
Must the union of two computably enumerable sets be computably enumerable?
\item
Intersection?
\item
Complement?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No.
  The  set~$\N$ is computably enumerable.
  Its subset, the complement of the \HP{} set, $\complement{K}$, 
  is not computably enumerable.
  \item Yes.
  Let $W_{e_0},W_{e_1}\subseteq\N$ be computably enumerable,
  via $\map{\TMfcn_{e_0},\TMfcn_{e_1}}{\N}{\N}$.
  Dovetail enumerations of the two sets.
  That is, run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$0$ for a step each.
  Then, run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$0$ for a second step,
  and also run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$1$ for a step each.
  Continue in this way, and when
  a number is enumerated into either of $W_{e_0}$ or~$W_{e_1}$,
  enumerate it into their union.
  \item Yes.
  As in the prior item, 
  dovetail enumerations of the two sets.
  When a number has been enumerated into both of $W_{e_0}$ or~$W_{e_1}$,
  enumerate it into their intersection.
  \item No.
  The \HP{} set~$K$ is computably enumerable but its complement is not.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Show that if $h$ is a partially computable function and
% $S$ is a computably enumerable set, then the inverse image
% $h^{-1}(S)$ is computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% A set $S\subseteq \N^k$ is a \textit{projection}
% of the set $T\subseteq\N^{k+1}$
% if $S$ is the set of $k$-tuples $\sequence{x_0,\ldots x_{k-1}}$ such that
% there is a $i\in\N$ so that $\sequence{i,x_0,\ldots x_{k-1}}\in T$.
% Prove that the projection of a computably enumerable set is also
% computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
Finish the proof of \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn} by showing that
the second and third items are equivalent.
\begin{answer}\verified
Let $S$ be the domain of a partial computable function~$f$.
We will produce a partial computable function~$g$ whose range is~$S$
by dovetailing.

First run the computation of~$f(0)$ for a step.
Then run the computation of~$f(0)$ for a second step
and the computation of~$f(1)$ for a step.
Next run the computation of~$f(0)$ for its third step,
run the computation of~$f(1)$ for its second step,
and run the computation of~$f(2)$ for a step.
Continue in this way.
Define $g(0)$ to be the first number~$i$ where the computation of~$f(i)$
halts, define $g(1)$ to be the next number~$j$ where the computation of~$f(j)$
halts, etc.
Clearly $g$ is partial computable and has range~$S$.

The other direction works much the same way.
We are given a partial computable~$g$ whose range is~$S$ and we will 
produce a partial computable~$f$ whose domain is~$S$ by
dovetailing computations of~$g$. 
First run~$g(0)$ for a step.
Then run the computation of~$g(0)$ for a second step
and the computation of~$g(1)$ for a step.
Next run~$g(0)$ for its third step,
$g(1)$ for its second step,
and~$g(2)$ for a step.
Continue in this way.
When a computation halts, $g(i)=k$, define $f(k)$ to have some nominal
value, such as~$42$.
Clearly $f$ is partial computable and has domain~$S$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{computably enumerable|)}\index{set!computably enumerable|)}












\section{Oracles}\index{oracle|(}\index{set!oracle|(} \label{sec:Oracles}
\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{background/pix/delphi.jpg}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Collier_(painter)}{Priestess of Delphi (Collier 1891)}}
The problem of deciding whether a machine halts 
is so hard that it is unsolvable.
It this the absolutely hardest problem
or are there ones that are even harder?

% \margingraphic{0.23\textwidth}{background/pix/delphi.png}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythia}{The oracle at Delphi}}
What does it mean to say that one problem is harder than another?
We have compared problem hardness already, 
for instance when we considered the problem
of whether a Turing machine halts on input~$3$.
There we proved that if we could solve the halts-on-$3$ problem
then we could solve the \HP.
That is, we proved that halts-on-$3$ is at least as hard as the \HP.
So, the idea is that
one problem is at least as hard as a second one
if solving the first would also give us a solution to the 
second.\footnote{%
  We can instead conceptualize that the first problem is at least as general as
  the second.
  For instance, the problem of inputting a natural number and
  outputting its prime factors is at least as general as the problem of
  inputting a natural and determining whether it is divisible by seven.}

Under Church's Thesis we interpret the unsolvability of the \HP{} to
say that no mechanism can answer all questions about
membership in~$K$.
So if we want to answer questions about problems that are related to~$K$ then 
we need the answers to be supplied in some way that
isn't an in-principle physically realizable discrete
machine\footnote{%
  Turing introduced oracles in his PhD thesis.
  He said, \protect\citetext{``We shall not go any 
  further into the nature of this oracle
  apart from saying that it cannot be a machine.''}{%
  \autocite{TuringThesis}}}

Consequently, we posit 
an \definend{oracle}\index{oracle}
that we attach to the Turing machine and
that acts as the characteristic function of a set. 
Thus to see what could be computed if we could solve
the \HP{}, we can attach a $K$-oracle that
answers,
``Is $x\in K$?''
This oracle is a black box, meaning that we can't open it to 
see how it works.\footnote{%
  Opening it would
  let out the \citetext{magic smoke}{See \autocite{wiki:MagicSmoke}.}, 
  the stuff
  inside an integrated circuit that makes it work.
  After all, once the smoke get out, the circuit no longer works.}

\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine01.pdf}
\end{center}

We can formally define computation with an oracle $X\subseteq\N$ by
extending the definition of Turing machines.
But \citetext{we will instead describe it conceptually}{%
  For a full treatment see \autocite{Rogers}.}.
Imagine adding to a programming language 
a Boolean function \codeinline{oracle?},
or allowing queries to an oracle in a flowchart.\footnote{%
  We allow a program to use one such query, or 
  more than one, or none at all. 
  }
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{2.5in}
\begin{lstlisting}
(if (oracle? x)
    (displayln "It is in the set")
    (displayln "It is not in the set"))
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}
\qquad
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts21.pdf}}
\end{center}
% If $x\in X$
% then this takes one branch, while if $x\notin X$ then it takes the other.

We can change the oracle without changing the program code\Dash
in the picture if we swap out the black box $X$~oracle for a $Y$~oracle
then the white box is unchanged.
(Of course, the values returned by the oracle may change,
which may change the tape output when we run
the two-box system.)
% But the white box stays the same.

The rest of what we have previously 
developed about Turing machines carries over.
In particular, each such machine has an index.
That index is source-equivalent, 
meaning that from an index we can compute the
machine source
and from the source we can find the index.

Therefore to fully specify a relative computation, we must specify
which machine we are using and which inputs, along with 
specifying the oracle set.
That explains the notations for the white box, 
the Turing machine that can make oracle calls, 
\definend{$\TM_e^X$},\index{oracle Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!with oracle}
and for the function computed relative to an oracle, 
\definend{$\TMfcn^X_e(x)$}.\index{computation!relative to an oracle}

\begin{definition}  \label{def:TuringReducible}
Let~$X$ be a set.
We say that the set
$S$ 
is 
\definend{computable from the oracle~$X$}\index{computable!from a set}
or 
\definend{$X$-computable}\index{computable!relative to a set}
or 
\definend{computable relative to~$X$},\index{computable!relative to a set}
or 
\definend{reduces to~$X$}\index{reduces to}\index{set!reduces to} 
or is 
\definend{Turing reducible to~$X$},\index{Turing reducible}\index{T reducible@$T$ reducible} 
denoted \definend{$S\leq_T X$}, 
if the characteristic function of~$S$ is one of the
functions computed relative to~$X$,
that is, if $\charfcn{S}=\TMfcn^X_e$ for some~$e$. 
\end{definition}

Observe that the reduction function
$\TMfcn^X_e$ is total since the characteristic 
function is total.

The terminology
`$S$ reduces to $X$' can cause confusion.\footnote{%
  We have discussed this earlier, in the \protect\HP{} section on
  page~\protect\pageref{pageref:reducesto}.}
The notation $S\leq_T X$ is more suggestive:~$X$ 
is more general than~$S$, or ``knows more'' than~$S$, in that
we can get answers for questions about~$S$ by
using answers to questions about~$X$.%

\begin{example}  \label{ex:KLeqTHaltsOnThree}
Recall the problem of determining, given~$e$, whether $\TM_e$ halts
on input~$3$.\index{Halts On Three problem@\probname{Halts On Three} problem}
It asks for a machine that acts as the
characteristic function $\charfcn{A}$ of the set
$A=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on~$3$}}$.
We will show that $K\leq_T A$.

To satisfy the above definition we produce an oracle machine $\TM_e^{X}\!$.
We first reprise \cref{ex:HaltOnThree}.
We started the function $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$ below.
The flowchart in the middle,
by Church's Thesis, shows that there is a 
Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$;
let it have index~$e_0$.
The \smn{} theorem gives
a family of machines $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}$ parametrized by $x$,
as outlined on the right.
Then $k\in K$ if and only if
$s(e_0,k)\in A$, for any $k\in\N$.
\begin{center}
  $\psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \;\diverges &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}$
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1033.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1034.pdf}}
\end{center}

With that we can build the oracle machine. 
% the white box pictured earlier.
The machine below is $\TM_e^{X}\!$.
It uses the constant~$e_0$ 
from the prior paragraph.
If it is connected to an $A$~oracle 
then it computes the characteristic function of $K$, 
by the prior paragraph's final sentence.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem} \label{th:TReductionReflexiveAndTrans}
\begin{thmparts}
\item \textsc{(Reflexivity)} Every set is computable from itself, $A\leq_T A$.
\item \textsc{(Transitivity)} If $A\leq_T B$ and $B\leq_T C$ then $A\leq_T C$.
\end{thmparts}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For Reflexivity we must show how to 
compute $\charfcn{A}$ using an $A$~oracle.
So, given a number~$x$, we want to 
decide whether $x$ is an element of~$A$ by using the $A$
oracle.
That's trivial because those are exactly the questions that the oracle answers.

For Transitivity suppose that $\TM_e^B$ computes the characteristic
function of~$A$ and that
$\TM_{\hat{e}}^C$ computes the characteristic function of~$B$.
Then we can compute the characteristic function of $A$
directly from~$C$, by going through
in the computation of $A$ from $B$ and replacing the
$B$-oracle calls with calls to $\TM_{\hat{e}}^C$.
\end{proof}

So the sets of natural numbers are arranged in a partial order,
with some sets preceding others in the sense that $S$ is before $X$
if $S\leq_T X$.
We next show that there are sets that come at the very beginning,
not preceded by any others.   

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing}
For any  $X\subseteq \N$,
all computable sets 
are Turing reducible to $X$.
In particular, $\emptyset\leq_T X$ and $\N\leq_T X$.
Further, a set is computable if and only if it is reducible 
to the empty set, or to any computable set.  
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume that a set is computable, so that
its characteristic function is computable.
That characteristic function
can be computed from~$X$ using an oracle Turing machine simply by ignoring the
oracle.

For the second statement suppose that the characteristic function of~$S$ 
can be oracle computed
by reference to a computable set,
so that $\charfcn{S}=\TMfcn_e^X$ where 
$\charfcn{X}$ is computable. 
Then replacing oracle calls in the machine $\TM_e^X$ with 
direct computations of $\charfcn{X}$ will compute $\charfcn{S}$ without
using an oracle.  
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
Two sets $A,B$ are 
\definend{Turing equivalent}\index{Turing equivalent}\index{set!Turing equivalent} 
or 
\definend{$T$-equivalent},\index{T equivalent@$T$ equivalent}\index{set!T equivalent@$T$ equivalent} 
denoted \definend{$A\equiv_T B$}, if both $A\leq_T B$
and $B\leq_T A$.
% For any set~$A$, the collection of sets Turing equivalent to $A$ is the
% \definend{Turing degree}\index{Turing degree}\index{set!Turing degree} 
% of~$A$.
% We denote
% the Turing degree of a set named~$A$ by using the 
% boldface lower case letter, here~\definend{$\textbf{a}$}.
\end{definition}

Showing that two sets are $T$-equivalent, that they are inter-computable,
shows that the two seemingly-different problems are actually versions
of the same problem.

We next give another example of equivalent problems, beyond pairs
of computable sets. 
Of course, the \HP{} is
to decide whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.
A person may perceive that there is 
a more natural problem, that of deciding whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$.

\begin{definition} \index{K0, set of halting pairs@$K_0$, set of halting pairs}
  $\definend{K_0}=\set{\sequence{e,x} \suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$}}$
\end{definition}

We will show that the two are equivalent,
that if you can solve the one problem then
you can solve the other. 
Thus our choice of~$K$ for a touchstone problem is a 
matter of convenience and convention.\footnote{%  
  We use $K$ 
  because it is the standard in the literature
  and because it has some technical advantages,
  including that it falls out of the diagonalization development that we did
  at the start of this subsection.}

\begin{theorem}
$K\equiv_T K_0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $K\leq_T K_0$, suppose that we have access to a $K_0$-oracle.
This will determine $K$ from that oracle.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts23.pdf}}
\end{center}

For the $K_0\leq_T K$ half, 
% The problem we must solve is:~given a pair $\sequence{e,x}$,
% determine whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$.
consider the flowchart on the left below.
This machine 
halts for all input triples $\sequence{e,x,y}$ if 
and only if $\sequence{e,x}\in K_0$.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine implementing
it; let that machine be~$\TM_{e_0}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp03.pdf}
  \qquad
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp04.pdf}
\end{center}
Get the 
flowchart on the right by applying the \smn{} theorem
to parametrize $e$ and~$x$.
That is, on the right is a sketch of $\TM_{s(e_0,e,x)}$.

Now to make the oracle Turing machine.
Given a pair $\sequence{e,x}$, the
right-side machine above $\TM_{s(e_0,e,x)}$
either halts on all inputs~$y$ 
or fails to halt on all inputs, depending on whether $\phi_e(x)\converges$.
In particular, 
$\TM_{s(e_0,e,x)}$ halts on input $s(e_0,e,x)$\Dash that is, $s(e_0,e,x)\in K$\Dash
if and only if $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts24.pdf}}
\end{center}
Given oracle~$K$, this machine acts as the characteristic function of $K_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
The \HP{} is at least as hard as any computably enumerable
problem:~$W_e\leq_T K$ for all~$e\in\N$. 
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
the computably enumerable sets are the columns of $K_0$, as 
% \begin{equation*}
$
  W_e
  =\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_e(y)\converges}
  =\set{y\suchthat \sequence{e,y}\in K_0}
$.
% \end{equation*}
So $W_e\leq_T K_0\equiv_T K$.
\end{proof}

Because the \HP{} is in this sense the hardest of the computably enumerable
problems, we say that it is 
\definend{complete}\index{complete}\index{computably enumerable!K is complete@$K$ is complete}\index{K, the Halting problem set@$K$, the Halting problem set!complete among c.e. sets}
among the c.e. sets.

We have started to picture the partial order of computability.
All computable sets sit together at the beginning.
% Sets further from the beginning are, intuitively, less computable.
The \HP{} set $K$ is as far from the beginning as any computably
enumerable set can be.
We finish this section by describing a way, given a set, 
to jump further up the partial order than that set.

\begin{theorem}
There is no index~$e\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_e^K$ is the 
characteristic function of 
$\smash{K^K}=\set{x\suchthat \smash{\TMfcn_x^K}(x)\converges}$.
That is, where the 
\definend{relativized \HP{}}\index{relativized Halting problem}\index{Halting problem!relativized} 
is the problem of determining membership in $\smash{K^K\!}$,
its solution is not computable from a~$K$ oracle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume otherwise, that there is a computation relative to a
$K$~oracle, $\TM_e^K\!$, that acts as the characteristic function of $K^K\!$.
\begin{equation*}
  \charfcn{K^K}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
  1  &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\converges$}  \\
  0  &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
We will adapt the proof that the \HP{} is unsolvable.
Following that argument, consider the 
function below and note that it is computable relative to a $K$~oracle.
To make that clear, the flowchart illustrates the function's construction;
for the branch it uses the assumption that $\charfcn{K^K}$
is computable as~$\TMfcn_e^K\!$.
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.35\textwidth}
  \begin{equation*}
  f^K(x)=
    \begin{cases}
    42    &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\diverges$}  \\
    \uparrow  &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\converges$}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{minipage}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts03.pdf}}
\end{center}
Since $f$ is computable, it has an index.
Let that index be~$\hat{e}$, so that
$f^K=\TMfcn^K_{\hat{e}}$.

Now feed $f$ its own index\Dash that is, consider
$f^K(\hat{e})=\TMfcn^K_{\hat{e}}(\hat{e})$.
If that diverges then the first clause in the definition of~$f$
gives that $f^K(\hat{e})\converges$,
which is a contradiction.
If instead $f^K(\hat{e})$ converges then the second clause in the 
definition of~$f$ 
gives $f^K(\hat{e})\diverges$, also a contradiction.
Either way, assuming that~($*$) can be 
computed relative to a $K$~oracle gives an impossibility.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
Any set~$S$ is reducible to its relativized \HP{}, $S\leq_T K^S\!$.
\end{theorem}

% This proof is an adaptation of the reducibility ones
% that we saw earlier in this chapter.

\begin{proof}
On the left below is an oracle machine that is intuitively
mechanically computable.
So Church's Thesis gives that it has an index:~it is 
$\TM_{e_0}^X$ for some~$e_0$.
Use the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, giving the
uniformly computable family of
machines $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}^X$ charted on the right.
\begin{center}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts04.pdf}}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts05.pdf}}
\end{center}
The machine $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}^X$ halts for any input~$y$ 
if and only if $x$ is a member of the oracle.
% With an $S$ oracle, $\TM_{s(e_0,x)}^S$ halts for any input~$y$ 
% if and only if $x\in S$.
Taking the oracle to be~$S$ and $y$ to be $s(e_0,x)$ gives:~$x\in S$ 
if and only if $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}^S(s(e_0,x))\converges$,
which in turn holds if and only if $s(e_0,x)\in \smash{K^S}\!$.
So if $\smash{K^S}$ is the oracle for the following machine
\begin{center}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts25.pdf}}
\end{center}
then it acts as the characteristic function of~$S$.
Thus $S\leq_T K^S$.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}
$K\leq_T K^K$, but $K^K\not\leq_T K$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This follows from the prior result, and the one before that.
\end{proof}

The start of this section asks whether there are 
any problems harder than the \HP.
We now have the answer:~one problem strictly harder than computing
the characteristic function of~$K$
is to compute the characteristic function
of~$K^K\!$.



\begin{exercises}[Recall from page~\pageref{def:ComputableFcn} that a
  Turing machine is a decider for a set if it computes the characteristic
  function of that set.]

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
\begin{credit}
  \url{http://people.cs.aau.dk/~srba/courses/tutorials-CC-10/t5-sol.pdf}
\end{credit}
Suppose that the set $A$ is Turing-reducible to the set~$B$. 
Which of these are true?
\begin{exparts}
\item A decider for $A$ can be used to decide~$B$.
\item If $A$ is computable then~$B$ is computable also.
\item If $A$ is uncomputable then $B$ is uncomputable too.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is wrong.
  For instance, the empty set is reducible to the \HP{} set, 
  $\emptyset\leq_T K$, but while a decider for 
  $\emptyset$ is trivial, there is no computable decider for~$K$.

  The correct statement is the opposite:~if $A\leq_T B$ then 
  a decider for~$B$ can be used to decide the set~$A$.
  \item This is wrong. 
  As in the prior item, if $A$ is the empty set then it is computable.
  Then $A\leq_TB$ where $B$ equals the \HP{} set~$K$,
  but $B$ is not decidable.
  The other way around is right:~if $B$ is decidable then $A$ is decidable also.
  \item True.
  If $A\leq_T B$ then $\TMfcn_e^B=\charfcn{A}$ for some index~$e\in\N$.
  If we could compute~$B$ then we could use that ability to compute~$A$.
  So if~$A$ is uncomputable then~$B$ is also uncomputable.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Both oracles and deciders take in a number and return $0$ or~$1$, 
giving whether that number is in the set.
What's the difference?
\begin{answer}\verified
  % A decider is computable.
  % An oracle, typically, is not.
  A decider is a Turing machine 
  (whose computed function is the characteristic function of some set).
  An oracle is an addition to a Turing machine, a set that the machine 
  can query.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/40605/67754}{SE user Karolis Juodel\.{e}}
\end{credit}
Your friend says, ``Oracle machines are not real, so why talk about them?''
What do you say?
\begin{answer}\verified
  First of all, they are fascinating and fun.
  Is that not enough?

  Beyond that, they help us understand the relationships among problems.
  In the Complexity chapter we will expand on the idea to help understand
  how many everyday, practical, problems are related.
  This will include trying to understand which are easy and which are
  hard. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your classmate says they answered a quiz question to define an oracle with,
``A set to solve unsolvable problems.''
Give them a gentle critique.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Their answer captures the spirit of the reason for considering oracle 
  computations.
  But it is lacking in two ways.
  The first is a technicality:~there are oracles such as the
  empty set, where computations relative to that oracle can only solve 
  problems that you could solve already.

  The second lack is that their answer doesn't refer to the mechanism at all.
  That is, the oracle set doesn't solve the problems.
  We might informally speak of an oracle as solving problems but for a 
  definition we should say that
  we give a machine access to answers about membership in the set and then
  the input-output behavior of the machine is the solution to those problems. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there an oracle for every problem?
For every problem is there an oracle?
\begin{answer}\verified
  We take the first question to ask:~is there a most-powerful oracle, 
  one that solves every problem?
  The answer is no.
  Given an oracle~$X\subseteq \N$, the problem of deciding membership
  in $K^X\!$, the \HP{} in that oracle, is not solvable from~$X$.

  The second question asks whether, given a set~$S\subseteq N$,
  there is an oracle that suffices to determine membership in~$S$. 
  The answer is yes;~the oracle~$S$ will do.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/96806/67754}{SE user Noah Schweber}
\end{credit}
A person in your class asks, ``Oracles can solve unsolvable problems, right?
And $K^K$ is unsolvable.
So an oracle like the~$K$ oracle should solve it.''
Help your prof out here; suggest a response.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each oracle can only compute answers to some problems.
  (Countably many of them.)
  But for each oracle there are problems that it cannot solve. 
  (Because there are uncountably many subsets of~$\N$,
  uncountably many problems.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your study partner confesses, ``I don't understand relative computation.
Any halting computation must take only finitely many steps.
So if there are 
oracle calls there are only finitely many of them.
But a finite oracle is computable, and so by
\cref{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing} it is reducible to any set.''
Give them a prompt.
\begin{answer}\verified
  In an oracle computation that halts, there are indeed only finitely many
  calls to the oracle.
  But the calls when the computation's input is~$0$ 
  may well differ from the calls when
  the computation's input is~$1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Where $B\subseteq\N$ is a set, let $2B=\set{2b\suchthat b\in B}$.
We will show that $B\equiv_T 2B$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Give a flowchart sketching a machine that, given access to oracle~$2B$,
will act as the characteristic function of~$B$.
That is, this machine witnesses that $B\leq_T 2B$.
\item
Sketch a machine that, given access to oracle~$B$,
will act as the characteristic function of~$2B$.
This machine witnesses that $2B\leq_T B$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
See \figureref{fig:BIsEquivToTwoB}.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1045.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1044.pdf}}
  \caption{Showing that $B\leq_T 2B$ and $2B\leq_T B$.}
  \label{fig:BIsEquivToTwoB}
\end{figure}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The flowchart on the left 
is straightforward.
\item 
For the flowchart on the right, notice that is $x$ is odd then $x/2$ is a 
natural number.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We can use oracles for things other than determining the characteristic
functions of sets.
Sketch a machine that, with access to the oracle
$P=\set{p\in \N\suchthat \text{$p$ is prime}}$,
will input a number and print out a list of the primes
dividing that number.
\begin{answer}
See \figureref{fig:OracleSetOfPrimes}.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1046.pdf}}
  \caption{Oracle machine that can use the set of primes to find prime in the input.}
  \label{fig:OracleSetOfPrimes}
\end{figure}
\end{answer}

\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The set 
$S=\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$ and $\TMfcn_e(4)\converges$}}$
is not computable.
Sketch how to compute it using a $K$ oracle.
That is, sketch an oracle machine that shows $S\leq_T K$.
\hint~as in 
\cref{ex:KLeqTHaltsOnThree}, you can 
use the \smn{} theorem to produce a family of machines 
where the $x$-th member halts on all inputs if and only if $x\in S$. 
\begin{answer}
Mimicking \cref{ex:KLeqTHaltsOnThree}, we go in two steps.
First, on the left of \figureref{fig:HaltsOnThreeKOracle} 
is a flowchart sketching a program.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
Let the index of that machine be~$e$.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1047.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1048.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
  \caption{The set $\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(3)\converges$ and $\TMfcn_x(4)\converges$}}$ reduces to~$K$.}
  \label{fig:HaltsOnThreeKOracle}
\end{figure}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a family of machines parametrized by~$x$.
The $x$-th member of that family is shown in the middle.
Clearly it halts for all input if and only if $\TM_x$ halts on inputs~$3$
and~$4$.
Thus, $\TM_x$ halts on inputs~$3$ and~$4$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in K$. 
Restated, there is a computable function, namely $x\mapsto s(e,x)$, so that
$x\in\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_x(3)\converges}$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in K$,
and therefore $\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_x(3)\converges}\leq_T K$.

The second step uses that computable function $x\mapsto s(e,x)$ for the
oracle machine.
That machine is on the right of the figure.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For the set $S=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$, 
show that $S\leq_T K_0$.
\begin{answer}
By definition, 
$K_0=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \TMfcn_e(x)\converges}$.
So $e\in S$ if and only if $\sequence{e,3}\in K_0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that $K\leq_T \set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all input $y$}}$.
\hint~one way is to use the \smn{} theorem to produce a family of machines 
where the $x$-th member halts on all inputs if and only if it
is a doubler. 
\begin{answer}\verified
Let $D=\set{x\suchthat  \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all inputs~$y$}}$.
  Consider this function.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      2y         &\case{if $\TM_x$ halts on input~$x$}  \\
      \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1051.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1052.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
    \caption{Showing  that $K\leq_T \set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all input $y$}}$.}
    \label{fig:MachinesDoubler}
  \end{figure}%
  We can produce this behavior with 
  a program that takes two inputs, $x,y\in\N$, simulates
  running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$, prints~$2y$, and then stops.
  See the flowchart on the left of \figureref{fig:MachinesDoubler}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine, with 
  an index~$e$, so that $\TMfcn_e=\psi$.

  Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ that
  take in one input~$y$ and output~$2y$ if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$, or
  diverges otherwise.
  These are sketched in the middle of the figure.
  Then $x\in K$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in D$.
  On the right of the figure is an oracle machine giving that
  $K\leq_T D$, as required.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the set 
$\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(j)=7$ for some $j$}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that it is not computable using Rice's theorem.
\item Sketch how to compute it using a $K$ oracle.
\hint~one way is to use the \smn{} theorem to produce a family of machines 
where the $x$-th member halts on all inputs if and only if machine~$\TM_x$ 
outputs~$7$ on some input. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Let 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(j)=7$ for some $j$}}$.
This set is not empty because one element is an index of 
a Turing machine that outputs~$7$ for all inputs.
This set is not all of~$\N$ because one non-element is an index of a
Turing machine that fails to halt on all inputs.

To see that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$
and that $\hat{e}$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$, there is an input~$j$ so that $\TMfcn_e(j)=7$.
Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, for the same~$j$ we
have $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(j)=7$, and therefore $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
\item
As in \cref{ex:KLeqTHaltsOnThree}, we go in two steps.
The first is that on the left of \figureref{fig:EverOutputsSevenOracle} 
is a flowchart sketching a program.
The second chart expands on the nested loop involved in `dovetail'.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes this flowchart.
Let the index of that machine be~$e$.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1035.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1037.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1036.pdf}}
  \caption{Computing $\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(j)=7$ for some $j$}}$ with a $K$ oracle.}
  \label{fig:EverOutputsSevenOracle}
\end{figure}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a family of machines parametrized by~$x$,
shown in the third flowchart.
Clearly it halts for all inputs~$y$ if and only if $\TM_x$ outputs a~$7$
for any input.
Thus, $\TM_x$ ever outputs a~$7$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in K$.
That means there is a computable function, $x\mapsto s(e,x)$,
where  $x\in\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(j)=7$ for some $j$}}$
if and only if $s(e,x)\in K$.

The second step is to use the computable function $x\mapsto s(e,x)$
to get the oracle machine in \figureref{fig:EverOutputsSevenOracleII}.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
  \caption{Oracle machine for computing $\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(j)=7$ for some $j$}}$ from $K$.}
  \label{fig:EverOutputsSevenOracleII}
\end{figure}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let
$S=\set{x\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(3)\converges$ and $\TMfcn_{2x}(3)\converges$ and $\TMfcn_x(3)=\TMfcn_{2x}(3)$}}$.
Show $S\leq_T K$ by producing a way to answer questions about 
membership in~$S$ from a $K$ oracle.
\hint~one way is to apply the \smn{} theorem
to produce a family of machines whose $x$-th member halts on all inputs
if and only if $x\in S$.
\begin{answer}
On the left of \figureref{fig:PeAndPTwoEGiveSameOnThree} 
is a sketch of a program.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with the same behavior.
Let the index of that machine be~$e$.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1038.pdf}}
  \quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1039.pdf}}
  \quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
  \caption{The set $\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_x(3)\converges=\TMfcn_{2x}(3)\converges}$ is $T$-equivalent to $K$.}
  \label{fig:PeAndPTwoEGiveSameOnThree}
\end{figure}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a family of machines parametrized by~$x$, 
whose $x$-th member, $\TM_{s(e,x)}$, is shown in the middle.
Clearly it halts for all inputs~$y$ if and only if $x\in S$.
In particular, it halts for $y=s(e,x)$ if and only if $x\in S$.
Thus, we have a computable function $x\mapsto s(e,x)$ so that
$x\in S$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in K$.
With that, the oracle machine on the right gives that 
$S\leq_T K$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recall that a computable function~$\TMfcn$ is 
total\index{total function}\index{function!total} 
if $\TMfcn(y)\converges$ for all $y\in\N$.
The set of total functions is 
$\text{Tot}$.\index{Tot@$\text{Tot}$, set of total computable functions}
Show that $K\leq_T\text{Tot}$.
\begin{answer}
Consider the routine sketched on the left of \figureref{fig:KLeqTot}.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
Let its index be~$e$.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1040.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1041.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts22.pdf}}
  \caption{Computing $K$ with a $\text{Tot}$ oracle.}
  \label{fig:KLeqTot}
\end{figure}
Apply the \smn theorem to get, as shown
in the middle, the machine $\TM_{s(e,x)}$ that computes the partial 
function $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.
This shows that there is a computable function, 
namely $x\mapsto s(e,x)$, so that 
$k\in K$ if and only if $s(e,k)\in\text{Tot}$.
On the right is an oracle machine using that computable function to 
show that $K\leq \text{Tot}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
