A computable partial function~$\TMfcn_x$ is 
\definend{extensible}\index{extensible}\index{function!extensible}
if there is a computable total function~$\TMfcn$ where
whenever $\TMfcn_x(y)\converges$ then the two agree,
$\TMfcn_x(y)=\TMfcn(y)$.
The set of extensible functions is $\text{Ext}$.\index{Ext@$\text{Ext}$, extensible functions}
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that this function is not a member of 
$\text{Ext}$:~if $x\in K$ then $\operatorname{steps}(x)$ 
is the smallest step number~$s$
where $\TM_x$ halts on input~$x$ by step~$s$, 
and $\operatorname{steps}(x)\diverges$ otherwise.
\item
Prove that $K\leq_T\text{Ext}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item If this function had a
computable extension~$g$ then we could solve the \HP,
by running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$ for $g(x)$-many steps.
If it hasn't halted by then, it will never halt.
\item
Consider the routine sketched on the left of \figureref{fig:ExtLeqTot}.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
Let its index be~$e$.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1042.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1043.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1049.pdf}}
  \caption{Computing $K$ with a $\text{Ext}$ oracle.}
  \label{fig:ExtLeqTot}
\end{figure}
In the middle is $\TM_{s(e,x)}$, computing the partial 
function $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.
If $x\notin K$ then
$\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)$ is undefined for all inputs~$y$, and 
this function is a member of $\text{Ext}$, since for instance it
is extended by the function that always returns~$0$, which is
obviously computable.
If $x\in K$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=\operatorname{steps}(y)$ for all
inputs~$y$, and by the first item this is not a member of~$\text{Ext}$. 
This gives a computable function, namely $x\mapsto s(e,x)$, so that, 
by the first part of this question,
$k\in K$ if and only if $s(e,k)\notin\text{Ext}$.
The oracle machine on the right shows $K\leq \text{Ext}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A$ and~$B$ be sets.
Show that if $A(q)=B(q)$ for all $q\in\N$ used in the oracle computation
$\TMfcn^A(x)$
then $\TMfcn^A(x)=\TMfcn^B(x)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
The computation will proceed in exactly the same way, so it will come
to the same result.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that $A\leq_T \setcomp{A}$ for all $A\subseteq\N$.
\begin{answer}\verified
See \figureref{fig:ALeqAComp}.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1050.pdf}}
  \caption{Computing $A$ from an $\setcomp{A}$ oracle.}
  \label{fig:ALeqAComp}
\end{figure}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that $K\nleq_T\emptyset$.
\begin{answer}\verified
If $K\leq_T\emptyset$ were true then
there would be an oracle machine whose computed function~$\TMfcn^\emptyset$
is the characteristic function of~$K$.
But because membership in~$\emptyset$ is itself computable we could 
replace oracle calls with the computation itself, so that any 
time the machine asks if $x$ is an element of the oracle, we replace
that with `no'.
That mechanically computes~$K$, which is impossible.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Assume $A\leq_T B$ and suppose that an outline of the 
oracle computation looks like this.
(This is not the general case.
The actual machine might more than one test.
Or, it might write a number of \str{1}'s and them conditionally erase
all of them, or all but one of them.)
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1053.pdf}
\end{center}
Decide whether each is
True or False, and 
briefly explain. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\setcomp{A}\leq_TB$
\item $A\leq_T\setcomp{B}$
\item $\setcomp{A}\leq_T\setcomp{B}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
Refer to \figureref{fig:ALeqBImplies}.
The flowchart give in the question statement is on the left.
\begin{figure}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1053.pdf}}
  \quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1054.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1055.pdf}}
  \caption{Oracle machines for what $A\leq_T B$ implies.}
  \label{fig:ALeqBImplies}
\end{figure}
\begin{exparts}
\item True.
This is shown in the flowchart in the middle.
Basically, interchange the outputs of \str{1} and~\str{0}.
\item True.
This is shown in the right-hand flowchart.
It is the same as the left-hand one but it tests whether $f(x)$ is not 
a member of the oracle.
\item True.
This is the left-hand flowchart again.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is the number of oracles countable or uncountable?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Any subset of~$\N$ can be an oracle, so there are uncountably many oracles. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A$ and~$B$ be sets.
Produce a set~$C$ so that $A\leq_T C$ and $B\leq_T C$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  One is 
  $C=\set{\cantor(0,a)\suchthat a\in A}
      \cup\set{\cantor(1,b)\suchthat b\in B}$.
  Clearly from this set we can answer questions about membership in 
  each of~$A$ and~$B$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix an oracle.
Prove that the collection of sets computable from that oracle is countable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  There are only countably many programs that can involve an
  \codeinline{oracle} call.
  That is, there are only countably many oracle-enhanced Turing machines.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The relation $\leq_T$ involves sets, so we naturally ask how it interacts
with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Does $A\subseteq B$ imply $A\leq_T B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T A\cup B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T A\cap B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T \setcomp{A}$?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set~$K$ is a subset of the natural numbers~$\N$
    but $K\nleq_T\N$, because the \HP{} is unsolvable.
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set $K$ is not Turing reducible to~$\N$, 
    but $\N=K\cup\setcomp{K}$.
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set $K$ is not Turing reducible to~$\emptyset$, 
    but $\emptyset=K\cap\setcomp{K}$.
  \item Yes.
    We can write this oracle-using program:~read the input~$x$
    and if \codeinline{oracle(x)} then output~$0$, otherwise
    output~$1$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A\subseteq\N$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Define when a set is \definend{computably enumerable in an oracle}.\index{computably enumerable!in an oracle}\index{oracle!computably enumerable in}
\item
Show that $\N$ is computably enumerable in $A$ for all sets~$A$.
\item
Show that $K^A$ is computably enumerable in~$A$.
% \item
% Show that the relation $B\sim A$ if $B$ is computably enumerable in~$A$
% is not transtive.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The natural definition is that a set~$B$ is 
    \definend{computably enumerable} in the set~$A$ if $B$~is the 
    range of a total $A$-computable function, or if $B$ is empty.
    That is, $B$ is c.e.\@ in $A$ if
    $B=\set{\TMfcn_e^A(x)\suchthat x\in\N}$ for some index~$e$ where the
    function is total, or if $B=\emptyset$.
  \item As in \cref{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing}, we can output the 
    elements of~$\N$ without even referring to the oracle.
    The function $\TMfcn^A(x)=x$ is computable, and does not need to 
    ever refer to the oracle.
  \item Dovetail.
    First run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for one step.
    Then run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for a second step,
    and the computation of $\TM_1^A$ on input~$1$ for one step.
    Next run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for its third step,
    the computation of $\TM_1^A$ on input~$1$ for its second step,
    and the computation of $\TM_2^A$ on input~$2$ for one step.
    As computations halt, enumerate the index into $K^A$.
  % \item
  % Hint: use $\emptyset$, $K$, and $\setcomp{K}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% Instead, define re in A, and prove that if S is re in A and S comp
% is re in A then S is recursive in A?

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that
%   $\text{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y$}}$
% is $T$-equivalent to $K^K$.
% \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{oracle|)}\index{set!oracle|)}




% =====================================================
\section{Fixed point theorem}\index{Fixed point theorem|(}

Recall our first example of diagonalization,\index{diagonalization} 
the proof that the
set of real numbers is not countable, on page~\pageref{th:RealsNotCountable}.
%<*discussion:RecallCantorThm>
We assumed that there is an $\map{f}{\N}{\R}$
and considered its inputs and outputs, as illustrated in this table.
\begin{equation*} \setlength{\arraycolsep}{0.75\arraycolsep}
  \begin{array}{c|r@{\hspace*{.35em}.\hspace*{.35em}}ccccccc@{\hspace*{1em}\ldots\;}}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\text{\small $n$\hspace*{0.25em}}} 
    &\multicolumn{8}{c}{\text{\hspace*{-1.00em}\small\tabulartext{$f(n)$'s decimal expansion}}}  \\ 
   \cline{1-9}
   \rule{0em}{2.15ex}% bit more vertical room 
   0  &42  &3  &1  &2  &7  &7  &0  &4  \\  
   1  &2   &0  &1  &0  &0  &0  &0  &0  \\  
   2  &1   &4  &1  &4  &1  &5  &9  &2  \\  
   3  &-20  &9  &1  &9  &5  &9  &1  &9  \\  
   % 4  &  0 &1  &0  &1  &0  &0  &1  &0  \\
   % 5  &-0  &6   &2  &5  &5  &4  &1  &8  \\[-.5ex]
   \multicolumn{1}{c}{\vdotswithin{$0$}} 
    &\multicolumn{4}{c}{\vdotswithin{$1$}}  
  \end{array}
\end{equation*}
Let row~$n$'s decimal representation be 
$d_n=\hat{d}_n.d_{n,0}\mskip 1mu d_{n,1}\mskip 1mu d_{n,2}\ldots$\sentencespace % 
Go down the diagonal to the right of the decimal point to get 
the sequence of digits $d_{0,0}, d_{1,1}, d_{2,2}, \ldots$,
which above is $3,1,4,5,\ldots$.
Using that, 
construct a number~$z=0.z_0\mskip 1mu z_1\mskip 1mu z_2\ldots$
by making its $n$-th decimal place be something other than~$d_{n,n}$.
In our earlier example we took a digit transformation~$t$
given by~$t(d_{n,n})=2$ if $d_{n,n}=1$, and $t(d_{n,n})=1$
otherwise, so that the table above yields $z=0.1211\ldots$\sentencespace
Then the diagonalization argument culminates in verifying that~$z$ 
is not any of the rows. 
%</discussion:RecallCantorThm>



\subsection{When diagonalization fails}\index{diagonalization}
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFails>
What if the transformation is such that the diagonal is a row, 
that $z=f(n_0)$? 
Then the array member where the diagonal crosses that row is
unchanged by the transformation, 
$d_{n_0,n_0}=t(d_{n_0,n_0})$.
Conclusion:~if diagonalization fails then the transformation has
a fixed point.
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFails>

%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsi>
We will apply this to sequences of
computable functions, 
$\TMfcn_{i_0},\TMfcn_{i_1},\TMfcn_{i_2} \ldots$\sentencespace
We are interested in effectiveness so we don't consider
arbitrary sequences but instead take the indices 
$i_0,i_1,i_2\ldots$ to be computable, meaning that for some~$e$ we have
$i_0=\TMfcn_e(0)$, $i_1=\TMfcn_e(1)$ ,$i_2=\TMfcn_e(2)$, etc.
In short, a sequence of computable functions has this form.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{\phi_e(0)},\,\TMfcn_{\phi_e(1)},\,\TMfcn_{\phi_e(2)}\,\ldots
\end{equation*}
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsi>
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsii>
Below is a table with all such sequences,
all effective sequences of effective functions.
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsii>
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsiii>
\begin{center}\small \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
  \begin{tabular}{rr|ccccc}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &\multicolumn{4}{c}{\tabulartext{Sequence term}}  \\
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
      &$n=0$ &$n=1$ &$n=2$ &$n=3$ &\ldots \\  \cline{3-7}
   \multirow{5}{*}{\tabulartext{Sequence}}
    &$e=0$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=1$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=2$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=3$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(3)}$  &    \\[-.5ex]
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{$e=3$}]{\vdots}}$}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{=}]{\vdots}}$}
      &$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{=}]{\vdots}}$
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsiii>
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsiv>
Each entry $\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(n)}$ is a computable function.
If the index $\TMfcn_e(n)$ diverges then the function as whole diverges. 
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsiv>

%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsv>
The natural transformation is this, where $f$ is a computable function. 
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_x\mapsunder{t_f}\TMfcn_{f(x)}
\end{equation*}
We next argue that under this transformation,
diagonalization fails.
% (because the transformed diagonal is
% a computable sequence of computable functions)
Thus, the transformation~$t_f$ has a fixed point.
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsv>

\begin{theorem}[Fixed Point Theorem, Kleene 1938]\hspace*{-0.5em}\footnotemark[1]  \label{th:FixedPointThm}
%<*th:FixedPointThm>
\index{Fixed point theorem}\index{Kleene's fixed point theorem}\index{Recursion theorem}
\hspace*{-0.5em}For any total computable function~$f$
there is a number~$k$ such that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}$.
%</th:FixedPointThm>
\end{theorem}\footnotetext[1]{%
  This is also known as the Recursion Theorem
  but there is another widely used result of that name.
  This name is more descriptive for this result.}
\begin{proof}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmi>
The flowchart on the left below sketches a function
$f(n,x)=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_n(n)}(x)$.
Church's Thesis says that some Turing machine computes this function; let that
machine have index~$e_0$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$n$, giving the right chart, 
which describes a family of 
machines.
The $n$-th member of that family, $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,n)}$,
computes the $n$-th function on the diagonal of the above array,
$\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_0(0)},\,\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_1(1)},\,\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_2(2)}\,\ldots$\sentencespace
%</pf:FixedPointThmi>
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts06.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts07.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \TMfcn_{s(e_0,n)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{\TMfcn_n(n)}(x)  &\case{if $\TMfcn_n(n)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow                &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmii>
The index~$e_0$ is fixed, so $s(e_0,n)$ is a function of one variable.
Let $g(n)=s(e_0,n)$, so that the diagonal functions are 
$\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_0(0)}=\TMfcn_{g(0)}$, $\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_1(1)}=\TMfcn_{g(1)}$, \ldots\sentencespace
The function $g$ is computable and total, because $s$ is computable and total.

Under~$t_f$ those functions are transformed to
% $\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_0(0)},\,\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_1(1)},\,\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_2(2)},\>\ldots$\sentencespace
$\TMfcn_{f\!g(0)},\,\TMfcn_{f\!g(1)},\,\TMfcn_{f\!g(2)},\>\ldots$\sentencespace
The composition $\composed{f}{g}$ is computable and total since
$f$ is specified as total.
%</pf:FixedPointThmii>
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{fg(n)}\,(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_n(n)}(x)   &\case{if $\TMfcn_n(n)\converges$}     \\
    \uparrow                 &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts08.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmiii>%
As the flowchart underlines, 
$\TMfcn_{f\!g(0)},\,\TMfcn_{f\!g(1)},\,\TMfcn_{f\!g(2)},\>\ldots$
is a computable sequence of computable functions.
Hence it is one of the table's rows.
Let it be row~$v$, making $\TMfcn_{f\!g(m)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_{v}(m)}$ for all~$m$.
Consider where the diagonal sequence $\TMfcn_{g(n)}$ intersects that row:
% \begin{equation*}
  $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}=\TMfcn_{f\!g(v)}$.
% \end{equation*}
The fixed point for $f$ is $k=g(v)$.
%</pf:FixedPointThmiii>
\end{proof}

The Fixed Point Theorem says that when we try to diagonalize out of the 
partial computable functions, diagonalization 
fails.
That is, 
\citetext{the notion of partial computable function seems to have an
in-built defense against diagonalization}{%
  \autocite{Odifreddi}, p~152.}.

The Fixed Point Theorem
applies to any total computable function.
Consequently, it leads to many surprising
results. 


%<*cor:SuccessorFcnSame>
\begin{corollary}
There is an index~$e$ so that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e+1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The function~$f(x)=x+1$ is computable and total.
So there is an $e\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_{e}=\TMfcn_{f(e)}$.
\end{proof}
%</cor:SuccessorFcnSame>

%<*cor:WeEqualsSete>
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN}
There is an index~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ halts only on $e$.
\end{corollary}
%</cor:WeEqualsSete>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:WeEqualsSetei>
Consider the program described by the flowchart on the left below.
By Church's Thesis it can be done with a Turing machine,
$\TM_{e_0}$.
Parametrize to get the program on the right,
$\TM_{s(e_0,m)}$.
%</pf:WeEqualsSetei>
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts09.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts10.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \TMfcn_{s(e_o,m)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
  42         &\case{if $x=m$}  \\
  \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}%
%<*pf:WeEqualsSeteii>
Since $e_0$ is fixed (it is the index of the machine on the left),
$s(e_0,x)$ is a total computable function of one variable,
$f(m)=s(e_0,m)$, where
the associated Turing machine halts only on input~$m$.
That function has a fixed point, $\TMfcn_{f(e)}=\TMfcn_e$,
and the associated Turing machine $\TM_e$ halts only on~$e$.
%</pf:WeEqualsSeteii>
\end{proof}

This says that there is a Turing machine that halts
only on one input, its index.
Rephrased for rhetorical effect, 
\citetext{this machine's name is its behavior}{%
  \definend{Nominative determinism}
  is the theory that a person's name has some influence over what they do
  with their life.
  Examples are: the sprinter Usain Bolt,
  the US weatherman Storm Fields,
  the baseball player Prince Fielder.
  and the Lord Chief Justice of England and Wales named Igor Judge,
  I~Judge.
  See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominative_determinism}.}.\footnote{%
  The use of `name' for `index'  
  is meant to be evocative.}

\begin{corollary}
There is an $m\in\N$ such that  
$\TMfcn_m(x)=m$ for all inputs~$x$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Consider the function $\psi(x,y)=x$.
As the flowchart on the left below shows, it is computable.\footnote{% 
  The flowchart is overkill here since the function 
  is obviously computable.
  But when it is not obvious, as in
  some exercises, we need an outline of how to compute the function.}
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
Let that machine have index~$e_0$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_{e_0}(x,y)=x$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts17.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2.5em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts18.pdf}}
\end{center}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a
uniformly computable family of functions parametrized by~$x$,
given by $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=x$.
Define $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ by $f(x)=s(e_0,x)$.
Because~$e_0$ is fixed as it is the number of the Turing machine that 
computes $\psi$, this is a total function of one variable.
The Fixed Point Theorem says that there is an~$m\in\N$ with 
$\TMfcn_m(y)=\TMfcn_{f(m)}(y)=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,m)}(y)=m$ for all~$y$.
\end{proof}

% We close with an intriguing result.

% \begin{corollary}
% There is a program that exactly outputs its own source code; there is a
% Turing machine that outputs itself.
% \end{corollary}

% \begin{proof}
% Define a rewriter function that outputs its input;
% call it~$\operatorname{echo}$.
% If given the input of the source code of a Turing machine $\TM$ then
% its output is the source of $\TM$.
% By the Fixed Point Theorem, there is some Turing machine
% $\TM$ such that $\TM$ has the same behavior as $\operatorname{echo}(\TM)$.
% But that behavior is to output the source code of $\TM$.
% So this machine outputs its own source.
% \end{proof}

% The prior result gives a Turing machine that prints its own index.
% Since the index is source-equivalent, it raises the question of whether there
% is a program that self-reproduces, that prints its own source.
% The answer is that there is such a program; see \cref{sec:SelfReproduction}.

% \begin{remark}
This machine's behavior is the same as its index.
Imagine finding that 
the machine $\TM_7$ outputs~$7$ on all inputs.
This may seem to be an accident of our choice of scheme for numbering machines.
But the corollary says that such a thing must happen
for any acceptable machine numbering.
Further, since a Turing machine's index is source-equivalent, 
this result suggests that there
is a program that prints its own source, that self-reproduces.
See  \cref{sec:SelfReproduction}.
%\end{remark}

% The prior result gives a Turing machine that prints its own index.
% Since the index is source-equivalent, it raises the question of whether there
% is a program that self-reproduces, that prints its own source.
% The answer is that there is such a program; see \cref{sec:SelfReproduction}.





% ...........
\subsection{Discussion}\index{Fixed point theorem!discussion|(}
The Fixed Point Theorem and its proof are often 
\citetext{considered mysterious, or at any rate obscure}{%
  For example, ``The recursion theorem \ldots{} has one of the most
  unintuitive proofs where I cannot explain why it works, only that it
  does.'' \autocite{blog:ComputationalComplexityRecursionThm}}.
Here we will develop a point about names.

Compare the sentence \textit{Atlantis is a mythical city}  
with
\textit{There are  two t's in `Atlantis'}.
In the first we say that `Atlantis' is
\definend{used} because it has a value, it names something. 
In the second `Atlantis' is not referring to something\Dash its
value is itself\Dash so 
\citetext{we say that it is \definend{mentioned}}{%
  We can have a lot of fun with the use-mention distinction.
  One example is the old wisecrack that answers the statement,
  ``Nothing rhymes with orange'' with ``No it doesn't,''
  that turns on the distinction between nothing and `nothing'. 
  Another example is the conundrum that 
  we all agree that $1/2=3/6$, 
  but one of them involves a~$3$ and the other does not\Dash
  how can different things be equal?
  The resolution is that the assertion that they are equal
  refers to the number that they represent, not to the representation itself.
  That is, in mention `$1/2$' and `$3/6$' are different strings
  but in use, they point to the same number.}.\footnote{%
  We see this distinction in programming books.
  In the sentence, ``The number of players is \texttt{players}''
  the first `players' refers to people while the second is a program 
  variable.
  The typewriter font helps with the distinction.
  Similarly in this book we use $a$ for variables, which have a value,
  and \protect\str{a} for characters, which are a value.}
  % We have done something like the same thing with
  % the paragraph's single-quoted ``\,`Atlantis'\,'' mentions. 
  % (This is quoted because it mentions a mention.)}
% This difference is the reason for the second sentence's
% quotation marks.
This is
the \definend{use-mention distinction};\index{use-mention distinction}
we are using `Atlantis' on two different levels.

\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \relax
}{%
\margingraphic{0.29\textwidth}{background/pix/xkcdpointers.png}{\label{fig:xkcdpointers}\href{https://xkcd.com/138/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
}
A version of this happens in computer programming.
In the C language program below 
the second line's 
asterisk means that \codeinline{x} and~\codeinline{y} are 
pointers.\index{pointer, in C}
The four vertical arrays show a machine's memory cells over time.
They imagine
the compiler associates~\codeinline{x}
with register~$123$ and~\codeinline{y} with~$124$.
The first has that cell~$123$ holds the number~$901$
and cell~$124$ is~$902$.
% We say that \codeinline{x} points to $901$ and that \codeinline{y} 
% points to~$902$.

% When a compiler associates a variable with a cell, the variable becomes
% a name for that cell. 
Variables such as \codeinline{x} and \codeinline{y} are 
names for their cells.
If there were an ordinary 
variable~\codeinline{a} (not a pointer) that the compiler associated with
cell~$122$ then the statement \codeinline{a = 5} would result in memory 
location~$122$ receiving a value of~$5$.
But because these variables are pointers,
we have declared interest 
in the contents of the memory cells that they point to: cell~$123$ 
is itself a name for location~$901$,
and $124$ names~$902$.
The second vertical array illustrates, showing the effect of
running the \codeinline{*x = 42} statement. 
The system 
does not put $42$ into $123$, 
rather it
puts $42$ into $901$.
% \footnote{%
%   Using the \,\codeinline{*} operator to access the value stored at a pointer
%   is called dereferencing that pointer.
%   % There is a matching referencing operator, \codeinline{&},
%   % that gives the address of an existing variable.
%   % Both allow a programmer to control the indirection that pointers provide.
%   }
Next, with \codeinline{y = x} the system sets the cell named by \codeinline{y} 
to point to the same address as~\codeinline{x}'s cell, address~$901$.
Finally, the last line puts $13$ where \codeinline{y} points, 
which is at this moment
the same cell to which~\codeinline{x} points.
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Pointers in a C program.}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/memory/memory04}
  \end{fig}
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Pointers in a C program.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[poster=last]{1}{background/asy/memory/memory}{00}{04}}
  \end{animatedfigure}
}

The \codeinline{x} and~\codeinline{y} variables are being used
at two different levels of meaning.
On one level,
\codeinline{x} refers to the contents of register $123$, so it names~$123$.
But the other level is that the system is set up to refer to
the contents of the contents, that is,
to what's in address~$901$.
On this level, \codeinline{x} and~\codeinline{y} are names for names.

As to the role played by names in the
Fixed Point Theorem, 
the proof takes 
$g(n)$ to be this.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{g(n)}(x)=
  \TMfcn_{s(e_0,n)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{\TMfcn_n(n)}(x)  &\case{if $\TMfcn_n(n)\converges$}  \\
    \uparrow                &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
It is not the case that 
$g(n)$ must equal $\TMfcn_n(n)$. 
Instead, $g(n)$ is an index of what is sketched in the right-hand of the 
pair of flowcharts in the proof.
It is a name for a procedure that
computes the function above.

Informally, what $g(n)$ names is,
``Given input~$x$, run $\TM_n$ on input~$n$ and if it halts with
output~$w$ then run $\TM_w$ on input~$x$.''
Shorter:~``Produce $\TMfcn_n(n)$ and then do $\TMfcn_n(n)$.''

Next, from~$f$ the proof considers the composition and gives it a name
$\composed{f}{g}=\TMfcn_v$.
Putting $v$ into the prior paragraph has $g(v)$ naming, 
``Compute $\TMfcn_v(v)$ and then do $\TMfcn_v(v)$.''
That's the same as
``Compute $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$ and then do $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$.''

Note the self-reference:~it may at first glance 
appear that to compute $g(v)$ we need $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$,
which involves computing~$g(v)$, and so
the instructions for $g(v)$ paradoxically
contains itself as a subpart.
However, that impression is wrong.
Instead, $g(v)$ first computes the name, the index $s(e_0,v)$
of $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$, and after that
runs the machine numbered $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$.
So $g(v)$ and $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$ are names for machines that
compute the same function.
Thus $g(v)$ does not contain itself\Dash more precisely, 
the set of instructions for
computing $g(v)$ does not contain itself.
Instead, it contains a name for the instructions for computing itself.

In particular, in~($*$) above, regardless of whether 
$\TMfcn_n(n)$ converges we can nonetheless compute the index~$g(n)$
and from it the instructions for the function.
There is an analogy here with `Atlantis'\Dash despite that the referred-to
city doesn't exist we
can still sensibly assert things about its name.
\index{Fixed point theorem!discussion|)}

In summary, the Fixed Point Theorem is deep, 
showing that surprising and interesting behaviors
occur in any sufficiently powerful computation system.


% ................................................
\begin{exercises}
% Exercises from https://ehshive.com/1105/uncategorized/teacher-feature-eric-langevin/?

% \recommended
% \begin{exercise}
% Your friend asks you about the proof of the Fixed Point Theorem, 
% \cref{th:FixedPointThm}.
% ``The last line says $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}$; isn't this just
% saying that  $g(v)=\TMfcn_v(v)$?
% Why the circumlocution?''
% What can you say?
% \begin{answer}\verified
%   Saying that the two are the same function,  
%   that $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}$, is very different than
%   saying that the indices are the same,
%   that  $g(v)=\TMfcn_v(v)$.
%   (That is, we can easily write two different programs to compute the same
%   function.)
%   In particular, the Padding Lemma, \cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}
%   on page~\pageref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices},
%   says that every computable function (including the partial functions) has many
%   indices.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show each.
\begin{exparts*}
\item There is an index~$e$ such that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e+7}$.
\item There is an~$e$ such that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{2e}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to the computable function~$f(x)=x+7$.
  \item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to~$f(x)=2x$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that there must be a Turing machine $\TM_e$ whose input-output behavior
is the same as $\TM_{\hat{e}}$, 
but where all of the digits in the index $\hat{e}$ are one
larger than those in~$e$, except that a $9$ in $e$ changes to a 
$0$ in $\hat{e}$.
For instance we might have $\TMfcn_{35}=\TMfcn_{46}$.
\begin{answer}
Consider the function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ defined by $f(x)=y$ where all of the
digits in $y$ are one greater than the digits in~$x$, 
except that a $9$ in $x$ changes to a $0$ in $y$.
Thus $f(35)=46$ and $f(99)=0$.
Clearly this is a computable function that is total.
Apply the Fixed Point theorem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What conclusion can you draw about acceptable enumerations of Turing machines
by applying the Fixed Point Theorem to each of these?
\begin{exparts*}
\item the tripling function $x\mapsto 3x$
% \item the adds-five function $x\mapsto x+5$
\item the squaring function $x\mapsto x^2$
\item the function that gives $0$ except for~$x=5$,
  when it gives~$1$
\item the constant function $x\mapsto 42$  
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No matter what numbering scheme we use for Turing machines,
  if it is acceptable then there is a Turing machine that computes the
  same function as the machine with three times its index. 
  That is, there exists a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{3k}$.
  % \item No matter what numbering scheme we use,
  % as long as it is acceptable, there is a Turing machine that computes the
  % same function as the machine whose index is five more, 
  % so that there exists
  % $k\in\N$ with $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{k+5}$.
  \item For any acceptable numbering scheme,
  there is a Turing machine that computes the
  same function as the machine whose index is the square.
  That is, there exists a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{k^2}$.
  \item Consider this $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x=5$}  \\
      0  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  For any acceptable numbering for Turing machines
  there will be a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}$.
  \item
  If the function is $f(x)=42$ then the Fixed Point Theorem gives
  that for any acceptable numbering there is a $k\in\N$ so that 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}=\TMfcn_{42}$.
  The number $k=42$ comes to mind, but the Padding Lemma shows that there are
  infinitely many such indices~$k$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
We will prove that there is an~$m$ such that 
$W_m=\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_m(x)\converges}=\set{m^2}$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item You want to show that there is a uniformly computable family of 
  functions like this.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}  
  \end{equation*}
  Define a suitable $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$, argue that it is intuitively
  mechanically computable, and apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the family
  of $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$. 
\item Observe that $e_0$ is fixed so that $s(e_0,x)$ 
  is a function of one variable
  only, and name that function~$\map{g}{\N}{\N}$.
\item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to get the desired~$m$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
  \item
  Consider the function on the left and observe that it is computed 
  by the program sketched on the left of
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsOnlyESquared}.
  Church's Thesis gives there is a Turing machine with this behavior.
  Let that machine's index be~$e_0$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1027.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1028.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts sketching the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsOnlyESquared}
  \end{figure}%
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the family of functions on the right.
  \item  The number~$e_0$ is fixed because it is the index of the Turing machine
  that we chose to compute~$\psi$.
  So, define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$.
  \item 
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives a $m\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,m)}$. 
  Thus $W_m=\set{m^2}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
We will show there is an index~$m$ so that 
$W_m=\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_m(y)\converges}$ is the set consisting
of one element, the $m$-th prime number.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Argue that $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$ such that $p(x)$ is the
  $x$-th prime is computable.
\item Use it and the \smn{} Theorem to get that this family of 
  functions is uniformly computable: $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=42$ if 
  $y=p(x)$ and diverges otherwise.
  % \begin{equation*}
  %     \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
  %   \begin{cases}
  %     42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
  %     \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  %   \end{cases}
  % \end{equation*}
  \item Draw the desired conclusion.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  The function $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$ such that $p(x)$ is the $x$-th prime number 
  is computable by Church's Thesis.
  \item
  We want to get the family of functions on the right side 
  below, computed by the machines sketched on the right of 
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsOnlyEthPrime}, as a uniformly computable
  family.
  By now this is a familiar situation.
  Consider the function on the left, observe that it is computed 
  by the machine sketched on the left of the figure, cite
  Church's Thesis to get that it has an index, and let that index be~$e_0$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1025.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1026.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts outlining the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsOnlyEthPrime}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the uniformly computable 
  family of functions on the right.
  \item
  The number~$e_0$ is fixed so $s(e_0,x)$ is a function only of the variable~$x$.
  To emphasize that, define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$. 
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives a $m\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,m)}$, which halts only when the 
  input is the $m$-th prime.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
Prove that there exists $m\in\N$ such that
$W_m=\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_m(y)\converges}=10^m$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Consider the function on the left.
  By Church's Thesis it is computable and so has an index;~let 
  that index be~$e_0$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=10^x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=10^x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the uniformly computable 
  family of functions on the right.
  The number~$e_0$ is fixed so define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$.
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives $k\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{g(k)}=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,k)}$, 
  and so $W_k=\set{10^x}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show there is an index~$n$ so that 
$W_n=\set{\TMfcn_n(x)\converges}=\set{0,1,\ldots\, n}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We use \cref{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN} as a guide.
  Consider the function on the left below.
  It is computed by the machine sketched on the left in 
  \figureref{fig:WeEqualsZeroOneUpToE}, so Church's Thesis gives that
  there is a Turing machine index for this machine; let it be~$e_0$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\leq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\leq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \tag{*}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1023.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1024.pdf}}
    \caption{A sketch of the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$, and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeEqualsZeroOneUpToE}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn~Theorem to get the 
  family of machines sketched on the right, which compute
  the family of functions given on the right of~($*$).

  Because~$e_0$ is the index of the machine on the left, it is fixed.
  So $s(e_0,x)$ is a function of the single variable~$x$.
  Let $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ be defined by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$.
  By the Fixed Point Theorem \cref{th:FixedPointThm}, the function~$g$ has a
  fixed point so that there is a~$n\in\N$ with
  $\TMfcn_n=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,n)}$.
  Observe that $W_n=\set{0,1,\ldots{} n}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The Fixed Point Theorem says that for all~$f$ (which are computable and total)
there is an~$n$ so that $\TMfcn_n=\TMfcn_{f(n)}$.
What about the statement in which we flip the quantifiers:~for
all $n\in\N$, does 
there exist a total and computable function~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ so that
$\TMfcn_n=\TMfcn_{f(n)}$?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes, we can take $f(x)=x$.
  Or, if we want $f(n)\neq n$ then this is the Padding Lemma.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Let $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ be the function whose effect on Turing machines is
% that $\TM_{f(e)}$ is the same as $\TM_e$ except that the final
% states and non-final states are interchanged.
% Give a fixed point.
% \begin{answer}
% A fixed point is a function, not a machine.
% Consider the function $\TMfcn_{e}$ given by $\TMfcn_{e}(x)\diverges$ for all
% inputs~$x$.
% For any machine associated with that function,
% whether a state is final or not has no effect on the machine's behavior
% because whether a state is final is only relevant when the machine halts.
% So the transformed machine will have the same behavior.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
Prove or disprove the existence of the set. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item $W_m=\set{\TMfcn_m(y)\converges}=\N-\set{m}$
\item $W_m=\set{x\suchthat \text{$\phi_m(x)$ diverges}}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  The function on the left below is computed 
  by the machine sketched on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsAllButE}.
  Church's Thesis gives that it has an index; let that index be~$e_0$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\neq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\neq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1029.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1030.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsAllButE}
  \end{figure}%
  The \smn{} Theorem parametrizes~$x$ to get the family of functions on the 
  right.
  The number~$e_0$ is fixed so define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$. 
  Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to get a $k\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{g(k)}=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,k)}$, 
  and so $W_k=\N-\set{k}$.
  \item
  No such computably enumerable $W_m$ exists.
  The set $M=\set{m\suchthat \TMfcn_m(x) converges}$ is computably
  enumerable, by dovetailing.
  It is the complement of the given set, so if the given set were computably
  enumerable then the two would be computable.
  But showing that they are not computable is routine, for instance
  with Rice's Theorem.   
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that there is a program that outputs its own source code, that is, 
% there is a Turing machine that outputs a string representation of itself.
% \begin{answer}
% Define a machine that outputs its input, so that it computes
% $\TMfcn(x)=x$.
% If given the input of the source code of a Turing machine $\TM$ then
% its output is the source of $\TM$.
% By the Fixed Point Theorem, there is some Turing machine
% $\TM$ such that $\TM$ has the same behavior as $\TMfcn(\TM)$.
% But that behavior is to output the source code of $\TM$.
% So this machine outputs its own source.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\Cref{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN} shows that there is a computable function
$\TMfcn_n$ with domain~$\set{n}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that there is a computable function $\TMfcn_m$ with range~$\set{m}$.
\item
Is there a computable function $\TMfcn_m$ with range~$\set{2m}$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Consider the function $\psi(x,y)=x$.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior\Dash
  it is computed by the machine on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:RangeOfPhiMEqualsSetM}\Dash so let that be
  machine~$\TM_{e_0}$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1031.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1032.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and for $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:RangeOfPhiMEqualsSetM}
  \end{figure}%
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the machine on the right, 
  which computes the family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,x)}(y)=x$.
  There, $e_0$ is fixed since it is just the index of the Turing machine on 
  the left, so let $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ be given by $g(x)=s(e_0,x)$.
  By the Fixed Point Theorem there is an $m\in\N$ where
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e_0,m)}$, which has range~$\set{m}$.
  \item This is the same as the prior answer, but starting with
  $\psi(x,y)=2x$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that if $\map{f}{\N^2}{\N}$ is a total computable function of
% two variables then there is an index~$e$ so that
